# A. PROJECT/PROGRAMME SUMMARY

## Project/Programme Summary

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) funding proposal template is designed for accredited entities to provide detailed information about their project or programme. The proposal must include cross-references to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

## Key Points:

- Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes
- Annexes provided must be consistent with the details in the funding proposal
- Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents
- The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60 pages
- Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time
- Recommended font is Arial, size 11
- Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP
- Accredited entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4

## Submission Instructions:

Please submit the completed proposal to:  
fundingproposal@gcfund.org  

Please use the following name convention for the file name:  
"FP-[Accredited Entity Short Name]-[Country/Region]-[YYYY/MM/DD]"

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## Project/Programme Information

This section contains detailed information about the project or programme, including but not limited to:

- Project description
- Objectives and expected outcomes
- Target beneficiaries
- Implementation timeline
- Budget breakdown
- Location of implementation

# C. FINANCING INFORMATION

## Financing Information

This section outlines the financial aspects of the project, including:

- Total requested funding amount
- Funding source details
- Cost breakdown
- Financial management plan
- Budget allocation per component

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## Expected Performance Against Investment Criteria

This section evaluates how the project will meet the investment criteria set by the GCF, including:

- Environmental benefits
- Social impact
- Economic viability
- Climate change mitigation and adaptation
- Co-benefits with other sustainable development goals

# E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

## Logical Framework

The logical framework provides a structured approach to planning and monitoring project activities. It includes:

- Inputs
- Activities
- Outputs
- outcomes
- assumptions and risks
- indicators for measuring progress

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Risk Assessment and Management

This section details the risk assessment process and management strategies, including:

- Identification of potential risks
- Likelihood and impact assessment
- Risk mitigation measures
- Monitoring mechanisms
- Contingency plans

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## GCF Policies and Standards

This section covers the policies and standards that apply to the project, including:

- GCF policies on gender equality
- Environmental and social standards
- compliance requirements
- monitoring and evaluation procedures

# H. ANNEXES

## Annexes

The annexes contain supporting documentation such as:

- Feasibility studies
- Gender action plans
- Term sheets
- Project implementation plans
- Budget documents
- Monitoring and evaluation frameworks

# A.20. Executing Entity information  
The project will be executed directly by AGRA through their Programme Operational Unit – which falls under the same legal entity as the PSAA Applicant. As a continental institution in agriculture and food systems, AGRA is well placed to undertake the planned activities through its widespread infrastructure and partnerships on the continent, having strong AGRA country teams and infrastructure in the target countries. Using AGRA's continental forums, the Africa Food Systems Forum (AFSF) and Agribusiness Deal Room, AGRA brings on board close working relationships with governments, partners and other stakeholders to enhance ownership and coordinate key actors, knowledge and resources, which are fundamental to take food loss innovation under changing climates to scale. The executing entity role of AGRA will be fulfilled both through the Nairobi-based headquarters, and their country offices, all of which are the same legal entity as the PSAA Applicant.  

# A.21. Executive summary  
4. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity and challenges for agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the pressures of a changing climate. The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty. The effects of climate change are expected to be severe in Africa, where the capacity to adapt and respond to a changing climate is weak — exacerbating the continent’s food insecurity and climate vulnerability challenges.  

5. The impacts of climate change have increased over the past decades in Africa, manifesting in more frequent, intense, and prolonged extreme weather events, such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, pest outbreaks, landslides, desertification, and sandstorms. These extreme weather events have resulted in increased temperatures and humidity, shifts in precipitation patterns, water stress, and soil erosion. Most African countries already face recurrent droughts that affect growing seasons, often leading to short growing periods reducing the viability of farming in marginal agricultural areas. Projected reductions in yield in some countries could reach as much as 50% by 2030, and crop net revenues may fall by up to 90% by 2100, with smallholder farmers being the most affected.  

6. Food security, however, is affected not only by changes in crop production but also by changes throughout the crop value chain, including post-harvest. There is, therefore, a crucial need to address the impacts of climate change on the full agriculture value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, processing, and distribution.  

7. While there is a wealth of information on the observed and projected impacts of climate change on crop production pre-harvest (i.e., germination, fruiting, cultivation, maturation, seed or grain quality, and most of all yields), relatively little attention has been paid to how climate change variability and hazards affect harvesting, post-harvest handling and storage (e.g., threshing, sorting, drying, warehousing), and processing, transport and logistics (grading, packaging, storage, transport, distribution, and marketing).  

8. Of the range of climate hazards that affect harvest and post-harvest value chains of cereal crops and legumes, scholarly publications and country experts pointed to variable, erratic, and unpredictable rainfall (including an increase in heavy rainfall events), high temperatures (extreme heat), and flooding as the priority hazards of concern. Literature and stakeholders also highlighted drought as a key hazard.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 5

## Important hazard

To a lesser degree, sources also acknowledged the role of wildfires and landslides.

Consistently, stakeholder input coalesced around the several stage-specific risks and impacts:

- **Harvesting Processes**: shattering of the grain/seed due to untimely heavy rains; challenges to field drying due to excessive rainfall; rotting, mold, and mycotoxin (principally aflatoxin) contamination in the field due to high humidity; damage to stored harvest from flooding; pest and rodents.

- **Post-Harvest Handling and Storage**: damage to or decay of grain/seed quality from high temperatures and humidity; rotting, mold, and mycotoxin (aflatoxin) contamination due to damage to storage infrastructure from flooding;

- **Processing, Transport, and Logistics**: damaged or inaccessible road networks and transport infrastructure due to flooding; damage to processing and storage facilities and infrastructure from excessive rainfall and flooding; damage to or decay of grain/seed quality from high temperatures and humidity, and lower shelf life; disruption of mobility and access to markets due to extreme weather, impeding distribution and marketing.

## 8. In addition to the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food losses, such losses also contribute to greenhouse gas emissions.

## 9. In response to the critical gaps noted above, the RE-GAIN programme will support the innovative adaptation of the harvest and post-harvest value chain in seven African countries — Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Tanzania, Uganda, and Zambia; strengthening food security by addressing the climate change impacts on post-harvest food losses through the wide-scale adoption of Food Loss-Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) among smallholder farmers. In addition to increasing the amount of food available for consumption due to reduced losses, this approach will also improve the quality of produce leaving the farms, thereby increasing nutritional value, marketability and income potential, while simultaneously reducing the GHG emissions associated with food loss. To achieve the project goal, actions will be taken across three primary pathways — farmer-centric, supplier-centric, and institutional support — which together will form the foundation of a sustainable and scalable market for FL-RS in the target countries. These interconnected solutions will address food loss under smallholder settings of Africa.

## 10. The first development pathway focuses on increasing demand and absorption capacity for FL-RS among smallholder farming communities. This will involve raising awareness among farmers of the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food loss, as well as on the availability of FL-RS options on the market and how FL-RS will help reduce food loss under changing climate conditions. This awareness raising will be coupled with training on the use of specific FL-RS that have been identified for key crops in each country as well as on the effective use of weather information and advisories, while supporting the initial transfer of suitable technologies to the farmers to catalyse uptake and demonstrate the effectiveness of FL-RS. These activities will be further supported through actions to improve market linkages between farmers and agri-value chain actors such as processors and aggregators.

## 11. The second development pathway complements the demand-side activities by enhancing the supply and affordability of FL-RS. The first output of this will support business development linked to the local supply of FL-RS, targeting both existing suppliers, as well as entrepreneurs seeking to enter the market – particularly MSMEs, local cooperatives and youth groups. Support to local businesses will increase the supply of FL-RS in local markets, which in turn will enable cost reduction, underpinning the outcome of improved affordability. This will be complemented by the establishment of innovative financial mechanisms for smallholders and MSMEs to support the adoption of FL-RS. In particular, the project will bring together key stakeholders — including suppliers, agro-dealers, financial institutions, MSMEs and local farmers — to establish finance models that stimulate the FL-RS market, using catalytic co-payments and conditional procurement to provide added security to the transactions.

## 12. Combined, the increased demand and the enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS stimulate the market, enabling suppliers to develop products and services that are affordable to smallholder farmers, thereby facilitating a balance between supply and demand, and establishing a self-sufficient market that will operate beyond the project support. The integrated nature of these Outputs and the causal pathways reinforcing the market development are depicted in Figure 3 below.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 6

These first two development pathways will be underpinned and sustained by the third, which seeks to create an enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS; which will not only mobilise increased investment in the sector but also provide wider policy interventions in strengthening the market systems and advisory services. This will involve policy revisions and put in place enforcement mechanisms in each of the target countries in support of enabling FL-RS — including stimulating tax incentives and promoting standards for FL-RS quality — as well as creating platforms for the scaling and replication of successful FL-RS business models.

13. Overall, the resulting uptake of FL-RS, at scale, will reduce food loss, improve food quality and enhance local capacity, thereby enabling the long-term outcome of improved food and nutritional security. This will have the co-benefit of increasing food safety, while the increased investment in FL-RS resulting will create additional employment opportunities in the market. Moreover, the project will reduce GHG emission, both directly – through the reduced emissions from the wasted food itself – and indirectly by reducing land use change for agricultural expansion driven by the need to compensate for food losses.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate context

### 14. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity and challenges for agricultural transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the pressures of a changing climate.

The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty. The effects of climate change are expected to be severe in Africa, where the capacity to adapt and respond to a changing climate is weak — exacerbating the continent’s food insecurity challenge.

### 15. A great deal of attention has been paid to the impacts of climate change on crop cultivation, with extensive research on how climate variability affects crop yields, and management strategies to address these challenges.

However, food security is influenced not only by changes in crop production but also by the entire crop value chain, including post-harvest stages. There is, therefore, a crucial need to consider how climate change impacts the full agriculture value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, processing, and distribution.

### Harvesting and Post Harvesting Value Chains

### 16. The RE-GAIN programme will support the adaptation of the harvest and post-harvest management innovations in seven African countries — Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Tanzania, Uganda, and Zambia; strengthening food security by addressing the climate change impacts on post-harvest food losses.

For the purpose of the RE-GAIN Programme proposal, the post-harvest value chain is considered to start at the point of harvest, and spans across three key stages, as shown in Figure 1. Strategic

Figure 1. Strategic value chain stages included in the RE-GAIN Programme.

### Climate Risks for Post-Harvest Value Chains

### 17. Climate change-driven (or exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-production stages of the value chains compound the climate change-driven productivity and yield losses during cultivation. This results in a double-blow to farming communities from climatic factors such as rising temperatures, higher moisture levels (warmer air holds more moisture), increased precipitation, more frequent or intense heavy rainfall events, flooding.

### 18. Climate change is thus a threat-multiplier to crop value chains. Climate change impacts in the post-production value chain, which result in substantial post-harvest losses and reduce the output that farmers have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize livelihoods of small-holder farmers (IFPRI, 2022). This perpetuates vulnerability to climatic shocks and stresses and prolongs the cycle of persistent poverty.

### 19. In the face of climate change-driven post-harvest losses, farmers have an impetus to keep less of the reduced output for their domestic consumption and to offset the volume losses (during harvesting, field-

### Adaptive capacity – current capacity to anticipate or respond to the impact of climate change on food loss  
24. All seven RE-GAIN countries are recognized as having low adaptive capacity and low readiness for climate change impacts, in terms of their relative global standing. Per the ND-GAIN index¹⁹, based on scores for 2021, the countries’ ranking on adaptive capacity¹⁰ and readiness¹¹ – out of 192 countries – is presented in Table 3. This economy-wide low level of adaptive capacity also applies to the agricultural sector, including post-harvest management of value chains. This was affirmed and validated through inputs in national and sub-national stakeholder workshops, at which stakeholders identified several factors that contribute to low adaptive capacity against climate-induced risks and impacts. Stakeholder inputs across all 14 crops in the seven countries points to consistent, common factors affecting adaptive capacity in all fourteen value chains (of eight different crops). This consistency is both understandable (given common or similar barriers in all seven countries’ agricultural value chains) and valuable (as it offers opportunity for RE-GAIN’s  

¹⁹ Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative, n.d  
¹⁰ Under the ND GAIN Index, ‘Adaptive Capacity’ is the ability of society and its supporting sectors to adjust to reduce potential damage and to respond to the negative consequences of climate events. This captures a collection of means, readily deployable to deal with sector-specific climate change impacts.  
¹¹ Under the ND GAIN Index, ‘Readiness’ is the ability to make effective use of investments for adaptation actions thanks to a safe and efficient business environment. Readiness has three components: economic readiness, governance readiness, and social readiness.

## 25. Across the board, the major factors of vulnerability that impede adaptive capacity, highlighted by stakeholders in all seven countries, are summarised as follows:

- **Infrastructure**: Reliance on climate-vulnerable (non-climate-robust) storage and transport infrastructure leads to greater vulnerability and exposure to ambient temperature and moisture, as well as during extreme weather conditions such as flooding and heavy rainfall events.
- **Finance**: Lack of access or limited access to credit reduces the ability for risk-management, and limits financial capacity to invest in climate-adaptive technology, equipment, facilities, and tools. Limited information about markets reduces the ability to plan for volumes of storage, transport, and sale, with the risk of more of the harvested crop being exposed to temperature and humidity than optimal.
- **Technology**: Reliance on traditional methods and practices rather than greater mechanization and specialized tools (for harvesting, threshing, sorting, grading, packaging) reduces ability to protect the harvested crop from the impacts of high temperatures, moisture, humidity, and exposure to flooding. Limited information about pest control also increases the harvested crop’s exposure to climate-sensitive pests (including insects).
- **Capacity**: Lack of/limited access to technology, equipment, facilities, and tools (for threshing, drying, storage, and transport) reduces ability to protect harvested crop from the impacts of high temperatures, moisture, humidity, and increases exposure to flooding and heavy rains.

## 26. Climate change-driven (or exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-production stages of the value chains compound the climate change-driven productivity and yield losses during cultivation. This results in a double-blow to farming communities from climatic factors such as rising temperatures, higher moisture levels (warmer air holds more moisture), increased precipitation, more frequent or intense heavy rainfall events, flooding. Climate change is thus a threat-multiplier to crop value chains. The impacts from climate change in the post-production value chain stages, which result in substantial post-harvest losses and reduce the output that farmers have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize livelihoods of small-holder farmers. This perpetuates vulnerability to climatic shocks and stresses and prolongs the cycle of persistent poverty.

## 27. Furthermore, in the face of climate change-driven post-harvest losses, farmers have an impetus to keep less of the reduced output for their own domestic consumption and to offset the volume losses (during harvesting, field-drying, on-farm storage) by selling more of what remains in the market. This worsens household food insecurity in countries that already have markedly high levels of undernourishment (per FAO figures). All of these reduce adaptive capacity and must be ameliorated to ensure resilience of smallholder farmers in the seven countries.

## 28. In light of these findings, it is imperative for RE-GAIN to support adaptation to climate change through harvest and post-harvest loss-reduction solutions, and enable climate resilience in crops’ post-harvest value chains of smallholder farmers and communities.

# Adaptation Needs and Climate Change Solutions

29. The impact of climate change on post-harvest food value chain stage is felt largely through the exposure of the grain/seed to high temperatures, high moisture levels, direct contact with water (such as during heavy rainfall events), and damage caused by extreme weather events such as flooding. Additionally, high temperature and moisture levels also foster the growth of pests and diseases, including fungal and parasitic agents. Thus, the most effective solutions for reducing climate-change induced food losses in the post-harvest value chain is to reduce the harvest seed’s/grain’s exposure to the climatic variable or hazard. This involves interventions that improve efficiency and speed of harvest and related actions (such as threshing, sorting, grading etc. – to reduce the time period of exposure), as well as interventions that offer higher protection from climatic elements (barriers between the grain/seed and the external environment), plus interventions that provide higher levels of actionable information on the levels of or prevalence of the climatic variables (e.g., measurement tools).

30. The mere provision of such FL-RS solutions by itself is not enough if this is not accompanied by capacity-building, training, awareness-generation of how best to use such solutions, and how to increase financial wherewithal to maintain and augment such solutions in the future. To address the pressing need for broader implementation of solutions aimed at reducing climate-related harvest and post-harvest food loss, it is necessary to raise awareness and build capacity to promote the adoption of Food Loss Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) in the light of expected climate impacts. This needs to be complemented by strengthened multisectoral institutional capacities, facilitating the uptake of FL-RS by end users and service providers, increasing options of solutions’ availability, and enabling practical application through policy interventions. There is also an urgent need for enhanced financial access for farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), empowering them to invest in climate-friendly FL-RS and incentivising vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers of climate-adapted FL-RS, fostering a robust end-to-end market ecosystem.

31. For this programme, a key focus of adaptation support needs to be on strengthening the capabilities of countries to develop climate-resilient post-harvest infrastructure, through providing physical solutions alongside technical and institutional capacity building for actors across harvest and postharvest value chain stages – from processing through to market distribution. This includes investing in strategic approaches that leverage finance and build markets for food loss reduction strategies. By enhancing access to markets of existing postharvest solutions, the program will encourage farmers to adopt FL-RS products and services, thereby boosting their climate and economic resilience.

## Table 4. Solutions to reducing/preventing food losses induced by climate change

| Typology of Solution | Climate change solution | Climate change hazard being addressed (enhanced protection from): | Applicable crops |
|----------------------|-------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------|
| Physical Food-Loss Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) (infrastructure, equipment, tools, machinery interventions) | Mechanical multi-crop threshers and shellers | Increased average temperatures<br>Extreme heat and heatwaves<br>Increase in number of hot days over 35°C<br>Higher moisture levels and flooding from heavy rains (days with rainfall > 20mm)<br>Higher moisture levels and flooding from large 1-day rains and large 5-day rains) | Maize, Wheat, Rice, Teff, Beans, Soybeans, Cowpeas, Groundnuts |
| Non-Physical Food-Loss Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) (awareness-generation, capacity-building) | Gender-responsive awareness campaign on the impacts of CC on post-harvest food losses and the availability of FL-RS | Through reduction of vulnerability and enhancement of adaptive capacity against the following hazards:<br>Increased average temperatures<br>Extreme heat and heatwaves | - |
| Non-Physical Food-Loss Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) (awareness-generation, capacity-building) | Demonstration, training and tech. transfer for the use of weather/climate information, FL-RS and related practices | - | - |

## According to the World Bank estimates, a one percent reduction in postharvest losses in Sub-Saharan Africa could lead to economic gains of $40 million each year, and most of the benefits would go directly to smallholder farmers12. Moreover, food loss and waste depict an extremely inefficient use of resources and account for about 3.3 gigatonnes of greenhouse gas emissions globally13. In addition, large amounts of water and fertilizer go into the production of this food that never reaches human mouths. Recovering the food that is lost during harvest and postharvest handling can help close that calorie gap in Africa while strengthening livelihoods and improving food security — without requiring any additional environmental cost.  

## Complementarity with ongoing and planned initiatives  
The RE-GAIN Programme operates in a complex environment of ongoing initiatives, and has been designed with this in mind. While many of these initiatives are focused on the production side of the agriculture value chain, there are also initiatives in each of the target countries targeting various aspects of the post-harvest value chain. Through their ongoing operations, AGRA has developed a strong understanding of the landscape of initiatives in the target countries and has developed working relations with many of the organisations operating these initiatives. The design of this programme has closely considered the ongoing work in each country and seeks to complement and collaborate with these initiatives to maximise the effectiveness of the programme14. A list of the key initiatives in each of the target countries is presented in Table 10-2 of Annex 2.

# B.2. (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram

## Goal Statement

33. IF the capacity of the target countries and communities to respond to climate-triggered food losses is strengthened through improved and inclusive access to financing, promotion of context-specific and gender-responsive innovations to reduce food losses, and better enabling conditions for public and private investments, THEN smallholder farmers will have enhanced food security and livelihood resilience, BECAUSE the widespread use of food loss-reduction technologies will reduce food loss and reduce the carbon footprint of food systems, while increasing household income and building the resilience of smallholder farmers, MSMEs and rural communities to climate shocks.

## Barriers

34. Several barriers currently impede the uptake of food loss-reducing solutions in Africa. While the specifics of each barrier might vary between countries and farming systems, six common barriers have been identified, cutting across several themes, including limited knowledge of the options available and capacities to use what is available, access to and supply of affordable harvesting and postharvest equipment and technologies, and access to sustainable and inclusive finance opportunities, all encompassed by an overarching lack of an enabling environment. An outline of these core barriers is presented below, with deeper country-specific analysis provided in the appendices to the Feasibility Study (Annex 2).

### Barrier 1: Inadequate access to information and solutions for smallholder farmers

35. While most smallholder farmers are acutely aware of the impacts of post-harvest losses on their food and income security, many do not have access to the information needed to adopt improved post-harvest processing and storage practices needed reduce such losses. The resulting knowledge gaps extend across the full range of pre- and post-harvest processes — including harvesting, handling, storage, transporting, and packaging of agricultural commodities — and are typically more significant for women-managed farms, contributing to notable gender gaps in farm productivity and profitability. For some farmers, the knowledge gaps are fundamental, in that they lack the basic understanding of resilient post-harvest processes and the options available to reduce food loss. For others, there is a general understanding of the need for improved practices, or even the availability of food loss-reducing solutions (FL-RS) but lack the understanding of where and how to access such solutions, or how to properly implement them. These knowledge gaps are systemic in the agriculture sector of the target countries, with limited integration of FL-RS information into sectoral policies and agricultural extension services. As a result, farmers lack access to the required information to adopt FL-RS. Overcoming this barrier requires a combination of targeted and inclusive awareness raising among smallholder farmers, as well as the establishment of an enabling environment in which information on FL-RS is mainstreamed into agriculture sectoral policies and support services.

### Barrier 2: Limited awareness of climate change and climate risks

36. As noted above, climate change impacts the agriculture sector across its entire value chain – from production, through harvest, storage, processing and distribution. Despite this significant impact, many smallholder farmers do not fully understand climate change and the associated climate risks and the impacts they have on food security, and consequently are not adequately motivated to adopt resilient solutions. Given that the impacts of climate change on the agricultural sector are expected to increase in the future, the consequences of inaction stemming from limited awareness will become increasingly severe. Although there is growing understanding among farmers of the impacts of climate change on production – particularly as observed during increasingly frequent extreme climate events – such awareness is less prevalent for post-harvest losses. This is partly due to the links being less obvious to the observer, for example where a changing climate influences the prevalence of pests or disease during storage. This lack of awareness perpetuates the information gaps noted in Barrier 1. Specifically, when a farmer does not understand the links between climate change and food loss, efforts to seek resilient solutions are often subdued. Moreover, a lack of awareness of potential future climate trajectories

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 19  

## Barrier 3: Limited availability of appropriate harvest and post-harvest technology  

37. While a wide range of FL -RS options have been developed globally, penetration of such solutions into smallholder farming communities in Africa is limited due to limited access to technologies and practices, low community engagement and insufficient demand and perceptions of risk from FL -RS providers. This lack of market penetration has been attributed to several closely linked factors. First, because of the limited awareness noted in Barriers 1 and 2, there is currently limited demand for FL -RS in rural communities. The lack of demand is further exacerbated by unavailability of working capital or access to affordable finance (see Barrier 4 below), with many farmers therefore considering it unfeasible even if they wanted to adopt improved FL-RS.  

38. Second, as a result of the limited demand, suppliers have not demonstrated sufficient interest to develop the market for FL -RS. In particular, without the economies of scale driven by sufficient demand, FL -RS providers are not incentivised to develop localised production, distribution networks and markets that would bring the cost of FL-RS down. This also inhibits research into improving FL-RS solutions that could improve efficiency or affordability of the products.  

39. Third, the development of local markets for FL-RS is further constrained by the perceived risk for suppliers of engaging with smallholder farmers. As with financial institutions, FL-RS providers need to balance risk in setting up operations within, and providing services to, smallholder farming communities. Creating a market for smallholder farmers – for whom working capital is limited – requires innovative market tools to be developed, many of which require the sharing of risk between different stakeholders – including providers, users and financial operators. However, such systems ultimately rely on the ability of smallholder farmers to secure sufficient income from their produce. Consequently, FL-RS suppliers often perceive smallholder farmers as high risk because of their general limited capacity to sustainably produce, process and market agricultural goods.  

40. The ultimate result of these challenges is that smallholder farmers do not have easy access to affordable FL-RS. To overcome this barrier, a catalyst is required to initiate demand and establish a sustainable local market that would provide the necessary incentives to attract FL-RS providers.  

## Barrier 4: Limited access to finance for FL-RS uptake   

41. As noted above, the market for FL-RS for smallholder farmers in Africa is influenced by the limited access to markets for smallholder farmers, working capital for MSMEs and access to finance for the purchasing of equipment. While there is a diverse financial market across all target countries — ranging from commercial banks to micro-finance institutions (MFIs) — several inter-related factors impede access to finance. First, MSMEs are perceived as high risk, largely because of the uncertainty of production (particularly for rainfed agriculture under changing climate conditions) and limited access to smallholder produce. The feasibility of agricultural loans is dependent on MSMEs being able to develop a profitable business case for the FL-RS equipment to be able to repay the loan at the end of the season. However, the vast array of factors that impact the business case creates a high level of uncertainty, while inadequate on-farm post-harvest storage and processing can lead to further food losses before a farmer is ever able to use the FL-RS services. This uncertainty is being exacerbated by climate change, with growing risk that entire harvest can be lost in a single extreme climate event. Consequently, FIs are often hesitant to provide agricultural loan products that target agrifood MSMEs. In instances where financial products have been developed, the terms of the credit are often untenable for agrifood MSMEs.  

42. Second, and exacerbating the risk perceptions mentioned above, is the lack of or limited collateral and track record among smallholder farmers and the companies serving them, including youth groups, agro-dealers and cooperatives. To offset the risk and comply with Central bank regulation, FIs generally require collateral from the borrowers, generally in the form of an immovable asset, such as land or property. However, most small and medium sized enterprises especially women and youth led businesses lack suitable collateral or have already pledged their assets, restricting access to available finance products. For example, land tenure arrangements in rural areas of Africa are generally dominated by communal land

### Barrier 5: Inadequate policy and regulatory frameworks to enable FL-RS investments  
43. Development of national policies, strategies, frameworks and implementation plans aiming to address  
agricultural sector and climate change challenges has been a priority for many of the African governments  
in the recent years. Despite this, there are still considerable gaps in the policy and regulatory environment  
across the target countries that impede the mainstreaming of sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector.  
This gap is underpinned by several factors. First, there is limited or no use of the scientific evidence base  
in the formulation of policies and strategies on post-harvest loss reduction. This challenge is exacerbated  
by limited technical and institutional capacities and systems for institution-level data collection, monitoring,  
and evaluation of implementation and effectiveness policies and programmes.  

44. Second, policies generally lack coordination and harmonisation across sectors , limiting opportunities for  
integrated approaches to building resilience in the agriculture sector . For example , the volatility and  
vulnerability of food systems, and associated food loss, is often exacerbated by poor infrastructure and/or  
uneven and deteriorating power or energy access; and the lack of harmonisation of policies across these  
different sectors prevents collaborative action, perpetuating the challenges.  

45. Third, where policies or regulatory frameworks are in place, they are often constrained to agricultural input  
subsidies — mainly for fertilizer and seeds — with no subsidy on post-harvest technologies. This further  
extends to the focus of information and extension services, which are generally geared towards primary  
production, and less so to post -harvest processes. Similarly, existing taxation regimes do not favour  or  
incentivise investments in storage and processing technologies for food in the smallholder system.  

46. The underdeveloped private sector in these countries require policy incentive mechanisms, for example  
import tax exemption that would enable reduction of cost of import and distribution of FS equipment and  
services. Further, tax exemptions on key components that can be used in the manufacturing or assembly  
of FL-RS should also be a key consideration in these countries.  

47. The lack of access to finance as described above is also restricted by Central bank regulation an d  
prudential guidelines on loan loss provisioning, consideration of movable assets as collateral and value of  
guarantees for banks in reducing their reserve requirements. Addressing these policy barriers can unlock  
financing for equipment (movable assets), storage facilities (fixed assets) and other FL-RS identified. The  
existence of warehouse receipt legislation and authorities are also an important factor to consider  
enhancing the uptake and usage of storage solutions. Without cash in hand at harvest, farme rs will be  
hesitant to store their produce.     

### Barrier 6: Insufficient public sector capacity to steer investments in FL-RS   
48. Actions to overcome the full range of barriers described above require a strong public sector to create the  
enabling environment and steer investments into FL -RS. The public sector pl ays an important role in

## Stepping Investment

The proposed project will contribute to food security in seven African countries by promoting the wide-scale adoption of Food Loss-Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) among smallholder farmers. In addition to increasing the amount of food available for consumption due to reduced losses, this approach will also improve the quality of produce leaving the farms, thereby increasing nutritional value, marketability and income potential, while simultaneously reducing the GHG emissions associated with food loss.

To achieve the project goal, actions will be taken across three primary pathways — farmer-centric, supplier-centric, and institutional support — which together will form the foundation of a sustainable and scalable market for FL-RS in the target countries. Interconnected solutions will be addressing food loss under smallholder setting of Africa.

### Figure 2. Interconnected solutions addressing food loss under smallholder setting of Africa.

## First Development Pathway

The first development pathway focuses on increasing demand and absorption capacity for FL-RS among smallholder farming communities. Activities under this pathway will target overcoming Barriers 1 and 2, raising awareness among farmers of the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food loss, as well as on the availability of FL-RS options on the market and how FL-RS will help reduce food loss under changing climate conditions.

This awareness raising will be coupled with training on the use of specific FL-RS that have been identified for key crops in each country as well as on the effective use of weather information and advisories, while supporting the initial transfer of suitable technologies to the farmers to catalyse uptake and demonstrate the effectiveness of FL-RS.

These combined activities will be catalysing the demand and build the absorption capacity needed to achieve short-term Outcome 1: FL-RS adopted by smallholder farmers. Contributions to Outcome 1 are further enhanced through Output 1.2, which will improve market linkages between farmers and agri-value chain actors such as processors and aggregators. This will enhance the value-add of FL-RS, ensuring that the increased quality produce resulting from improved harvesting methods, post-harvest handling, storage, and processing is able to make it to suitable markets to maximize income; creating sustainability in the whole value chain that will enable future scaling of and maintain demand for FL-RS.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 22

## 51. The second development pathway complements the demand-side activities by enhancing the supply and affordability of FL-RS (Outcome 2). This component involves two Outputs, one focused on business development for the improved provision of FL-RS on local markets, and the second focused on complementary financial mechanisms for smallholders and MSMEs to enable the adoption of FL-RS.

Activities under Output 2.1 will support business development linked to the local supply of FL-RS, targeting both existing suppliers, as well as entrepreneurs seeking to enter the market – particularly MSMEs, local cooperatives and youth groups. The business development support will include providing access to market intelligence, as well as facilitating access to existing credit/guarantee schemes as well as any new financial mechanisms established under Output 2.2. Support to local businesses will increase the supply of FL-RS in local markets, which in turn will enable cost reduction, underpinning the outcome of improved affordability. This will be complemented by activities under Output 2.2 aimed at establishing innovative financial mechanisms for smallholders and MSMEs to support the adoption of FL-RS. In particular, the project will establish partnerships across the FL-RS value chain to create accessible finance opportunities based on the foundations of risk sharing and cooperation. These partnerships will bring together key stakeholders — including suppliers, agro-dealers, financial institutions, MSMEs and local farmers — to establish finance models that stimulate the FL-RS market. Catalytic co-payments and conditional procurements under the system using GCF grants will provide added security to the transactions, further enhancing the risk-sharing nature of the approach. Finance models will be adapted to fit the local context of each target community, considering the nature of the targeted crop and the required solutions, the needs of the local farmers, and the capacity of stakeholders to absorb risk. Where available, the development of financial modalities will integrate existing credit lines available from local FIs, as well as supporting the inclusion of FL-RS in climate-resilient input packages.

## 52. Combined, the increased demand from Outcome 1 and the enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS from Output 2 will be catalytic in the market, overcoming Barriers 3 and 4. Specifically, the transactions facilitated by the project will stimulate the market, enabling suppliers to develop products and services that are affordable to smallholder farmers, thereby facilitating a balance between supply and demand, and establishing a self-sufficient market that will operate beyond the project support. The integrated nature of these Outputs and the causal pathways reinforcing the market development are depicted in Figure 3 below.

### Figure 3: Causal pathways underpinning the Theory of Change

## 53. These first two development pathways will be underpinned and sustained by the third, which seeks to create an enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS (Outcome 3); which together with Outcome 2 will not only mobilise increased investment in the sector but also provide wider policy interventions in strengthening the market systems and advisory services. Through Output 3.1, the capacity of national institutions will be enhanced to enable investments in FL-RS – specifically tackling Barriers 5 and 6. This will involve policy revisions and put in place enforcement mechanisms in each of the target countries in support of enabling FL-RS — including stimulating tax incentives and promoting standards for FL-RS.

# B.3. Programme description

58. The programme description below outlines the overarching design of the programme’s approach as it applies across all seven countries. The activities are presented thematically as they apply across the focal countries as a whole, with specific details on the nature of interventions for each individual country presented in the country-specific Appendixes to Annex 2.

## Crop and Site Selection

59. Key crops were identified by expert assessment, supported by AGRA and the National Designated Authority (NDA) of each target country through consultative processes. Two crops per target country under AGRA’s portfolio were selected to ensure that sufficient resources would be available for the crafting and execution of targeted solutions (as depicted in Figure 10). Selected crops are representative of the agricultural dynamics of each country and aligned with the specific needs and strategic agricultural goals of the nation. In addition, these crops hold substantial importance to the country’s food security and/or experience particularly high rates of loss within the value chain. Finally, these crops are produced in large parts of the country through a significant number of smallholder farmers engaged in the value chain. The key crops therefore reflect the agronomic and economic realities of each country and provide opportunities for targeted enhancement of food security and sustainable agricultural practices and improved management of these crops is also expected to reduce GHG emissions given the aggregated contributions of these value chains to country’s GHG emissions, contributing to the NDCs of the target country. Please see Annex 2: Section 3 and 4 for details on the climate vulnerability of selected crops.

60. Within each country, a set of target regions have been identified, as outlined in Table 5. Within these sites, the specific beneficiaries will be selected following a set of eligibility criteria, as presented in Section 5.2, 5.6 and 6.4 of Annex 2. The selection of beneficiaries will involve social and environmental risk screening, including screening for the presence of indigenous communities 15; in locations where indigenous communities are present, careful consideration and tailored approaches will be designed to address the unique circumstances and challenges faced by Indigenous communities in these areas with continuous engagement to proactively include their participation in the RE-GAIN programme.

## Component 1: Food Loss-Reduction Solutions (FL-RS) demand side development to increase the adoption of FL-RS by farmers  

## Outcome 1: Increased adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers  

61. The first project component focuses on enhancing demand for, and adoption of, FL-RS in the target communities. It is this adoption of FL-RS that will ultimately reduce food losses and result in the core benefits of the programme for smallholder farmers – namely improved food security, increased resilience to climate shocks, and reduced GHG emissions. The approach for achieving this Outcome is grounded to two programme Outputs that will directly support the farmers through awareness, training, technology transfer and market linkages. However, given the integrated nature of the project approach, achieving the full impact of the Outcome will also rely on the supply-side interventions under Outcome 2, and the strengthening of the enabling environment under Outcome 3.

## Output 1.1: Smallholder farmers supported to adopt FL-RS  

62. At the heart of the demand-side development is the direct support provided to smallholder farmers to not only know what FL-RS options are available, but also to understand the need for adopting such options – particularly when considering the impacts of a changing climate – as well as how to optimise the use of FL-RS. Activities under this project will involve technical support targeting several core themes:  

- Raising awareness among farmers of the impacts of climate change on food loss and the availability of improved, climate-resilient FL-RS options;  
- Training farmers on the use of improved FL-RS, as well as adapting existing post-harvest food loss-reduction technologies to climate change;  
- Training farmers on best practices for complementary crop management (including use of weather/climate information) to maximise the efficiency of FL-RS;  
- Actively demonstrating the effectiveness of FL-RS systems in local contexts; and  
- Supporting the transfer of technologies and boosting immediate availability of FL-RS solutions in target communities.  

63. Training activities will cover three key stakeholder groups, namely: i) smallholder farmers; ii) extension workers and agro-dealers; and iii) agricultural traders and processors.  

64. The activities under Output 1.1 will focus specifically on short-listed FL-RS interventions, tailoring the approach to each community’s individual context. AGRA will train and engage VBAs (Village Based Advisors), who are community-based, and are usually young people who are lead farmers, unemployed youth and/or entrepreneurial farmers to provide extension services and agribusiness linkages. This extension model has been well developed and is operational in these target countries. They will play an important role in training smallholder farmers on FL-RS solutions.  

An evaluation of proposed physical FL-RS was conducted across all seven target RE-GAIN countries to identify those with the highest potential to reduce harvest and postharvest food losses and protect the harvests against growing impacts from climate hazards. The output of this assessment established a shortlist of FL-RS options tailored to each country and crop, as presented in Section 5 of Annex 2 – Feasibility Study. These solutions include:  

- Communal storage structures;  
- Warehouses;  
- Harvesting machinery;  
- Hermetic bags;  
- Mechanical multi-crop threshers and shellers;  
- Metal and plastic silos;  
- Moisture meters;  
- Storage protectants and control agents;  
- Storage structures;  
- Tarpaulins and plastic sheets.  

Further details on the nature of interventions per country are available in the Appendixes to Annex 2.

## Activity 1.1.1: Conduct an awareness-raising activity targeting smallholder farming communities focused on the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food losses and the availability of locally-appropriate and climate-resilient FL-RS  

65. The limited awareness on post-harvest food loss management at both the value chain and policy level, combined with inadequate communication on the importance of post-harvest food loss reduction has led to the limited adoption of available FL-RS as well as inadequate integration of food loss reduction strategies into relevant policies and strategies by governments in Africa. This has, in turn, resulted in

## Activity 1.1.2: Train smallholder farmers on the use of improved climate-resilient FL-RS

68. Training will be provided to develop the capacities of smallholder farmers on improved crop management and storage techniques to reduce food losses and to maintain quality of produce, increase farmers' income by reducing losses and improving marketability, and improve supply of financial services and FL-RS to smallholders and other value chain actors. AGRA will procure the services of suitable local entities (for example, NGOs, VBAs) to facilitate training in target communities. Training will then be provided through various channels — such as onsite demonstration of best practices, agricultural extension services, lead farmers and Village-Based Advisors (VBAs), farmer cooperatives, industry associations, and digital platforms — depending on the local context and the existing networks of the local service provider.

Approximately 5,350 VBA will be engaged for training, each targeting 200 to 250 farmers, resulting in a total reach of ~1,100,000 people trained.

69. The nature and content of the training will be tailored to each group and demand as outlined below.

Training will include baseline topics, including an introduction to the RE-GAIN programme and the core concepts of food security, post-harvest food losses and the impact of climate change – building on and leveraging material from the awareness campaign (Activity 1.1.1). The sequencing of the training will also, where feasible, follow Activity 1.1.5 to enable integration of the equipment demonstrations (Activity 1.1.6) into the training of farmers. In addition to this baseline, technical training will be provided on specific subject matter tailored to the local context of each community. A list of potential training topics has been identified, which will be refined for the local needs in each target community during the first year of implementation, once the communities are identified. This list includes:

- Major triggers and incidences of food loss and the impact they have on food security and health
- Identification of the optimal timing of harvesting, thrashing and transport
- Methods to reduce contamination of produce by mycotoxins and other diseases at harvest and storage
- Use of available weather forecast data

### Activity 1.1.3: Train agricultural traders and processors on complementary services for FL-RS

71. Building on the training of farmers, the programme will provide additional training to agricultural traders (agro-dealers, aggregators etc) and processors on complementary services that support and maximise the effectiveness of FL-RS. This training will include the same baseline concepts, including an introduction to the RE-GAIN programme and the core concepts of food security, post-harvest food losses and the impact of climate change. Beyond that, the training will include best-bet approaches to introduce FL-RS to smallholder farmers and the types of initial support required to enhance demand, tailored to the processing and trade of produce, covering topics such as:

- Post-harvest food losses at the trade and processing stages, their causes and solutions;
- Improved quality management and adherence to quality standards;
- Improved storage, transport logistics and packaging (including food-safe packaging materials and methods);
- The sustainable use of storage protectants;
- Farmer aggregation methods and systems and associated risks of food loss;
- Improved processing (including whole grain processing) and value addition;
- Supplier management;
- Effective marketing strategies;
- Access to finance and credit.

# Activity 1.1.5: Conduct scoping missions to identify and engage manufacturers of FL-RS equipment

74. Under this activity, AGRA will develop an inventory and characterization of FL-RS equipment manufacturers and agro-dealers that will be engaged in developing/strengthening local markets and enhancing the immediate availability of affordable of FL-RS (establishing the basis for delivery models under Component 2). By mapping the market and building networks of local manufacturers and importers/agro-dealers, this activity will enable a better understanding of current suppliers of FL-RS, and their production scalability potential. To ensure the reach of FL-RS to remote and rural areas, the programme will facilitate engagement and increased collaboration between solution manufacturers and local agro-dealers in those target areas, thereby ensuring availability of solutions, and their accessibility by local farmers. Where feasible the programme will facilitate the development or strengthening of platforms that will bring together demand, supply and other stakeholders that enhance commerce and an exchange of goods and services.

75. The scoping missions will include assessments of the current market in each target region to better understand the market needs and opportunities. This will include identifying opportunities for exchanges between African manufacturers and companies in other developing countries on the continent and beyond to help introduce new technologies into the African market. # Activity 1.1.6: Conduct demonstrations of locally-appropriate FL-RS

76. The programme will facilitate active demonstrations of short-listed FL-RS to target communities. This activity will be sequenced after and will build on the relationships developed under Activity 1.1.5, partnering with vendors for the implementation of product demonstrations, leveraging the increased sales of their solutions through the programme-supported outreach as an incentive for vendors to engage in demonstrations. Under this model, agro-dealers or suppliers set up and/or provide the inputs for an FL-RS demonstration that will be established either at the agro-dealers' property or on-farm in partnership with the smallholder farmers. Where possible, demonstrations will be done in conjunction with the training under Activity 1.1.2 to improve efficiencies and ensure full connectivity between the theoretical training and practical demonstrations. Demonstrations, trainings and the awareness creation of the food loss–climate change nexus will be designed taking into account local context. They will take the form of a combination of on-farm and communal demonstrations, presented through one-on-one farmer trainings, innovation hubs, and by agro-dealers, targeting farmer and youth groups, aggregators and processors. The project will undertake detailed participatory assessment to design and implement the various modalities of demonstrations and trainings. At least 2 demonstrations will be done in each target district per year for five years, resulting in a total of 410 total demonstrations.

### Output 1.2: Improved market linkages between agri-value chain actors  

## 79. The proliferation of unstructured market systems is one of the major causes of post-harvest losses, with poor handling and low compliance with quality standards creating substantial risk for produce during the post-harvest phase of production. To address this challenge, RE-GAIN will provide technical assistance to enable the development of more structured relationships between producers, value-adding agents and off-takers. By structuring the linkages on the premise of increased food quality linked to improved post-harvest handling, these activities will create incentives for quality compliance and investments in climate-resilient FL-RS. Moreover, the improved market linkages will help de-risk potential external investments in FL-RS, enhancing opportunities for smallholder farmers and cooperatives to adopt FL-RS at scale.  

### Activity 1.2.1: Provide technical assistance to support the structuring of value chains and coordination between market actors  

## 80. The technical assistance under this activity will facilitate market linkages between smallholders, MSMEs and potential buyers – such as retailers, processors and exporters – using the consortia model as tested and proven by AGRA. Other opportunities such as institutional markets will also be explored on this programme to optimise the role of markets in the reduction of food losses.  

## 81. The process will start with an assessment of the existing market in each target community, drawing on and expanding the initial market assessment conducted during preparation of the RE-GAIN programme (see Annex 2). This extended market analysis will map out key actors in the agricultural value chains at the local level, including potential institutional markets (e.g. schools, hospitals, prisons) that are not yet fully accessible to smallholder farmers, but that have potential to act as reliable off-takers. Gaps in the value chains will be mapped, drawing connections between various actors to identify opportunities for filling gaps. Based on this stakeholder mapping, the programme will provide technical assistance to local actors to structure value chains for different crops and institutional markets. Actions to develop linkages will specifically consider existing constraints faced by women in the sector and identify and promote opportunities for women to actively engage in the market.  

## 82. This activity will culminate in clear action plans for structuring value chains for target crops in each community, including an outline of business development opportunities (see Output 2.1) to fill gaps in the market. The implementation of value chain development will be further supported by establishing local coordination mechanisms and supporting the establishment and/or strengthening of cooperatives where needed to facilitate improved coordination between actors.

# Activity 1.2.2: Facilitate linkages between farmers and institutional markets

83. Drawing on the structures identified under Activity 1.2.1, the RE-GAIN programme will engage key actors in the agri-value chain to link smallholder farmers to institutional markets such as school feeding programmes, food processors and other social safety programmes. AGRA will support local farmers and cooperatives in connecting them with other actors in the value chain and provide technical assistance to establish formal mechanisms that will secure offtake agreements for produce that meets the quality standards of the institutional market. This will include support for accessing institutional markets for wholegrain flour.

## Component 2: FL-RS supply side development to increase availability and affordability of FL-RS

### Outcome 2: Enhanced supply and affordability of FL-RS

84. The second component will complement the first by enhancing the supply and affordability of FL-RS. This will be achieved through two Outputs that address key gaps in the FL-RS value chain, introducing innovations that transform the market. The first Output will build the capacity of local MSMEs to provide access to FL-RS, either through improving local supply or offering services to farmers. This will be complemented by the second Output, which will implement innovative financial mechanisms that will catalyse investments in FL-RS through partnerships and conditional procurements. Ultimately, when combined with the demand-side interventions under Component 1, the two Outputs under this component will unlock the local market, encouraging vendors of FL-RS equipment to invest in distribution channels, marketing, maintenance capacity and other needed infrastructure to make the equipment available and serviceable in a sustainable manner. This shift will lead to reduced prices and increased affordability, creating a self-sustaining system that will enable the phased out of programme support.

### Output 2.1: Business development support provided to local MSMEs to facilitate the improved provision of FL-RS on local markets

85. Activities under this Output will develop the business case for investing in the FL-RS supply chain and support local MSMEs to develop business operations for the supply of FL-RS services. At the heart of this approach is the demonstration of solutions through pluralist extension services such as the VBA extension systems model and agro-dealers along with employing service models that enable shared access to larger, more expensive FL-RS equipment, such as harvesting machinery, mechanical multi-crop threshers and shellers (preferably solar-powered), moisture meters and communal storage structures. To ensure the sustainability of such models, the technical capacity of MSMEs will be enhanced, including for the development of sustainable business cases and improved business management practices. Activities under this Output will target at least 2,000 MSMEs.

## 92. One of the key transformative approaches in the RE-GAIN programme is the introduction of innovative financial models that will strategically use GCF grants as catalysts to develop the market for FL-RS. The approach taken in financial model design is focused on mitigating the current market dynamics and challenges faced by smallholder farmers in each of the programme’s target countries, and to work with the additional activities in the programme to respond to some of the key challenges identified and the gaps in the current delivery of food-loss solutions. The goal of the financial model design is to specifically address these challenges and support the growth and development of the market, as a whole. In addition to the core financial mechanisms, the programme will support several other pathways to financing of FL-RS, including the inclusion of FL-RS in existing input packages provided by local financial institutions, and structuring prefinancing partnership arrangements that include FL-RS.

## Activity 2.2.1. Facilitate the development and deployment of catalytic procurement models for FL-RS focusing on women and youth collectives, and enterprises as key beneficiaries.

### 93. Under this Activity, RE-GAIN will implement two financial models split into two distinct categories: interventions at a smallholder level; and interventions at a youth group/co-operative level. An overview of each of the two financial models is provided below, with further details – including descriptions of the key actors and eligibility criteria – provided in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. In all instances, the models draw on the outputs of the scoping exercises under Activities 1.1.5 and 1.2.1 to identify the potential actors and tailor the models to the local context. The models leverage partnerships between these actors to share risk and facilitate and incentivise the development of robust markets.

### 94. Interventions at a smallholder farmer level

### 95. Model 1 promotes the local supply of FL-RS interventions by using conditional procurements to effectively subsidise interventions at smallholder farmer level (referred to hereafter as 'smart-subsidies'). At its base, the smart-subsidy model enables agro-dealers to provide FL-RS to smallholder farmers at a discounted rate by using GCF funds to procure one item for every two items 21 procured and sold by an agro-dealer. The subsidy is passed down to the smallholder farmers as a discount on the purchase price22.

### 96. The interventions are typically focused on smallholder farmers, with lower individual ticket sizes to the end-users — i.e. for FL-RS such as tarpaulins and plastic sheets, metal and plastic silos, and hermetic bags. The flow of funds will be facilitated through a trust/escrow account23 at a local financial institution (FI) and released once proof of offtake by eligible smallholder farmers has been obtained. The instruction for release of the funds will be made by AGRA once the implementation unit is satisfied that the conditions have been met. On release, the funds will be transferred directly to the supplier. However, while funds will pass through an FI, it should be noted that no GCF funds will be transferred to a financial institution as the end beneficiary. The aim of the model is two-fold:  

- To stimulate production and manufacturer capacity by placing pre-emptive orders of FL-RS, however, maintaining control over risk through a conditional release of funds to the manufacturer; and  
- To reduce the cost of interventions at smallholder farmer level, additional profitability will drive additional demand and facilitate knowledge sharing about the benefits of interventions.

### 97. An overview of Model 1 is presented in Figure 7, with more detailed descriptions of each step in the text that follows. Under Model 1, RE-GAIN will target ~540,000 households for adoption of FL-RS. Average ticket size is $ 153 per household, of which approximately 30% on average will be subsidized through Model 1.

#### Note: the specific ratios will vary based on context of local markets in each country, with more developed markets receiving a smaller subsidy than less developed markets. A three-for-two model has been used as a base for demonstration purposes. The ratios for each target market will be finalised when the contract between suppliers FIs and AGRA are entered into. RE-GAIN staff will initiate negotiations on value for money in line with the state of the local market, which will be used to determine the ratios.

#### The maximum retail sales price and number of items per customer — as well as the eligibility criteria and geography where the sales at reduced cost can be made — will be included as part of the agreement between AGRA and the suppliers as a condition for participation in the scheme.

#### The account will be held in AGRA’s name with AGRA as the

## 104. Should the suppliers, agro-dealers or farmers require additional finance for their role in the system, they will be able to approach local financial institutions that already exist in all the target countries. For example, if the supplier needs additional working capital or capital investment to satisfy the additional FL -RS demand26, they can enter into a loan agreement with an FI to cover the additional liquidity needs for providing FL-RS. While AGRA can offer advice to suppliers or agro-dealers on this matter, the agreements themselves will be outside of the scope of the RE-GAIN Programme and will not include AGRA. The order placed by RE-GAIN will help de-risk the loan provision to the supplier, but no RE-GAIN Programme funds will be used to lend to the supplier or to make payments to an FI.

## 105. This model creates a win-win scenario for all actors, with the manufacturer ultimately receiving the full value of the FL-RS provided, the agro-dealer making income from their markup, and the farmers receiving FL-RS at a highly discounted rate. The market created will enable manufacturers to increase production with reduced risk, eventually bringing down the cost of FL-RS in the local market, enabling the smart-subsidies to be phased out.

## 106. Interventions at a cooperative or SME level  

## 107. Model 2 will use GCF grants to provide a unique approach to unlocking higher value items and food loss reducing equipment to farmer collectives or small to medium sized enterprises. The term ‘youth groups’ is used in this model description to highlight the priority given to this vulnerable demographic27; however, the model will also apply to other cooperatives, including women’s groups and local MSMEs. The aim of Model 2 is to create multi-stakeholder agreements with suppliers, youth groups, and financial institutions, with the goal of:  

- Enhancing creditworthiness – through repurchase assurances from the suppliers that lower the loss given default.  
- Reducing borrowing costs – Through a combination of the lowered credit risk (as per above) and subsidies on the purchase price. The structure will ensure higher value FL-RS become more affordable and thus accessible to youth groups who provide services to smallholder farmers.

## 108. At the heart of Model 2 is the engagement of local youth groups that will be supported to act as service providers for FL-RS that require more expensive equipment that can service multiple farmers — such as mechanical multi-crop threshers and shellers (preferably solar-powered), moisture meters and communal storage structures. The establishment of the service operations will be supported through the business development under Output 2.1, ensuring that the youth groups have the necessary foundation to provide a reliable service28. However, it is recognised that despite the proposed capacity development activities, some informal groups may still not fully meet the eligibility criteria for Model 2. In such instances, RE-GAIN will work with formalised MSMEs to partner with local youth groups; with the established MSMEs taking the lead in entering into the multi-stakeholder agreement (as per Step 1 below) and taking accountability for the management of the agreement. The MSMEs will recruit and work with informal youth groups from the community to operate the equipment and in the process develop their capacities29. Through this MSME-supervised approach, the informal youth groups will have the opportunity to build a track record, demonstrating their governance structures and creditworthiness for future opportunities. This approach leverages several key concepts to unlock the targeted benefits:  

- Collectivism provides benefits to smallholder farmers in economies of scale through cost sharing and increased bargaining power with off-takers. These should promote further profitability and therefore additional demand for FL-RS.  
- Post-harvest handling will increase the quality and quantity of agricultural produce, allowing smallholder farmers to capture more value, hence increased incomes.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 37

## Step 3: On receipt of the 70% payment from the FI, the supplier delivers the equipment to the youth group and reports on delivery to AGRA’s RE-GAIN PIU.

## Step 4: On receipt of the delivery report from the supplier, AGRA will release the 30% co-payment from the holding account to the supplier, completing the initial purchase agreement. At this stage, the youth group will have control over the use of the equipment, but ownership of the assets remains with the supplier or the FI (as agreed during Step 1).

## Step 5: The youth group, now in possession of the equipment, provides an FL-RS service to local farmers. Support under Output 2.1 will ensure that the youth groups are adequately capacitated to operate the service enterprise.

## Step 6: Smallholder farmers pay the youth group for the FL-RS service, with the youth group collecting income from multiple farmers; thereby distributing the cost of the equipment across multiple beneficiaries.

## Step 7: The youth groups use the income from the services to make repayments to the FI on the loan, covering the cost of the loan and the agreed interest. The upfront co-payment through RE-GAIN reduces the repayment burden on youth groups as compared to a scenario where a 100% loan would have been required, thereby decreasing the loan loss given default.

## Step 8: At the end of the agreed loan period, the FI concludes the transaction and reports on the outcome of the repayment, triggering Step 9.

## Step 9: The conclusion of the transaction will lead to one of two possible outcomes, as outlined below.

### Outcome 1: Market development was successful, indicated by the youth group operating an FL-RS service, enabling the full repayment of the loan. Under this scenario, the ownership of the asset will be formally transferred to the youth group, which will be able to continue to offer the service beyond the initial agreement, without the costs of servicing the loan.

### Outcome 2: Market development was unsuccessful, indicated by the failure of the youth group to make the required repayments on the loan. Under this scenario, the supplier’s repurchase assurance is triggered, through which the supplier buys back the asset (accounting for depreciation). The value of the repurchase will first go towards the repayment of any outstanding loan amount, and any associated transaction fees. Should the repurchase value exceed the outstanding loan amount, with any remaining value after transaction fees will be transferred to back to the youth group to compensate for any payments made before default.

## Alternative variations to these models may be introduced depending on the local context and the nature of the FL-RS. For example, the agreements may be adjusted to incorporate a rent-to-own mechanism31 directly with the supplier, through which incentives would be created for employment of local youth to operate the machines, with these youth taking ownership of the machine once the original cost of the machines is paid back from income generated by the machine.

In all cases, GCF grants will be used to make a co-payment on the equipment on behalf of the beneficiary (youth group or MSME), thereby reducing the financial burden of the transaction and de-risking the transaction for the suppliers or FIs involved in the agreement.

## Activity 2.2.2. Support inclusion of FL-RS in climate-resilient input packages provided by local financial institutions

While access to credit for farmers is limited across all the target countries, there are increasing efforts to develop tailored agricultural input packages from various financiers and government input subsidy

31 The rent-to-own model is a variation of Model 2, where the supplier can provide the equipment to the youth group without the need to include an FI. Under this variation, the youth group will make monthly rental payments to the supplier, but the ownership will remain with the supplier until such time that the agreed purchase value has been reached. In such instances, the GCF contribution will be used as a downpayment to reduce the monthly rental fees, or to reduce the duration of the rent-to-buy agreement.
