# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 38

## Programs

Financing is provided mostly through rural financial institutions, cooperatives, digital and alternative lenders, that assist farmers in accessing agricultural inputs. However, these packages generally focus on initial production, often excluding FL-RS from the list of supported inputs.

The RE-GAIN programme will, therefore, provide technical support through CIU and AGRA staff time to facilitate embedding the cost of FL-RS (e.g. hermetic technology) within the existing financing arrangements for input subsidies (in countries where such subsidy schemes exist like Ethiopia, Malawi and Zambia) to allow farmers to try out the technology and ask for inclusion in their input loan package in the following years.

Under this activity, GCF funds will not be used to directly fund subsidies, but rather to promote the bundling of FL-RS into existing subsidy programmes.

## Activity 2.2.3. Structure prefinancing partnership arrangements that include FL-RS

To further unlock financing for accessing FL -RS, the RE -GAIN programme will provide technical assistance through its PIU, CIU and AGRA staff to key value chain players — for example off-takers, input suppliers and farmers — and financial institutions to support the structuring of prefinancing partnerships that will derisk the provision of finance. Assistance will include convening key actors (through workshops, meetings, or other collaborative platforms), and training/capacity development that will help structure financial products based on risk sharing and risk reducing arrangements.

Some of these products are widely promoted by AGRA, after they have been successfully implemented in several of its focus countries, including Burkina Faso, Tanzania and Malawi.

## Component 3: Enabling environment to ensure sustainability of the FL-RS market

### Outcome 3: Strengthened enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS

The impacts of climate change on food loss in Africa is aggravated by the lack of an inadequate policy environment to support the development of sustainable markets for FL-RS. These policy-level constraints include the limited:

- i) availability of pan-African platforms on climate change that would facilitate action to minimize post-harvest losses and that are supported by evidence and responding to African needs and priorities;
- ii) access to climate change adaptation incentives, particularly finance to catalyse action and support SME investments;
- iii) weak regulatory and enforcement mechanism that would have reduced food loss;
- iv) biosystems-specific climate policies and implementation strategies supporting the entrepreneurial skills of youth in minimizing food losses.

In response to the challenges, Component 3 of the RE-GAIN programme will support the development of an enabling environment for the adoption of FL-RS at scale, working within the existing policy environment where needed, and supporting change where possible.

### Output 3.1: Enhanced capacity of national institutions to enable investments in FL-RS

To increase the sustainability and scalability of the positive impact created by the improved demand and supply of FL-RS, and improved access to financing and established market linkages, RE-GAIN programme will support the revision and operationalization of policies in support of enabling FL -RS investments, including tax exemptions/incentives, certification and standards for FL-RS quality, and promote successful FL-RS business models for scaling-up and replication.

Active involvement and support from government organizations, both central and local, will be crucial. The programme will align with other projects and programmes to leverage synergies, utilize existing laws and policies on FL reduction, SME promotion, and smallholder support, and ensure effective and efficient programme management. This includes rigorous monitoring and incorporating lessons learned.

Effective stakeholder engagement is essential and will involve raising awareness, providing programme information, and ensuring inclusivity for women, youth, minority groups, and all value chain actors. A grievance mechanism will also be put in place, with programme funds being used for awareness creation and mobilization through stakeholder engagement meetings 32.

Additionally, ensuring the availability of quality FL-RS and access to finance is vital to support long-term continuation.

> 32 The cost of the grievance mechanism itself will be covered by AGRA using AE Fees.

# Activity 3.1.1: Support the revision of policies in support of enabling FL-RS investments, including tax exemptions, reduced import duties, certification and standards for FL-RS quality

127. Under this Activity, AGRA will collaborate with government entities to identify critical policy gaps related to food loss, and revise and update key policies which are not responding to the current and future food losses and climate challenge scenario. This will include engaging relevant government ministries and agencies to review existing regulations and policies and proposing amendments to facilitate the development of local markets and support to farmers for the uptake of FL-RS. This will include introducing/strengthening national policies that provide financial incentives such as tax exemptions, subsidies, or reduced interest rates for FL-RS investments, making it more attractive for private and public sector investments.

Evidence generated and lessons learned in FL-RS from target communities and counties will be used to develop capacity, influence policy and promote best practices at scale.

128. AGRA will also actively participate in policy advocacy initiatives, working with stakeholders to ensure that proposed changes support the long-term success of FL-RS initiatives as part of the broader food systems of the respective countries. In addition to support for policy revision, AGRA will support local entities in implementing existing policies and regulations that have potential to support the uptake of FL-RS but are currently not fully enforced or supported. These actions will result in a supportive policy environment that enables the successful implementation of the RE-GAIN programme and widespread adoption of FL-RS solutions.

# Activity 3.1.2: Support institutional capacity building of key local entities to support the scaling of successful FL-RS models

129. Through this activity, AGRA will provide technical assistance to local and national government entities engaged in crop value chains (directly or indirectly) to build their institutional capacity to support the scaling of successful FL-RS models and policies in their countries. This will include actively involving central and local government through the establishment of formal partnerships and facilitating regular meetings and consultations to ensure alignment of the RE-GAIN programme with national and regional development priorities. These partnerships will be complemented by technical assistance, including guidelines, targeting tools and technology delivery mechanisms, to develop the analytical capacity of partner institutions on the topics of climate change and the contribution of FL-RS to GHG reduction and food security — enabling these metrics to be tracked and reported on in relation to the NDCs — as well as for the scenario-based prediction of mycotoxins during post-harvest period under changing climate.

130. RE-GAIN will also work with governments and other development partners to promote private sector investment in post-harvest infrastructure, including storage facilities, processing units and cold chains, as well as supporting regulatory bodies to develop and implement quality standards and certification processes for FL-RS products and services, ensuring they meet required levels of reliability and efficiency. Support will also be provided to enhance the capacities of extension services, and climate advisory services to monitor food loss in relation to climate conditions and the impact of FL-RS in reducing loss, as well as identifying and reporting on opportunities for upscaling and replication.

131. Finally, the programme will leverage on other investments to create synergies with other players in building institutional capacity of governments and the private sector in FL-RS, which may include enhancing coordination among country actors and institutions dealing with post-harvest loss under changing climates.

# Activity 3.1.3: Develop a business case for investments in FL-RS manufacturing, supply, service provision and financing to promote successful FL-RS business models for scaling-up and replication

132. A key step in unlocking investments and creating sustainable markets for FL-RS in Africa revolves around creating solid business cases that account for the needs of all actors – including manufacturers, suppliers, financers, agro-dealers, service providers and farmers. RE-GAIN will, therefore, research and compile case studies of successful FL-RS initiatives implemented through the programme, as well as other best practices observed across the region and develop models and approaches for scaling and influence. This research will form the foundation of the business case for FL-RS, detailing best practice and benefits of

### Activity 3.1.4: Implement a comprehensive monitoring, evaluation and learning system for FL-RS  
133. The programme will develop a robust monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) framework that includes clear performance indicators, data collection methods, and regular reporting schedules. This system will build on the foundation of existing MEL frameworks within the AGRA system to track the progress of programme activities, assess the impact of interventions, and identify areas for improvement. The MEL framework will also establish mechanisms for continuous learning by regularly reviewing M&E data, conducting periodic evaluations, and gathering feedback from stakeholders. These insights will be used to make informed adjustments to the programme, ensuring that it remains relevant and effective.

134. Through the MEL framework, the RE-GAIN Programme will foster knowledge sharing, improve performance through a continuous feedback loop and collaboration among stakeholders. This will be supported through the activities under Components 1 and 2 to encourage the formation of farmer groups, cooperatives and networks where farmers, MSMEs and private sector actors can exchange experiences, share best practices and collectively address challenges related to post-harvest management. The knowledge generated will be also shared regionally through AGRA’s networks to promote the scaling of the successful models across Africa and beyond. In the spirit of south-south cooperation, the programme will also coordinate with other projects to create synergies, identifying areas of overlap and collaboration. This will include developing joint action plans, sharing resources, and coordinating activities to maximize impact and avoid duplication of efforts.

## B.4. Implementation arrangements  
PSAA Applicant  

135. AGRA — an African-led institution with a vision of contributing to a food system-inspired inclusive agricultural transformation across Africa, of reducing hunger, improving nutrition, and adapting to climate change, and has been scaling agricultural innovations that help smallholder farmers towards increased incomes, better livelihoods, and improved food security will serve as the PSAA Applicant for this project.  

136. AGRA was founded in 2006, bringing 18 years of experience in a varied sectors related to food security, including seeds systems, sustainable farming, policy and state capability and inclusive markets and trade. Efforts across these main thematic areas are built on the foundation of several key cross-cutting themes, particularly climate change, nutrition and gender responsiveness.  

137. The organisation operates in 12 countries, employing over 300 staff, and managing an average annual operating budget of USD 90 million. AGRA also hosts the Partnership for Inclusive Agriculture Transformation in Africa (PIATA); a partnership between AGRA and five other major donors (BMGF, Rockefeller, BMW, MCF, FCDO and USAID) with AGRA as the implementing partner. PIATA is focused on working with public and private partners to support 12 countries in Africa to create and sustain Inclusive Agriculture transformation with a budget of over USD 500M in the next five years.  

138. As a continental institution in agriculture and food systems, AGRA is well placed to undertake the planned activities through its widespread infrastructure and partnerships on the continent, having strong AGRA country teams in the target countries. Using AGRA’s continental forums, the Africa Food Systems Forum (AFSF) and Agribusiness Deal Room, where AGRA brings on board connections with governments, partners and other stakeholders to enhance ownership and coordinate key actors, knowledge and resources, which are fundamental to take food loss innovation to scale.  

139. AGRA is known as a trusted broker by the governments it works with which will enable it to ensure the programme is aligned with national climate change policies, strategies, plans and instruments. Using AGRA’s ongoing projects across the continent as leverage, AGRA will drive a paradigm shift for building the resilience of smallholder farmers and agricultural communities in the seven focus countries and across Africa. The lessons learnt, especially on financing food loss reduction and its relationship with climate change, will have the potential of generating new knowledge on ways of engaging MSMEs and smallholder farmers.

### 140. The AGRA HQ senior leadership and technical leads will be responsible for the overall supervision and coordination of the project, including ensuring:  
i) funds are effectively managed to deliver results and achieve objectives;  
ii) the quality of project monitoring; and

The AGRA HQ team will be the primarily liaison with the GCF.

## Executing Entity (EE)

### 141. The project will be executed directly by AGRA through Programme Implementation Unit (PIU)34. Through this unit, AGRA will provide key resources, including Finance, Grant Management and Procurement Officers who will provide financial and administrative management, overseeing financial, contractual, procurement and logistics aspects for the project from the Nairobi Headquarters. The unit will oversee planning and quality assurance; supervise programme monitoring, evaluation and reporting; ensure timely realization of all programme deliverables; provide leadership and technical support to implementing partners; and ensure smooth communication flow across all programme partners. This executing role will be fulfilled both through the Nairobi-based headquarters, and AGRA’s country offices, and will report to the AGRA senior leadership.

### 142. The EE is responsible for:  
- Execution of the project  
- Procurement of services specifically (major procurement and contracting)  
- Facilitating partnerships  
- Managing contracts, monitoring results  
- Annual reporting by country offices to the PIU  

### 143. AGRA deploys a diverse set of delivery models to deliver its country and institutional strategy. It offers services through its expert staff, placed at headquarters in Nairobi; at the East, Southern and West Africa regional offices; as well as at country offices. AGRA staff work with downstream partners and local organizations to implement specific components of a contracted programme area with the aim to improve local organizations’ capacity, build institutional capacity and ensure long term ownership and sustainability of its interventions. AGRA provides Technical Assistance (TA) in the form of short-to medium-term expertise support (through consultants where needed) embedded within or seconded to mandated national, regional and continental institutions (e.g., government ministries, regional economic communities) to drive desired change, and in some instances consultants are hired to support specific assignments that require skilled expertise. AGRA is a convener (brings stakeholders together around a change agenda, e.g., the Africa Food Systems Summit) facilitating connections and interactions between different actors and stakeholders within the agriculture and food systems sector. AGRA utilizes advocacy and communication as key tools for change. The specific delivery models will be determined at the implementation stage and will depend on each country context.

## Responsible Units

### 144. The EE team at the Nairobi HQ will be supported by AGRA country offices in each of the seven target countries who will serve as responsible units. These units will support on-the-ground coordination and implementation, as well as being mandated for specific outputs/activities.

## 145. AGRA will establish a Programme Advisory Group (PAG) made up of senior representatives from AGRA’s Integrated Programme Management (IPM) unit35 that will serve as the starting point to guide innovation, impact scale and adaptive thought leadership to shape the partnership at the continental level. AGRA envisions this Advisory Group will meet on quarterly as part of the IPM meetings.

## Programme Implementation Unit

## 146. A central Programme Implementation Unit (PIU) will be established at AGRA’s Nairobi headquarters to oversee implementation of the entire programme across all seven countries. This unit will report to the PAG and be comprised of two sub-groups; a Programme Management Unit (PMU) and a Technical Expert Group (TEG), as described below.

### Programme Management Unit

## 147. The Programme will establish a management unit that will be functional for the entire duration and be responsible for day-to-day implementation of the project. The PMU will offer overall management, implementation and general technical direction of the entire programme, ensuring an integrated vision among different components. The PMU will consist of five full time positions: i) PMU Lead; ii) Senior Finance Officer; iii) Procurement Officer; iv) Project Analyst; and v) M&E Officer. The PMU will be based in AGRA Nairobi Headquarters, with in-country support from responsible units in the country offices.

### Technical Expert Group

The TEG, also situated within the Nairobi Headquarters, will provide expertise to assist the PMU in the technical implementation of the RE-GAIN programme. The TEG will include several full-time positions, including: i) Program Officer — Gender, Youth and Inclusion; ii) Technical Advisor — Inclusive Finance and BDS; iii) Technical Advisor — Extension and Value Chain Development. These full-time roles will be supported by several part-time technical team members, including: i) Technical Advisor — Inclusive Markets and Finance; ii) Lead — Sustainable Farming, Distribution and Youth in Extension; iii) Technical Advisor — Livelihood Resilience and Climate Adaption; iv) Head: M&E; and v) Technical Advisor — Food Loss Reduction Analytics.

## Country-level Implementation Units

The PIU will be assisted in project implementation within each target country by a country-level implementation unit (CIU) which will be established in each of the AGRA country offices36 and will be comprised of country-office staff. The CIUs will be responsible for managing day-to-day operations in each country, reporting directly to the PIU, as well as providing regular reports to the relevant Project Steering Committee (see below). Specific responsibilities of the CIU for each project Output are outlined in the first row of Table 6 below.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 47

## 157. Low productivity, aggravated by harvest and postharvest loss, has been the major driver of climate vulnerability as farming is expanding to fragile ecosystems, wetlands and mountains, causing deforestation, land degradation and drying of water resources, exposing farmers to extreme events.

## 158. In response to these climate change threats on food security, the RE-GAIN programme aims to enhance the climate resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholders by promoting the widespread adoption of FL-RS in seven African countries. AGRA, as the lead agency, is requesting GCF grant funds to support the roll out of a suite of physical and management interventions alongside capacity building and enhanced financial and market access. The rationale for GCF concessional support – beyond the basic climate change attribution described above – is presented below.

## Barriers to public or private finance alternatives

## 159. Experiences to date show that countries rarely invest in climate change adaptation, less so in reducing food loss. This is largely due to the constrained fiscal space in the public sector, which limits opportunity to invest in catalytic programme such as RE-GAIN using public funds. Constraints on public finance are further compounded by the already high levels of external debt, which prevents governments access to loan finance without putting considerable strain on the national economy. Given these constraints and the resulting high levels of external dependency, the target countries are reliant on external funding and support for climate change adaptation initiatives. However, international aid and climate finance flows may be insufficient or unpredictable, making it difficult for these countries to plan and implement long-term adaptation and mitigation strategies.

## 160. Looking to the private sector as an alternative, it is highly challenging to secure finance from conventional sources in the market for such resilience-building interventions unless market demand is created and appropriate market linkages are put in place. The return on investment for such programmes is often seen as unattractive for private sector investors, and the element of innovation that characterizes the necessary climate adaptation initiatives is viewed by the market as untested, with the absence of a track record of financial soundness translating to a high estimation of risk among potential investors. This challenge is compounded by the fact that resource-poor smallholder farmers may not be willing to invest in the FL-RS without a clear demonstration of the effectiveness of the solutions – particularly given that some of the technologies are unaffordable and farmers need a quasi-guarantee about the viability of these solutions before investing.

## 161. Based on the stakeholder mapping AGRA conducted during the country strategy development, existing interventions and support focus mainly on last mile delivery, with few actors willing or able to invest in unlocking the systemic challenges that would facilitate agriculture and food systems transformation. AGRA sees this as a gap and a potential opportunity for creating significant impact if interventions incentivize the markets inclusively, the right level of awareness and adoption is created and access to finance is de-risked.

## 162. Building the necessary capacity to develop and implement effective adaptation plans and investments requires substantial investments in education, training, and institutional strengthening, which, although catalytic, are generally considered public goods with limited direct return. International aid is, therefore, necessary to initiate a paradigm shift in the sector, supporting Africa-led solutions to tackle national priorities and investment plans.

## Consideration of alternative funding models.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 48  

## 163. In the process of designing the RE-GAIN programme, numerous other alternative finance models were considered. While each option has some merit, each faces considerable barriers that prevent these models from working on their own. These alternative models are summarised below and have been factored into the design of innovative finance models that the proposed programme will initiate through Component 2.

- Farmers pay cash for the hermetic technologies and for using the FL-RS services if they have savings and or alternative sources of income. This is often not the case in most contexts and countries.
- Farmers can get the FL-RS on credit from the supplier of the technology (agro-dealers) and/or service providers if these MSMEs have the liquidity to give credit to their customers. This is often not the case in most countries and contexts. A mix of finance from vendors and suppliers, own contribution from farmers and additional funding from GCF can solve for the access to finance.
- Service providers offering FL-RS could get equipment financing through wealthy individuals, family and friends or through financial institutions or equipment vendors. However, there is inherent risk, both for the lender and borrowers, with several barriers currently preventing access to finance. Innovative finance models are needed to overcome this barrier.
- While credit options are possible, borrowers would need to offset the risk for lenders through co-contributions as well as the provision of some form of guarantee or collateral.
- This option is further hindered by the high interest rates in some countries (particularly those with high inflation) and transaction costs for accessing financing are high in rural contexts (documentation, transport).
- There are risks for lenders (first time borrowers and their lack of collateral and transaction history) as the financing of FL-RS in food crops have been minimal till date.
- The costs for lenders (loan origination, assessment and monitoring) are higher than for other sectors and loan products.
- There is also risk to potential borrowers (market risk, profitability risk), as the business case for many of the FL-RS have not yet been proven in rural contexts for staple crops produced by small scale producers.
- Extension services (for example the provision of information on weather and harvesting practices) could be paid for with Government budget. However, most Governments are constrained in their budgets for extension services (they are over-indebted and have many competing priorities), hence the need for donor funds to complement the government budget.
- The proposed awareness activities could be financed by the suppliers of the FL-RS; however their reach is limited, and they might not be able to pay 100% of the investment costs for creating awareness in rural areas as the profit margins do not make up for that investment. Private sector does invest in the activities that give the highest return on investment and that might not be on products sold in rural areas to low-income small-scale farmers.

## Rationale for concessional funding

### 164. AGRA is requesting concessional finance in the form of grants for the RE-GAIN programme. The need for full concessionality is based on the nature of the investments, which require targeted capacity development support combined with strategic conditional procurements to catalyse a paradigm shift in the post-harvest value chain using innovative FL-RS in the seven target countries.

The specific details on the nature of intervention, their suitability for concessional finance, and how the programme will transfer the benefits of concessionality directly to farmers are described in more detail below.

- De-risk first time users to invest in new technology that remains unproven in their context. The “pay for two, get one free” type of investment will make sure the concessional funding end up in farmers' pockets.
- Reduce the cost of distributing FL-RS to rural areas where demand is not yet confirmed (the concessional funding will de-risk agro-dealer and distributors from remaining with unsold stock).
- Reduce the risk for FL-RS service providers to invest in equipment for which the business case is not yet proven. The manufacturers will be paid, and the service providers will not be left with unpaid debts in case the equipment is not able to be operated at profitable scale.
- De-risk investments in awareness creation by sellers and distributors of FL-RS (extension, demonstrations, etc.) because awareness creation does not necessarily translate in adoption of FL-RS and profit for the sellers of FL-RS.

# B 
## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 49  

### ▪ Incentivize farmers to adopt FL-RS by reducing the cost of the FL-RS and enhance the availability of FL-RS at the right time.   
165. The level of concessionality transferred to the end user through the ‘smart-subsidies’ will depend on a number of factors which differ per country and per FL-RS. The less developed the local context is on any of the following factors, the greater the subsidy relative to the co-contribution from the recipient. These factors include:  

- Is the FL-RS technology proven to be profitable in financial terms for all actors: farmers, agro-dealers, service providers?  
- Is the distribution network of FL-RS already in place and reaching all areas where the FL-RS is needed?  
- Are farmers already aware of the FL-RS and ready to adopt? Is there a ready market?  
- Are manufacturers capable of forecasting demand and prefinance stock of FL-RS where the demand is?  
- Does the Government have the knowledge and tools to provide the FL-RS information in time and with sufficient accuracy to help farmers harvest in the right time using the right practices?  

## B.6. Exit strategy  
166. Long-term sustainability is at the core of the programme design, with GCF investments seen as catalysers of change within local systems. A key aspect of this GCF intervention is having an exit strategy through the creation of an enabling environment to shift away from the smart-subsidies, catalysing further investments in FL-RS under Component 2. This approach to sustainability is centred on market development and stimulating the local supply chains to bring FL-RS to smallholder farmers at affordable rates — working through the private sector to foster innovation and commercial opportunities. To establish sustainable supply chains, the programme will forge market-led relationships with value chain actors such as off-takers, processors, aggregators, business development service providers and financial institutions, deepening and strengthening the networks that connect farmers to market. When combined with the demand-oriented actions of awareness, demonstration and market development under Outcome 1, the programme will result in a balance of supply, demand, and finance opportunity that will enable these relationships and commercial opportunities to persist within the target communities and expand to spill over areas beyond initial GCF investment period. Strengthening a market-led system is particularly important for the economic value that is being created through the adaptation components of RE-GAIN; these will ensure that the farmers and the supporting value chain actors continue to see value in the deployment of FL-RS even after the GCF project comes to an end. Further, the strengthening of an enabling public policy and institutions will ensure continued promotion of FL-RS through extension systems beyond the life of the project. Support for these systems among communities will be further strengthened by the long-term adaptation support provided through the environmental and social co-benefits. Such benefits, when combined with the strengthened awareness of climate risk, will further incentivise farmers to continue with FL-RS beyond the project lifespan.  

167. At the community level, the programme is expected to actively strengthen and grow agricultural MSMEs (including farmer cooperatives, women’s and youth groups) through access to finance and business development services to enhance development of FL-RS technologies, helping them attain a higher level of self-sufficiency that is expected to increase the sustainability of the full agriculture value chain, especially at post-harvest. It is also expected that by demonstrating a positive track record of successful direct investments in rural MSMEs and farmer organization, as well as by funding financial intermediaries for the creation or expansion of FL-RS investment portfolios, the GCF fund will incentivize the wider financial sector to focus on the agriculture sector through the design and development of appropriate products, thereby contributing to its increased sustainability of the programme. This will further enhance opportunities for continued investment in FL-RS beyond the initial subsidised finance models.  

168. To ensure balance in interventions and sustainability of the programme, an end-to-end process approach will be used to for inclusion of stakeholders, from food system input processes, productivity, output processes, post-harvest handling and marketing. This will be context-specific, and the depth of investments will depend on existing AGRA or partners’ investments. The programme will take a market-led approach to ensure sector sustainability, innovation and scale. Through the various capacity development activities, the programme will invest in strengthening the capacity of VBA extension workers.

### 171. Capacity development is a key aspect of the programme design, cutting across all three project components. Under Component 1, capacity development will focus on training smallholder farmers on the use of improved climate-resilient FL-RS (USD 6M) and agricultural traders and processors on complementary services for FL-RS (USD 1.4M). The capacity of extension workers and agro-dealers will also be enhanced to further scale the provision of training support to farmers, traders and processors, with specific training of trainers programme (USD 2M).

### 172. Under Component 2, similar capacity development support will be provided to train new FL-RS service providers such as MSMEs, cooperatives, incl. women- and youth-led initiatives (USD 3.2M), including business development support and market intelligence (USD 1.8M), as well as capacity and market development for all supply-side market actors (USD 1.8M).

### 173. Under Component 3, the programme will provide institutional capacity development support to key local entities to support the scaling of successful FL-RS models (USD 2.6M).

### In terms of technology, the project will not develop new technologies but will invest in technology transfers for existing FL-RS. This will be approached primarily through the development of innovative finance mechanisms under Component 2, using conditional procurements to leverage private finance through partnerships with financial service providers, agro-dealers, MSMEs, youth groups, and smallholder farmers (USD 32.7M).

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## D.1. Impact potential  
### Adaptation Impact 

174. The proposed programme will yield significant adaptation benefits for vulnerable smallholder farmers, their families, and communities across the seven target countries. At the core of these benefits is the expected reduction in climate change-induced food losses that are increasingly being experienced across Africa. Such food loss reductions will have multiple positive impacts on the lives and livelihoods of these highly vulnerable communities, mainly by enhancing the absorption capacity of smallholder farmers to seasonal climate and other shocks, building their resilience in the face of climate change. At the most fundamental level, reduced food loss will directly contribute to food security in the target communities — thereby contributing to GCF Adaptation Result Area (ARA) 2: Health and well-being, and food and water security. 

While climate change is known to impact the full spectrum of food systems — from production through to consumption — the proposed programme’s focus on post-harvest food loss addresses a critical, but somewhat under-served, component of food security. Reduced food loss through improved management at harvest and postharvest phases will not only improve food security, but also increase farmers income and farm-level profitability. Additionally, reduction of food loss also leads to preserving the integrity of natural ecosystems that further has a positive impact on the resilience of farmers. The impacts of this programme are, therefore, considered as complementary to the broader adaptation strategies for agricultural resilience within each target country, filling a key gap in the broader landscape of food security. However, despite the complementarity of this approach with others focused more on primary production, the expected impacts on food security would remain even when viewed in isolation.

175. The nature of the approach – targeting smallholder farmers, with particular focus on women, youth groups, and MSMES — means that the resilience benefits will apply to the most vulnerable people and communities – thereby contributing to GCF ARA 1. In addition to the increased food security, these benefits will include the increased resilience of rural livelihoods, with significant investments being made in developing local markets and building up farmer cooperatives, youth and women’s groups, and MSMEs. The resulting investments extend beyond just the initial GCF contribution, with catalytic grants creating sustainable systems that will leverage partnerships with financial institutions and private sector actors to mobilise significant investment beyond the proposed project.

176. Through these results, the project will directly benefit a total of 2.62 million people across the seven countries, distributed across the even target countries as shown in Table 7. Further details on the methodology for determining beneficiary numbers are presented in Supplementary Annex 1: Adaptation Impact Estimation.

## Post-harvest value chain — including farmers, agro-dealers, suppliers, off-takers and decision-makers — to establish sustainable programmes that enable farmers to adopt improved FL-RS in a manner that is financially viable and climate resilient.

A clear focus on achieving scale and sustainability is embedded in the programme design, both through the program’s continental reach and its ambition to work on the ground within seven countries through employing tested and functional last mile delivery systems. This commitment to achieving scale and sustainability is underpinned by the programme’s emphasis on achieving transformational systems change, as described below.

## Potential for scaling up and replication

178. The nature of the programme approach is such that it will stimulate local supply of affordable FL-RS options that will enable the scaling of interventions beyond the scope of the GCF investment. Specifically, the innovative finance mechanisms under Output 2.2, along with the MSME business development and market support under Output 2.1, will catalyse the establishment of and/or strengthen local supply chains that will bring FL-RS to smallholder farmers at affordable rates. A key feature of this approach is the notion of working through the private sector to foster innovation and commercial opportunity. AGRA’s delivery model will demonstrate and scale innovations that enable smallholder farmers to participate in both input and output markets, and in doing so to forge commercial relationships with off-takers, processors, exporters, commercial aggregators, business development service providers and financial institutions. This ecosystem focus builds sustainability and strengthens resilience by deepening and strengthening the networks that connect farmers to market. The FL-RS solutions will be disseminated at scale by integrating best practices into major national and regional flagship programs and anchor firms. When combined with the demand-oriented actions of awareness, demonstration and market development under Outcome 1, the programme will result in a balance of supply, demand, and finance opportunity that will enable the scaling of interventions in the target communities beyond initial GCF investment.

179. In addition to the scaling potential noted above, the programme is structured such that the approaches to stimulating the FL-RS market can be replicated; not only within other communities in the target countries, but also regionally across Africa. This replication potential is initially rooted in the demonstration effect the project will have, which when combined with the convening power of AGRA across Africa will attract additional market actors to engage in similar investment schemes. To further facilitate replication, Component 3 of the project will establish an enabling environment for sustainable FL-RS markets (see below). While these enabling activities will focus on replication at the national level, AGRA will facilitate collaboration with continental and regional bodies and national governments, including African regional climate institutions and Regional Economic Communities, to initiate the replication of the programme’s innovative models for scaling FL-RS across the region.

## Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

180. The innovations, especially on innovative financing and business models, within the programme have the potential to generate new knowledge on ways of engaging MSMEs and smallholder farmers in reducing their food losses and thus increasing their productivity and their resilience to climate shocks. Activities under Component 3 will capture these lessons and develop knowledge products to share best practices at the regional level. This knowledge sharing component will be fundamental to the replication potential described above.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and creation of an enabling environment

181. Through the partnerships that will be established under the innovative finance and business models, the programme will address key barriers that limit the scaling up of FL-RS. This will be complemented by the policy focus with national governments in partner countries to help them realize their commitments to tackling climate change and improving food security in their countries. This will include supporting national governments to enact forward-thinking policies and regulations that seek to integrate climate-resilient FL-RS into the agriculture sector, as well as advocating for the establishment of tax incentives, and the uptake of certifications and standards for FL-RS.

# D.3. Sustainable development

182. In addition to the core impacts described in D.1 above, the proposed programme will elicit multiple co-benefits that will contribute to the sustainable development goals (SDGs) of the target countries. Key contributions to the SDGs are listed in Table 8.

## Table 8. Key Sustainable Development Goal Contributions

| SDG | How the project contributes |
|-----|-----------------------------|
| SDG 2: End hunger, achieve food security and improved nutrition and promote sustainable agriculture | The programme will introduce FL-RS that will reduce food loss, thereby improving the net productivity of farms, contributing to food security. |
| SDG 5: Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls | The proposed project has a strong focus on gender-responsive actions that will empower women within the post-harvest agriculture value chain. |
| SDG 8: Promote sustained, inclusive and sustainable economic growth, full and productive employment and decent work for all | The business development and market strengthening approaches under the proposed programme will create inclusive employment opportunities, with a particular focus on opportunities for vulnerable groups such as women and the youth. |
| SDG 13: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts | The programme is directly focused on building resilience among smallholder farming communities by addressing the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food loss. |

## Environmental co-benefits

183. Programme interventions to reduce post-harvest food loss will have several environmental co-benefits.

- **Reduced land degradation and improved water use efficiency**: Reducing post-harvest food loss will increase the net production of agricultural land, thereby reducing area of land and amount of water needed to produce sufficient food to meet demand. FL-RS will, therefore, improve the efficiency of land and water use in the agriculture sector, thereby reducing the impact of agriculture on land and water resources. This includes curtailing some of the drivers of agricultural expansion associated with the need to compensate for food loss. The resultant reduction in the rate of land use change will help preserve natural ecosystems and the important services they provide.

- **Decreased in net use of fertilisers and pesticides**: The improved net-productivity of agriculture will reduce the environmental impact of fertilisers and pesticides, as well as the related energy demands, particularly as related to regrowing of lost food.

- **Reduction in air pollutants from waste food disposal**: Reduced food waste will limit the potential environmental impacts of improper disposal of spoilt food – for example the release of air pollutants when waste is burned, or the production of methane during decomposition (see mitigation benefits in Annex 22).

- **Increased regreening and improved land use**: Reduced expansion of farming will release more land to reafforestation, land rehabilitation and other services.

- **Reduced loss of biodiversity**: Land expansion into forests and fragile areas is the major cause of biodiversity loss on the continent, which is threatening the livelihoods of current and future generations.

# D. GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 57  

## Mitigation Impact  

Through the process of reducing food losses and creating capacity of key players in sustainable FL-RS solutions, the proposed programme will reduce greenhouse gas emissions; contributing to each country’s mitigation targets. Emission reductions will be achieved through two primary pathways. The first, and most direct pathway is the reduction of emissions arising from the production of compensatory food (that is, food produced to compensate for food lost in harvest and post-harvest without considering land expansion). The second emission reduction pathway is through the reduction of food loss as a driver of land use change linked to compensatory agricultural expansion. Combined, these pathways will result in an estimated total emission reduction of 111,275 tCO2e over 10 years. Details on the emission reduction methodology and justifications are presented in Annex 22, with a country-specific breakdown provided in Table 10.

## Socioeconomic and health co-benefits  

184. In targeting the livelihoods of highly vulnerable smallholder farming communities, the proposed programme will yield multiple socioeconomic co-benefits. Some of these co-benefits arise as a direct result of the core impact of improved food security, while others arise as a result of the actions to develop the FL-RS market in target communities.

- **Increased food quality and safety:** Improved post-harvest processes that reduce food waste will also improve the quality of food reaching consumers, which will enhance the nutritional value and reduce the risk of food-related illnesses resulting from contamination by pests, molds, aflatoxins and other pathogens. This will not only benefit individuals, but a more stable and safe food supply will enhance public health systems through reduced incidence of nutrition-related illness.
- **Job creation:** By working with local suppliers and agro-dealers, while also supporting the business development of MSMEs as service providers in the FL-RS value chain, the proposed programme will generate employment opportunities that will extend beyond the initial investment. This will particularly benefit vulnerable groups such as women and youth that will be actively targeted for support in business development initiatives.
- **Market stability:** Reducing food loss can help stabilize food prices by ensuring a more consistent supply. This can protect both producers and consumers from price volatility.

## Gender-sensitive development benefit  

185. The proposed programme has been designed with gender sensitivity at its core; with each component carrying key gender-responsive actions to not only ensure equal participation of women, but also actively empower women within the FL-RS value chain. This includes business development opportunities directly targeted at women’s groups and women-led enterprises, recognising them as key actors in the market and supporting these groups to establish robust, sustainable services in the FL-RS value chain. The delivery mechanism of the project which primarily focuses on targeting farmer groups provides a platform that are likely to promote gender and youth integration and therefore lead to development outcomes that are gender sensitive.

187. As noted in Section B.1, climate change impacts the full spectrum of food production systems in Africa, from preparation of the land through the production and harvest, to the processing, storage and marketing of the produce . While the impacts across the whole system are well documented, the lion’s share of research and resources for resilience interventions in the agricultural sector hav e been focused thus far on production, leaving a considerable gap in investments in the post-harvest stages of the process. In the absence of innovative initiatives to develop post-harvest food loss reduction systems , the target populations are left vulnerable to the impacts of post-harvest losses on their food security, which are being exacerbated by climate change. Given the high levels of poverty, weak markets, and low adaptive capacity experienced in smallholder farming communities, farmers are left unable to adopt climate-resilient FL-RS without initial external support. Moreover, the national governments of each country, while prioritising climate-resilient agriculture development, lack the financial, institutional and technical capacity to establish sustainable markets for FL-RS, with the majority of limited resources being directed towards production.

188. The RE-GAIN programme targets this vulnerable stage of the value chain that, despite the urgent need, has remained relatively underdeveloped at the global scale – particularly when considering smallholder farmers. By targeting local suppliers and agro-dealers, while also supporting the business development of MSMEs as service providers in the FL -RS value chain, RE -GAIN will build resilience directly within the target populations, integrating the solutions into local systems that meet the individual needs of each community.

189. More details on the needs of each country and the tailoring of interventions to each local context are provided in the country-specific Feasibility Studies appended to Annex 2.

#### Table 11. Key alignment with national policies and priorities for climate change and agriculture in each of the target countries.

##### CC Strategies and Policies

###### Burkina Faso  
Burkina Faso’s National Sustainable Development Policy (PNDD) includes a key focus on reducing post-harvest losses through improved storage, processing, and distribution techniques to enhance food security and promote sustainable agricultural practices. This is complemented by the National Economic and Social Development Plan II 2021-2025 which highlights improving the handling, storage, and processing of agricultural products to reduce post-harvest losses as part of the strategy to restore security and peace, strengthen national resilience, and transform the economy for inclusive and sustainable growth.

The National Climate Change Adaptation Plan (PNA) also addresses the need for resilience in agricultural systems amid climate variability and change, underscoring adaptive agricultural practices and sustainable methods to secure food and nutritional needs. The National Agricultural Investment Program further reinforces these efforts, providing strategic guidance to the agricultural sector to reduce post-harvest losses and improve food security by addressing aflatoxin-triggered losses, among other issues. Similarly, the National Strategy for the Development of Agricultural Entrepreneurship by 2025 includes focus on reducing post-harvest losses through improved storage facilities, processing techniques, and investments in modern infrastructure and training for farmers, while the Agricultural Value Chain Development Strategy 2019 -2023 focuses on increasing productivity and reducing harvest and post-harvest losses through disseminating conservation technologies, building capacity on good practices, and constructing storage facilities. This approach is also promoted in the Agro-sylvo-pastoral Production Sectoral Policy 2018-2027.

Finally, Burkina Faso’s revised its Nationally Determined Contribution for the period 2021 –2025 sets ambitious targets for reducing greenhouse gas emissions and adapting to climate change —including through the promotion of regenerative agriculture and food systems.

###### Ethiopia  
Ethiopia’s Agricultural Transformation Agenda focuses on improving productivity and reducing post-harvest losses through infrastructure development and training. The Postharvest Management Strategy in Grains (2018) and the Working Strategy for Strengthening Ethiopia’s Teff Value Chain (2015) also both focus on reducing post-harvest losses and enhancing value chains.

In addition to these policies aimed specifically at post-harvest, numerous national policies and strategies identify agriculture as a key sector for sustainable and climate resilient development. This includes the Ten-Year Development Plan (2021-2030), the Homegrown Economic Reform Agenda, and Climate Resilient Green Economy Strategy which focus on agriculture and private sector involvement as a driver to reach middle-income status by 2025. The programme also aligns with investment roadmaps for prioritizing agricultural investments laid out in the National Agriculture Investment Plan (2022) and the Agricultural Sector Policy and Investment Framework (2010-2020). Ethiopia’s updated NDC highlights the mitigation potential in land use and forestry, targeting significant GHG reductions by 2030. The National Adaptation Plan (2017) focuses on agriculture and industry, aiming to reduce food losses and increase resilience. Additionally, the Agricultural Extension Strategy (2017-2030) integrates best practices for effective extension services, and the Nutrition Sensitive Agriculture Strategy (2017-2021) aims to mainstream nutrition in agricultural programs.

###### Kenya  
Kenya’s Vision 2030 aims to transform the country into a newly industrializing middle-income nation by 2030, focusing on modernizing agriculture and achieving a 10% annual economic growth rate. The National Climate Change Action Plan (2018-2022) strengthens the path towards sustainable, climate-resilient development, addressing agricultural emissions and enhancing productivity and resilience. Kenya’s Agricultural Sector Development Strategy (2010-2020) and the National Agriculture Investment Plan (2019-2024) promote market-oriented production and post-harvest loss reduction. Kenya’s Nationally Determined Contribution (2020-2030) seeks to abate GHG emissions by 30% by 2030, with the agriculture sector playing a key role. The National Adaptation Plan (2015-2

## Malawi  

The programme approach aligns with the priorities of the two core development agendas for Malawi, namely Malawi 2063 and the Malawi Growth Development Strategy, which both include focus on long-term agricultural development and economic transformation. The need for addressing climate change impacts and post-harvest food losses are recognised in the National Agriculture Policy (NAP 2016–2020), which propose measures that include the use of CIEWS and community-based storage. This was further highlighted in the Vision 2020 – The National Long Term Development Perspective, which highlighted the need for clear policies and technologies to reduce post-harvest losses. Malawi’s updated NDC (2021) also identifies agriculture as the largest contributor to GHG emissions, emphasizing mitigation and adaptation measures to enhance food security and resilience.

## Tanzania  

Tanzania’s comprehensive policy framework addresses agricultural productivity, climate resilience, and post-harvest loss management. The Tanzania Development Vision 2025 and the Long-Term Perspective Plan (2011–2021) set long-term development goals, emphasizing food security and sustainable development. The National Strategy for Growth and Reduction of Poverty II and the Third National Five-Year Development Plan focus on post-harvest loss management and agricultural productivity. The Agricultural Sector Development Programme Phase II (2017/18–2027/28) and the Agricultural Sector Development Strategy (2015/2016–2024/2025) aim to modernize agriculture, improve infrastructure, and enhance market access.

The National Climate Change Strategy (2012–2018) and the updated Nationally Determined Contribution (2021) target GHG emission reductions and climate resilience in agriculture. The National Postharvest Management Strategy (2019–2029) and its Implementation Plan (2019–2024) focus on reducing crop losses through improved storage, technology adoption, and market systems. Tanzania’s policies also include the Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan (2014–2019) and the National Agriculture Policy (2013) to promote sustainable agricultural practices and resilience. The ASDP II Communication Strategy (2020–2028) identifies key information needs for smallholders and strategic interventions to reduce post-harvest losses. The National Environment Policy (NEP 2021) and the National Climate Change Response Strategy (2021–2026) outline environmental and climate change goals.

## Uganda  

Uganda’s policy landscape includes long- and mid-term strategies focused on agricultural development, climate resilience, and post-harvest loss reduction. Uganda Vision 2040 aims to transform Uganda into a competitive upper-middle-income country by 2040, emphasizing agricultural development and reducing post-harvest losses through expanded market infrastructure. The Green Growth Development Strategy (2017/18–2030/31) ensures sustainable development in agriculture, natural capital management, and climate resilience, focusing on income enhancement, job creation, climate change adaptation, and resource use efficiency. The Third National Development Plan (2020–2025) prioritizes reducing post-harvest losses to enhance food security and increase agricultural productivity, with a focus on agro-industrialization.

The National Agriculture Policy (2013) aims to promote food security and household incomes through sustainable agricultural productivity and value addition. It emphasizes reducing post-harvest losses through improved storage, processing, and value addition. The National Cooperative Policy (2011) seeks to improve governance, production, value addition, and marketing capacities of cooperatives, addressing challenges like poor storage facilities and high post-harvest losses. The National Agricultural Extension Strategy (NAES 2016/17–2020/21) aims to establish a sustainable farmer-centred agricultural extension system, enhancing capacity in production, agribusiness, value addition, and post-harvest management. The National Grain Trade Policy (2015) targets improved grain handling and storage to reduce post-harvest losses, which range between 26% to 37%. It focuses on enhancing pre- and post-harvest practices, standardizing warehouses, and improving market access.

The National Climate Change Policy (2015) promotes climate-resilient and low-carbon development, with adaptation strategies to enhance agricultural systems' resilience and improve food storage and management. Uganda’s updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 2022) prioritizes adaptation in agriculture, aiming to reduce post-harvest losses from 37% to 12% by 2025 and to 3% by 2

# D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## Financial Structure – adequacy, reasonableness and return on investment

193. Affordability, access to finance, market access, financial literacy, and consumer awareness are key challenges for smallholder farmers seeking to adopt practices that reduce food loss in the agricultural value chain. Key to viably addressing these challenges is RE-GAIN's programmatic approach that combines demand-side, supply-side, and enabling environment components.

194. The combination of both financial and non-financial approaches effectively ensures market failures resulting from both the supplier and consumers side of the agricultural value chain are addressed.

195. The two key financial models—1) catalytic grants for smallholder farmers and 2) catalytic grants for agricultural MSMEs—use concessional funding to incentivize private sector manufacturers and financial institutions (FIs) to  
- Provide physical solutions that are within the affordability envelope of farmers while remaining commercially viable to private sector suppliers.  
- Provide tailored financial products that are affordable to farmers, while mitigating credit risk to financial institutions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 62  

## 196. By focusing on a 5-year period, the program aims to demonstrate long-term market viability to suppliers (both physical and financial), build the necessary credit history required to access financial products, and encourage adoption of physical solutions by farmers.

## 197. Non-financial interventions drive adoption of these solutions from farmers by focusing on building capacity and awareness through training, educational materials, and village-based agent (VBA) demonstrations, addressing information gaps and promoting financial literacy. These interventions are critical to creating linkages between private sector manufacturers, MSMEs and small hold farmers.

## 198. This combined approach of addressing both supply and demand side constraints is expected to mobilize US$ 144.95 million worth of physical solutions and reach 553,900 smallholder farmer households over the next 5 years. Given the programme cost of US$ 105 million this represents a catalytic effect or return of 1.38 times the original investment from the GCF. The financial return on investment exceeds 1 time the original investment across each of the seven countries. This return excludes the long-term impact of the programme’s ability to demonstrate the market viability of these physical solutions to both users (farmers) and suppliers (manufacturers and FIs), which will catalyse new private sector markets while addressing food-loss and associated climate related challenges.

## Economic Efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project/programme and return  

## 199. The proposed programme is efficient and effective, with a total investment of US$ 105 million expected to generate significant climate adaptation outcomes. By reducing post-harvest food losses, the programme contributes directly to increased food availability, with co-benefits of reduced greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions; projected reduction of around 0.1 gigatonne of CO₂-equivalent emissions. This mitigation co-benefit is coupled with economic and social benefits such as enhanced food security, improved household incomes, and better health outcomes through reduced aflatoxin exposure, improved food safety and availability.

## 200. An economic analysis for the programme was undertaken using a cost-benefit analysis approach. Programme costs and benefits were assessed over a 10-year period and using country-specific social discount rates as presented in Annex 3. The analysis weighed up the total programme costs against the benefits associated with the uptake of equipment by smallholder farming households. These benefits included the income gains associated with the value of food that would otherwise have been lost in the absence of the programme interventions, the health benefits from reductions in aflatoxin exposure, the improved nutritional status (particularly of infants) from avoided food losses, and the climate benefits associated with reduced greenhouse gas emissions from food production. The results of the economic analysis suggest that, under the current set of assumptions, the total package of interventions is cost-beneficial, with the total programme investment generating an economic Net Present Value (ENPV) of US$ 69 million, with a benefit to cost ratio (BCR) of 8:1 and an Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) of around 56%. On this basis, there is a clear economic case for implementing the proposed programme. Additional analyses were conducted to test the sensitivity of the findings to changes in the underlying assumptions, including around the choice of discount rate, the shadow price of carbon, and the proportion of existing food production for which the quality (and safety) improves as a result of programme interventions. The results of these analyses are presented in Annex 3 and show that, at a programme level, the economic summary statistics are robust to changes in the input values.

## 201. At a country level, both the ENPV and BCR is positive across all seven countries. The highest net benefits are experienced in Burkina Faso and Ethiopia and can be explained, at least in part, by the choice of crops used to illustrate the impacts of interventions, and which have comparatively higher prices; the relatively high prevalence of aflatoxin-related illnesses in Burkina Faso.

## Application of best practices  

## 202. An evaluation of proposed physical food loss reduction solutions (FL-RS) was conducted across all seven target RE-GAIN countries to identify those with the highest potential to reduce postharvest food losses and protect the harvests against growing impacts from climate hazards. Stakeholder engagements in all seven countries provided critical nuances including advantages, disadvantages, and barriers for use,

particularly for smallholder farmers, while a detailed assessment of best practices was used to underpin the assessment.  

203. In addition to the above -mentioned prioritizations following the climate rationale, the final selection of solutions considered additional prioritization factors to ensure the success of the RE -GAIN programme and achieve lasting systemic changes in all target countries. Those factors included solution’s estimated potential in reducing food losses, estimated level of environmental pollution/ GHG emissions during the use of the solutions, current level of awareness of the farmers about the solution’s proper use and maintenance, availability of selected FL-RS in the country, and potential for their supply scalability and job creation through locally produced or assembled solutions and improving market linkages. The results of the evaluation, including major climate hazards for each country, a package of proposed solutions, together with their food loss reduction potential and existing barriers for their increased adoption is presented in Annex 2.  

204. Based off the above -described analysis , basket of options approach was selected for the RE-GAIN programme FL-RS adaptation strategy – adopting bespoke combinations of FL-RS tailored to the context of each country . The key best practice innovations for FL -RS identified include mechanical multi-crop threshers and shellers (preferably solar-powered) combined with moisture meters for monitoring the level of moisture in the target crops, and communal storage structures, with the FL -RS uses on the individual farm level, such as tarpaulins and plastic sheets for drying crops, hermetic storage technologies (hermetic bags, silos) used for storage of the crops, and storage protectants and control agents, preferably biological origin.  

205. In addition to the technical FL-RS designs described above, the overall project design considers AGRA’s experience and lessons learnt from previous projects . These lessons and best practice are described below.   

- AGRA’s last mile delivery model through training and supporting VBAs (village -based advisors) and agro-dealers in the target countries, who are largely in place to take the FL -RS solutions to farmers and their institutions.  
- AGRA has multiple and largescale projects working on food security and resilience in the target countries, which will allow this project to leverage at a scale and have a jump start.  
- AGRA has been working on multiple financial models, which could be used to move this project forward.  
- AGRA has a well fledged infrastructure and country teams in the target countries that will ensure a jump start and successfully delivery.  
- It was working with the governments of these target countries on major food security, climate and market issues, which this project will be leveraging both the political good will but also the infrastructure.  
- Farmer Education is a requisite. Partnerships with input providers and loss reducing technology manufacturers can help in training smallholder farmers to promote the sustainability of loss reducing efforts in the value chain.   
- Financial products should consider the production cycle and set requirements for repayment that are informed by the crop season.  
- Financing models should include risk sharing strategies for different market actors in the value chain  
- Partnerships and brokering agreements between financial institutions and strategically positioned market actors with the aim of unlocking barriers to accessing finance and encourage the development of tailor-made financial products that enable actors to innovate, invest for the expansion of operations and increase equipment acquisition for light to medium processing.  
- Create incentives for SMEs to invest in food loss reduction technologies.  
- Technical assistance to assist value chain actors to develop loan proposals and business plans for quick processing of loans.  
- Smallholder farmers must be convinced of the return on their investment requiring linkages to a reliable market that will provide a fair price for the product’s quality.  
- Promoting technologies for multiple crops to increase uptake of post -harvest technologies that are introduced through a program.  
- The involvement of technology distribution companies leads to faster adoption by farmers.

# E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

## E.1. Project/Programme Focus

- ☐ Reduced emissions (mitigation)
- ☒ Increased resilience (adaptation)

## E.2. GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1-2 pages)

### Assessment

#### Dimension: Current state (baseline) Potential target scenario (Description)

The uptake of food loss-reducing solutions in the target countries is low, particularly among smallholder farmers. While solutions are generally available, local supply markets are underdeveloped, restricting access and inflating prices. The combination of limited awareness, limited access, high prices, and low access to finance results in a very low adoption rate for FL-RS. As a result, farmers experience considerable levels of food loss which threatens food security. In the absence of FL-RS, there are few processes in place to mitigate the GHG emissions associated with food loss and the related land use change. Moreover, the limited demand constrains interest among manufacturers and suppliers to develop the local market, preventing the development of self-sustaining systems. This problem is exacerbated by a lack of innovative financial mechanisms that can catalyse investment in local market development.

#### How the project/programme will contribute (Description)

Low

Smallholder farmers understand the need for adopting FL-RS and have access to well-developed local supply markets that increase access and affordability of FL-RS. Strong partnerships are in place between stakeholders that facilitate the development of such markets, with manufacturers, agro-dealers, finance providers and services providers working together to enable smallholder farmers to access post-harvest technologies and services at an affordable rate. The increased demand and demonstration of effectiveness of the solutions drives further market development, enabling the scale of impact to extend within the target communities, including to other farmers as well as creating the pathway for use of FL-RS on other crops.

Under the RE-GAIN programme, AGRA will play a facilitation role in establishing the partnerships for the innovative financial models that underpin the transformation of FL-RS solutions in the target countries. Moreover, by providing smart subsidies and investing in MSME business development and market support, will catalyse the establishment of local supply chains that will bring FL-RS to smallholder farmers at affordable rates. AGRA's delivery model will demonstrate and scale innovations that enable smallholder farmers to participate in both input and output markets, and in doing so to forge commercial relationships with off-takers, processors, exporters, commercial aggregators, business development service providers and financial institutions. When combined with the demand-oriented actions of awareness, demonstration and market development under Outcome 1, the programme will result in a balance of supply, demand, and finance opportunity that will enable the scaling of interventions in the target communities beyond initial GCF investment.

#### Rating

Replicability

There are few robust investment models targeting food security through resilient post-harvest systems that reduce food losses. Some models have been piloted in individual countries or communities, but there are few systematic initiatives that demonstrate replicable models that are not reliant on continued subsidies.

Low

Mechanisms are in place that demonstrate the business case for and viability of FL-RS market development among smallholder farming communities in Africa. Robust financial models and clear demonstration of impact facilitate scaling into other communities, regions and countries across Africa.

The programme is structured such that the approaches to stimulating the FL-RS market can be replicated; not only within other communities in the target countries, but also regionally across Africa. This replication potential is initially rooted in the demonstration effect the project will have, which when combined with the convening power of AGRA across Africa will attract additional market actors to engage in similar investment schemes. To further facilitate replication, Component 3 of the project will establish an enabling environment for sustainable FL-RS markets (see below). While these enabling activities will focus on replication at the national level, AGRA will facilitate collaboration with continental and regional bodies and national governments, including African regional climate

# Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low emission climate-resilient development pathways in a country-driven manner

Development of national policies, strategies, frameworks and implementation plans aiming to address agricultural sector and climate change challenges has been a priority for many of the African governments in the recent years. Despite this, there are still considerable gaps in the policy and regulatory environment across the target countries that impede the mainstreaming of sustainable FL-RS in the agricultural sector.

This gap is underpinned by several factors. First, there is limited or no use of the scientific evidence base in the formulation of policies and strategies on post-harvest loss reduction. Second, policies generally lack coordination and harmonisation across sectors, limiting opportunities for integrated approaches to building resilience in the agriculture sector. Third, where policies or regulatory frameworks are in place, they are often constrained to agricultural input subsidies — mainly for fertilizer and seeds — with no subsidy on post-harvest technologies. This further extends to the focus of information and extension services, which are generally geared towards primary production, and less so to post-harvest processes. Similarly, existing taxation regimes do not favour or incentivise investments in storage and processing technologies for food in the smallholder system.

Low Policies and incentives are in place to facilitate the development of local markets and support to smallholder farmers for the uptake of FL-RS in each of the seven target countries. Manufacturers, importers and suppliers are incentivised to establish local markets through tax exemptions, subsidies and affordable access to finance.

The RE-GAIN programme will support the revision and operationalization of policies in support of enabling FL-RS investments, including tax exemptions/incentives, certification and standards for FL-RS quality, and promote successful FL-RS business models for scaling-up and replication. This will include collaborating with government entities to revise and update key policies which are not responding to the current and future food losses and climate challenge scenario.

RE-GAIN will engage relevant government ministries and agencies to review existing regulations and policies and proposing amendments to facilitate the development of local markets and support to farmers for the uptake of FL-RS. This will include introducing/strengthening national policies that provide financial incentives such as tax exemptions, subsidies, or reduced interest rates for FL-RS investments, making it more attractive for private and public sector investments.

# Core Indicator 6: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to technology deployment, dissemination, development or transfer and innovation

The deployment of FL-RS in the target countries is extremely limited, particularly among smallholder farmers. This includes extremely limited penetration of suppliers into rural markets. There are also few innovative financial mechanisms that can unlock local markets for FL-RS.

Low FL-RS technologies are disseminated in the local communities, both directly to farmers (for smaller, low-cost solutions) and through MSME service providers for larger solutions that can be shared among farmers. Innovative finance mechanisms are in place in the target communities linking manufacturers, suppliers, agro-dealers, financiers, service providers and farmers.

FL-RS technologies will be transferred to smallholder farmers, MSMEs, farmer cooperatives and youth groups through innovative finance models and smart subsidies. The RE-GAIN programme will facilitate partnerships for technology transfer through the innovative finance models, using the smart subsidies to derisk investments for suppliers and financiers. Beneficiaries will also be trained on the effective use of FL-RS, including the operation of service provision businesses.
