### Core indicator 7: Degree to which GCF Investments contribute to market development/transformation at the sectoral, local, or national level  

Local markets for FL-RS are underdeveloped, particularly among smallholder farming communities. Limited awareness and financial capacity of farmers constrains demand for FL-RS, which in turn constrains interest among manufacturers and suppliers to develop the local market. This problem is exacerbated by a lack of innovative financial mechanisms that can catalyse investment in local market development.  

low The local market is unlocked, encouraging vendors of FL-RS equipment to invest in distribution channels, marketing, maintenance capacity and other needed infrastructure to make the equipment available and serviceable in a sustainable manner. This shift will lead to reduced prices and increased affordability, creating a self-sustaining system that will enable the phased out of programme subsidies.   

RE-GAIN will build the capacity of local MSMEs to provide access to FL-RS, either through improving local supply or offering services to farmers. MSMEs and youth groups will be trained on technical topics related to FL-RS service provision, including:  
i) proper service management and process optimization;  
ii) the safe, effective, efficient and sustainable operation of the equipment;  
iii) compliance with international and local quality standards for post-harvest services; and  
iv) the management of the fee-for-service model.  

This will be complemented by the development of innovative financial mechanisms that will catalyse investments in FL-RS through partnerships and smart subsidies.

### Core indicator 8: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards  

While farmers are distinctly aware of the impacts of food loss on food security, many do not fully understand the specific causes of food loss, or the potential solutions available to reduce food loss. This includes limited understanding of basic harvesting practices that can reduce risk of food loss, or the related post-harvest practices and tool available. Moreover, many farmers are not fully aware of the impacts that CC has on food loss and the consequent need to change post-harvest practices.  

low Farmers are aware of the need and options available for FL-RS, thereby enhancing demand for, and adoption of, FL-RS in the target communities. This adoption of FL-RS will ultimately reduce food losses and result in the core benefits of the programme for smallholder farmers – namely improved food security, increased resilience to climate shocks, and reduced GHG emissions.  

RE-GAIN will provide direct support to smallholder farmers to not only know what FL-RS options are available, but also to understand the need for adopting such options – particularly when considering the impacts of a changing climate – as well as how to optimise the use of FL-RS. Moreover, RE-GAIN programme will identify and train local MSMEs and cooperatives to develop FL-RS service provision operations in the target communities.

# Activity 1.1.1: Conduct an awareness-raising campaign targeting smallholder farming communities focused on the impacts of climate change on post-harvest food losses and the availability of locally-appropriate and climate-resilient FL-RS

An awareness campaign will be conducted in each of the target communities across the seven countries, using local institutions, chiefdoms and community networks to increase the understanding of postharvest food losses among farmers, local stakeholders in the agriculture value chain, and the public, thereby establishing a foundation for increasing demand for FL-RS.

The nature of the campaign will be tailored to agricultural context each country, accounting for the target crops, the availability of local information dissemination systems (including mass media such as radio, television, digital platforms and social media, as well as local extension services), cultural considerations and local languages. The campaign will collaborate with local governments and farmer organisations, build on existing outreach systems to maximise reach and participation. AGRA will closely monitor the awareness campaigns, assessing impacts and adapting outreach strategies as needed.

- Develop an awareness-raising campaign for each target country.
- Deliver awareness campaigns in target communities in each of the seven countries.
- 7 Awareness raising campaigns developed and delivered.

# Activity 1.1.2: Train smallholder farmers on the use of improved climate-resilient FL-RS

Training will be provided to educate smallholder farmers on improved crop management and storage techniques to reduce food losses and to maintain quality of produce, increase farmers' income by reducing losses and improving marketability, and improve supply of financial services and FL-RS to smallholders and other value chain actors. AGRA will procure the services of suitable local entities (for example, NGOs) to facilitate training in target communities. Training will then be provided through various channels — such as onsite demonstration of best practices, agricultural extension services, lead farmers and Village-Based Advisors (VBAs), farmer cooperatives, industry associations, and digital platforms — depending on the local context and the existing networks of the local service provider.

The nature and content of the training will be tailored to each group. Training will include baseline topics, including an introduction to the RE-GAIN programme and the core concepts of food security, post-harvest food losses and the impact of climate change – building on and leveraging material from the awareness campaign (Activity 1.1.1). In addition to this baseline, technical training will be provided on specific subject matter tailored to the local context of each community.

- Develop training material for target crops in each country.
- Engage local partners (including suppliers and agro-dealers) to identify and agree on training delivery models for each community.
- Deliver training to target communities.
- Integrate the use of FL-RS into extension services curricula at the local level.
- Establish demonstration/learning sites in each of the project target areas.
- 14 Training methodologies (two crops in each of the seven countries).
- Training delivered in 7 countries.
- Two demos per year across each of the priority regions per country.

# Activity 1.1.3: Train agricultural traders and processors on complementary services for FL-RS

Building on the training of farmers, the programme will provide additional training to agricultural traders and processors on complementary services that support and maximise the effectiveness of FL-RS. This training will include the same baseline concepts, including an introduction to the RE-GAIN programme and the core concepts of food security, post-harvest food losses and the impact of climate change. Beyond that, the training will include on best-bet approaches to introduce FL-RS to smallholder farmers and the types of initial support required to enhance demand, tailored to the processing and trade of produce.

- Develop training material for complementary services in each target country.
- Deliver training to agricultural traders and processors.
- Training delivered in 7 countries.

# Activity 1.1.4: Implement a training of trainers programme targeting extension workers and agro-dealers

In addition to the direct training of farmers under the programme, RE-GAIN will facilitate broader training opportunities through a training of trainers (ToT) approach, particularly for VBAs. Using the same service providers procured for Activity 1.1.2, key stakeholders who engage regularly with farming communities — such as extension officers, VBAs and agro-dealers — will be trained as trainers, focusing on the subject matter outlined for Activity 1.1.2. In addition to ensuring that trainers have a firm understanding of the training content, they will be trained on the:

- Develop training material.
- Identify potential trainers in local organisations and VBAs.
- Host ToT workshops in each target country.
- 7 ToT workshops.

# Activity 1.1.5: Conduct scoping missions to identify and engage manufacturers of FL-RS equipment

Develop an inventory of FL-RS equipment manufacturers and agro-dealers that can be engaged in developing local markets and enhancing the immediate availability of affordable FL-RS (establishing the basis for delivery models under Component 2). By mapping the market and building network of local manufacturers and importers/agro-dealers, this activity will enable a better understanding of current suppliers of FL-RS, and their production scalability potential. To ensure the reach of FL-RS to remote and rural areas, the programme will facilitate engagement and increased collaboration between solution manufacturers and local agro-dealers in those target areas, ensuring availability of solutions, and their accessibility by local farmers.

The scoping missions will include assessments of the current market in each target region to better understand the market needs and opportunities. This will include identifying opportunities for exchanges between African manufacturers and companies based in other developing nations to help introduce new technologies into the African market. Through these scoping missions, AGRA will facilitate knowledge and technology sharing and transfer between suppliers, farmers, and service providers.

- Host scoping mission in each target community and surrounding areas to identify equipment manufacturers and agro-dealers.
- Develop a stakeholder map, linking stakeholders across various stages of the value chain.
- 7 Stakeholder maps, one for each target country.

# Activity 1.1.6: Conduct demonstrations of locally-appropriate FL-RS

The programme will facilitate active demonstrations of short-listed FL-RS to target communities. This activity will build on the relationships developed under Activity 1.1.5, partnering with vendors for the implementation of product demonstrations, leveraging the incentives for vendors to increase the sales of their solutions through the programme-supported outreach. Where possible, demonstrations will be done in conjunction with the training under Activity 1.1.2 to improve efficiencies and ensure full connectivity between the theoretical training and practical demonstrations.

- Engage project partners in target countries to develop demonstration strategies.
- Conduct demonstrations of non-physical solutions
- Conduct demonstrations of physical solutions
- Demonstrations of at least one physical and one non-physical intervention in each target community per year. 410 total demonstrations over 5 years

# Activity 1.1.7: Provide technical assistance to food processors to facilitate a shift to whole-grain flour production

In addition to the FL-RS approaches that will be promoted above, the programme will provide technical assistance to promote the transition from refining grains to wholegrain flour production. As depicted in Figure 4, wholegrain production can produce almost 50% more bread from the same quantity of unprocessed wheat, helping reduce the “hidden food loss” associated with over processing. The intervention will target food producers – millers in particular – providing technical assistance along several themes, including: i) developing MSME capacity; ii) raising awareness of market opportunities for wholegrains; iii) support for technology transfers; and iv) facilitating access to finance.

The activity will form part of a broader initiative that is promoting wholegrain food for use in school meal programmes, which the RE-GAIN programme will leverage and complement.

- Develop awareness raising material on wholegrain production
- Host capacity development workshops in target communities.
- Connect processors with technology providers for improved processing equipment suited to wholegrain production
- At least one capacity development workshop in each target community.

The process will start with an assessment of the existing market in each target community, drawing on and expanding the initial market assessment conducted

- At least one technical workshop per community

### Activity 2.1.1: Train new FL-RS service providers (MSMEs, cooperatives, incl. women- and youth-led initiatives)  

The RE-GAIN programme will identify and train local MSMEs and cooperatives to develop FL-RS service provision operations in the target communities. This training will prioritise women- and youth-led initiatives to maximise the social benefits and build resilience among these vulnerable groups. The programme will adopt a rigorous process of scouting and in-depth assessment using standardized tools of the potential service providers and food processors complemented by intensive vendor support to make sure operations and maintenance skills for the equipment are transferred.  

The identification of suitable entities will be supported by government (decentralised and local) authorities as well as through digital platforms, social media, industry players where potential service providers are connected or enrolled, and via the scoping exercises under Activity 1.2.1.  

Once service providers are identified and enrolled in the programme, a rigorous assessment of their capacity and skills will be conducted, before organising intensive “boot camps” to train these entities on the FL-RS service provision. These training camps will cover several technical topics related to FL-RS service provision, including: i) proper service management and process optimization; ii) the safe, effective, efficient and sustainable operation of the equipment; iii) compliance with international and local quality standards for post-harvest services; and iv) the management of the fee-for-service model.  

In addition to the technical training on FL-RS service provision, entities will be supported to develop their business into a sustainable entity. This will include training on basic business practices (such as financial literacy, cash flow management, record keeping, and risk management).  

- Host scouting missions to identify local MSMEs and cooperatives in each target community  
- Develop capacity development training material  
- Engage local vendors to support in training initiatives  
- Host training bootcamps in target community.  
- Host exchange visits between nearby communities  
- Shortlist of suitable MSMEs and Cooperatives  
- Agreements with local vendors signed to facilitate training  
- At least one training bootcamp in each target community  
- At least one exchange visit per community.

To complement the development of MSME service providers, the RE-GAIN programme will support business development among manufacturers, importers and distributors of FL-RS to boost local markets. This will include providing market intelligence to increase marketing, distribution and after-sales services to develop the supply chain for FL-RS products. # Activity 2.1.2: Provide business development support and market intelligence

To complement the development of MSME service providers, the RE-GAIN programme will support business development among manufacturers, importers and distributors of FL-RS to boost local markets. This will include providing market intelligence to increase marketing, distribution and after-sales services to develop the supply chain for FL-RS products. Entities will also be supported to develop partnerships with other actors in the supply chain, as well as to engage with innovative de-risking schemes piloted under Output 2.2.

# Activity 2.1.3: Capacity and market development for all supply-side market actors

In addition to the targeted support for FL-RS suppliers and service providers, RE-GAIN will provide general capacity development support to actors across the FL-RS supply chain to further develop the local supply market for FL-RS. The nature of this support will vary depending on the context of each target country and the actors involved but could include the convening of actors and development of partnerships, establishment of cooperatives to optimise the procurement and shared use of FL-RS among farmers, market mapping and dissemination of market intelligence reports, and building network of local manufacturers and importers/agro-dealers.

Implementation of the supply-side market development support will include linkages with the agri-value chains market development initiatives under 1.2.1, sharing resources to optimise cost efficiency and co-develop both sides of the market.

# Activity 2.2.1. Facilitate the development and deployment of catalytic procurement models for FL-RS focussing on women and youth collectives and enterprises as key beneficiaries

Implement three financial models, with varying levels of complexity and potential impact. Models are split into two distinct categories: interventions at a smallholder level; and interventions at a youth group/co-operative level. An overview of each of the three financial models is provided in Annex 2: Feasibility Study. In all instances, the models draw on the outputs of the scoping exercises under Activities 1.1.5 and 1.2.1 to identify the potential actors and tailor the models to the local context. The models leverage partnerships between these actors to share risk and facilitate and incentivise the development of robust markets. AGRA will collaborate with government entities to revise and update key policies which are not responding to the current and future food losses and climate challenge scenario. This will include engaging relevant government ministries and agencies to review existing regulations and policies and proposing amendments to facilitate the development of local markets and support to farmers for the uptake of FL-RS. This will include introducing/strengthening national policies that provide financial incentives such as tax exemptions, subsidies, or reduced interest rates for FL-RS investments, making it more attractive for private and public sector investments.

Evidence generated and lessons learned in FL-RS from target communities and county will be to develop capacity, influence policy and promote best practices at a scale.

AGRA will also actively participate in policy advocacy initiatives, working with stakeholders to ensure that proposed changes support the long-term success of FL-RS initiatives. In addition to support for policy revision, AGRA will support local entities in implementing existing policies and regulations that have potential to support the uptake of FL-RS, but are currently not fully enforced or supported. These actions will result in a supportive policy environment that enables the successful implementation of the RE-GAIN programme and widespread adoption of FL-RS solutions.

# E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

206. The M&E structure for RE-GAIN has been developed to be in-line with AGRA’s existing M&E structure, to ensure a cohesive approach, as well as with GCF’s policy requirements such as Monitoring and Accountability Framework and Integrated Results Management Framework. It has also been designed to target the GCF-supported project Outcome and Output key performance indicators (KPIs; listed above) and the broader programme performance, respectively. A baseline survey will be conducted during the inception phase of the programme, and AGRA will update the performance indicators where applicable based on the results alongside the baseline results as part of the inception report. The performance monitoring will be conducted on an ongoing basis by a dedicated M&E officer with close oversight from the AGRA M&E Team and Board. The GCF-supported project KPIs will be monitored during the implementation period as a subset of the broader performance monitoring of the programme over the long-term. The responsibilities of the M&E officer will include:

- **Data collection and performance measurement**:
  - The M&E officer will work closely with each project intermediary, to aggregate data on the non-financial fund metrics obtained directly from the intermediaries. This will include data to support the development of site-specific baselines; Economic activity; Market share; Production; Sales volume and value. Additional data will be periodically and systematically aggregated by intermediaries during implementation relating to production, sales volumes and value, human resources, among others.
  - Periodic site visits will be conducted to assess and verify the results presented by the intermediaries.
  - When appropriate and deemed necessary, external consultants may support the data collection and measurement process.

- **Performance assessment and recommending corrective actions**: Based on the predefined output and outcome targets and metrics, corrective actions may be defined by engaging with the intermediary.

- **Reporting**: Performance against the Outcome and Output KPIs are reported to the GCF on an annual basis.

207. The monitoring and reporting on the Outcome and Output KPIs will be integrated into the broader performance monitoring system for AGRA. AGRA has a comprehensive MRV system that extends from grantees, to country, region, corporate level review and verification processes supported by an online Management Information System (MIS). AGRA also has standard Data Quality Assurance (DQA) mechanism to ensure quality performance data are collected and reported. AGRA will integrate RE-GAIN into the existing MRV system. The M&E system for RE-GAIN will be validated and finalised by the AGRA Board, in collaboration with the AGRA M&E Team, during the programme’s inception and will continue to be updated as the programme progresses. The AGRA Board and M&E Team will work with the M&E officer to evaluate the interplay between project indicators and results to assess the effectiveness of the M&E system and amend it where appropriate.

## Evaluation

208. The performance will be independently evaluated against the Outcome and Output KPIs at the mid-term and final stages of the project implementation period, specifically in years 3 and 5, as per GCF evaluation policy. These evaluations will be conducted by an independent evaluation specialist, under the oversight of AGRA.

## Financial Reporting

209. AGA will track the program’s finances and will produce semi-annual reports that summarizes material developments of the se. AGRA will develop valuation processes and procedures for the underlying portfolio investments and ensure these are done in accordance with Accounting Standards Codification (ACS) 820-10 on a semi-annual basis on June 30th and December 31st.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigation measures

### Selected Risk Factor 1

- **Category:** Technical and operational  
- **Probability:** Low  
- **Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Lack of programme ownership by beneficiaries and target governments leads to weak uptake and/or sustainability of FL-RS.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
Detailed stakeholder engagement has been conducted throughout the programme design stage, and will continue throughout implementation to ensure that interventions are fully owned by and meet the individual needs of the target countries. This consultative process will ensure that the probability of this risk remains low.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

- **Category:** Technical and operational  
- **Probability:** Medium  
- **Impact:** High  

#### Description
Financial crisis caused by unstable macro-economic variables impacts the market development and increases the risk profile of financial mechanisms.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The approach to designing the financial models was built on a foundation of a detailed analysis of local markets and economic fundamentals, ensuring that the mechanisms are robust to withstand fluctuations in the macro-economic situation. Multiple derisking approaches have been integrated into the market development models, which will provide a buffer to reduce the potential impact of economic fluctuations on the effectiveness of the programme.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

- **Category:** Governance  
- **Probability:** Medium  
- **Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Political instability in some target countries leads to disruptions to the implementation of project interventions, or ineffectiveness of enabling environment actions.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
During times of political instability, AGRA will increase engagement with national stakeholders to ensure investments and programmes are not disrupted, reducing the potential impact of political instability on programme outcomes.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

- **Category:** Technical and operational  
- **Probability:** Medium  
- **Impact:** High  

#### Description
Lack of willingness from end users to adopt the FL-RS reduces market transformation potential and long-term impacts of the project.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The RE-GAIN programme will engage in extensive awareness campaigns and capacity development to ensure that farmers fully understand the threats posed by climate change on post-harvest loss, the benefits of FL-RS for reducing losses, and the financial viability of FL-RS solutions. Combined with supply-side investments that increase availability and affordability of FL-RS, these actions will reduce the probability of this risk to low.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

- **Category:** Credit  
- **Probability:** Medium  
- **Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Credit default and end beneficiaries’ failure to repay the loans and meet their contractual obligations may reduce the effectiveness of financial models and erode trust in the system.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The credits are administered by financial institutions with excellent knowledge of their customers and the market within which they operate. The financial models have also been designed with multiple levels of derisking, including smart subsidies to reduce burden on end beneficiaries (which will increase likelihood of being able to pay), as well as broad market development in agriculture value chains and capacity development of farmers and MSMES. Assessments will also be conducted by AGRA to ensure alignment of credit providers with policies and procedures on compliance and AML.

### Selected Risk Factor 6

- **Category:** Governance  
- **Probability:** Low  
- **Impact:** Medium  

#### Description
Regulatory constraints impede the implementation of the project interventions in individual countries.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
The interventions have been designed to account for legal frameworks and internal policies to ensure that the activities align with regulations in each country, ensuring the project can be implemented within the programme timeline.

# G. GCF Policies and Standards  
## G.1. GCF Policies and social risk assessment  

### Risk Categorisation  

210. The programme is assigned a Category B risk rating as per the GCF Revised Environmental and Social (E&S) Policy definition. The rationale for the risk rating is as follows:  

- The Programme will target established smallholder farms and MSMEs and as such will not be associated with any greenfield development or land acquisition;  
- The Programme is designed to facilitate the adoption of FL-RS solutions by smallholder farmers and MSMEs through existing supply chains in the countries. As such, the Programme will not directly be involved in the manufacturing, transportation and/or installation of the FL-RS solutions;  
- The E&S risks associated with the Programme are mainly associated with the regions targeted by the Programme in each in-scope country, the contextual circumstances of the existing smallholder farms targeted to participate in the interventions and the in-country partner(s) chosen to facilitate the interventions; and  
- The risk assessment conducted on the Programme highlighted limited adverse E &S risks and impacts that are few (see Programme E &S Risk Assessment ) and can be readily addressed through actionable mitigation measures (see Programme EandS Risk Management Framework).  

### Programme E&S Risk Assessment  

211. In order to meet the GCF EandS due diligence requirements expected on a Category B type project, AGRA conducted two desktop assessments, namely a country and region contextual E andS risk analysis for each in-scope country and focus crops and an intervention level E andS risk assessment taking into account the envisaged Programme activities. The key EandS risks stemming from these assessments are summarised as follows:  

#### Overall Programme E&S Risks  

212. i) AGRA procures the services of local NGOs and/or private sector actors to support the building of the intervention ecosystem (i.e., to build the connections between manufacturers, agro-dealers, and the end recipients such as smallholder farmers and MSMEs). Reputational risks may exist associated with the Programme partners chosen due to poor E&S performance and/or reputation.  

213. ii) AGRA will preferentially identify and partner with locally available manufactures of FL-RS technologies for inclusion into the Programme. Reputational risks associated with manufactures due to E andS related risk factors such as:  

- Sub-standard labour and working conditions including potential for poor occupational health and safety practices;  
- Limited pollution control measures and waste management; and/or  
- Road transport health and safety risks associated with transporters (internal/external) of FL-RS technologies to agro-dealers.  

214. iii) AGRA will partner with locally available financial institutions to provide financing at preferential rates to either manufacturers or smallholder farmers / MSMEs. Reputational risks associated with financial institutions due to poor and/or unethical lending processes (especially those related to microfinance institutions) which may lead to smallholder farmer indebtedness.  

#### Contextual E&S Risks  

215. i) Conflict areas were identified in certain regions in Burkina Faso and Ethiopia where the Programme is intended to be implemented may lead to challenges in meeting Programme objectives including potential health, safety and security risks to Programme personnel.

## 216. ii) Depending on the country, maturity and oversight of labour regulations, reputational risks associated with labour and working conditions may exist on the target smallholder farms and/or MSMEs. These may include the presence of child and/or forced labour practices on these farms, poor health and safety practices, non-compliance’s with labour laws and non alignment with the requirements of international standards such as those set out in the ILO and IFC PS 2.

## 217. iii) AGRA conducted a desktop Indigenous Peoples (IPs) screening and vulnerability assessment of the Programme. Of the seven countries assessed, no recognized IPs were identified in Zambia and Malawi. However, potential IPs were identified in specific regions within Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Tanzania, and Uganda. There are no circumstances in which the Programme will result in any form of displacement of IPs, affect access to land or natural resources, nor will the Programme interventions be likely to include activities that significantly alter the physical or social context of the local areas in which the interventions will be implemented. However, the identification of the IPs underscores the need for careful and proactive consideration of any risk that might arise during program implementation and requires tailored approaches to address the unique circumstances and challenges of indigenous communities in the identified areas. In this regard, the Programme will proactively work with IPs and their representatives to ensure that the programme (i) does not cause any significant harm to their way of life and livelihoods and (ii) enhances or complements their existing livelihoods. Further, using appropriately translated ESS and programme information to local languages, IPs will also be continuously engaged before and during programme implementation, with feedback and suggestions from leaders and representatives used as proactive measures to ensure a successful implementation of the programme.

## Intervention Level E&S Risks  

## 218. i) The Programme includes the introduction and promotion of novel FL-RS technologies. The innovative technology introduced to small holder farmers and/or MSMEs may lead to unintended health, safety and/or environmental risks such as injury or increased pollution generation as a consequence of recipients not being adequately trained on their correct use and/or waste disposal.

## 219. ii) AGRA procures NGOs to create awareness and demonstrate the FL-RS in collaboration with manufacturer and agro-dealers. These trainings are conducted at selected smallholder farms. NGO staff may not have adequate technical competencies to provide training on unintended health, safety and/or environmental risks associated with the FL-RS technologies. NGOs may deliver training that is not culturally suited to the target audience and may be biased from an inclusion and gender perspective.

## Programme E&S Risk Management Framework  

## 220. AGRA has in place a centralised Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) that will be applicable to all interventions initiated in AGRA’s focus countries. The ESMS is designed to ensure that potential negative E &S impacts of AGRA interventions are minimized, while positive effects on communities and the environment are enhanced. It follows the guidance of IFC Performance Standard 1 and includes an E&S policy and procedures, identification of risks and impacts, management program, organisational capacity and competency, emergency preparedness and response, stakeholder engagement, grievance management, monitoring, review and reporting.

## 221. In addition, AGRA has developed a Programme E&S Action Plan (ESAP), which provides a framework for E&S action across the entire RE-GAIN Programme. Once the specific communities and beneficiaries have been selected, the Programme ESAP will be adapted where appropriate to be specific to the E &S risks and impacts associated with the individual country, region, crop and combination of FL-RS applicable to the specific intervention envisaged. The Programme ESAP sets out mitigation measures for each of the risks identified in the E&S risk assessments conducted.

## 222. Full details on Programme E &S risk identification and mitigation are provided in Annex 6 of the Funding Proposal.

# G.2. Gender assessment and action plan

223. To ensure that the RE-GAIN Programme is designed, financed, implemented and monitored in a gender-responsive and socially inclusive manner, a comprehensive Gender Assessment and Gender and Inclusion Action Plan has been developed (Annex 8). These outputs comply with the GCF’s Gender Policy, as well as AGRA’s own Gender and Inclusion Strategy, ESMS Manual and Safeguarding of Vulnerable Persons and Youth Policy.

224. RE-GAIN’s Gender Assessment considers the various factors (including those exacerbated by climate change) that influence gender inequality in each of the seven target countries’ agriculture sectors and identifies opportunities for women, men and youth to contribute to inclusive resilience building within post-harvest value chains. These opportunities are captured in a Programme-wide Gender and Inclusion Action Plan, which defines activities, indicators and targets specific to women and youth in their capacities as smallholders, cooperative networks, MSMEs and FL-RS vendors.

225. The Gender Assessment combines extensive literature review, carried out on a country-by-country basis on the key contextual factors that enable and/or impede gender equality progress at a national-level, with primary findings drawn from 28 national and local stakeholder engagements spread evenly across the seven countries.

226. Using these information sources to examine the disparities (and underlying drivers) between women, men and youth within the context of agriculture and climate change in each of RE-GAIN’s countries, several clear, common and consistent themes on gender and inclusion emerge:

Improved, inclusive access to finance for women and youth is a fundamental enabler for increased and more equitable participation in FL-RS. The upfront cost of many solutions is a primary barrier to adoption by women and youth. It is therefore critical that inclusive and diverse finance options and advisory services are made available to these groups.

Inclusive and accessible training and information on FL-RS for women and youth is necessary to empower and capacitate them as farmers and business owners. This includes both technical and financial advisory support on: FL-RS tools, equipment and structures; the benefits of changing current practices or preferences; and on financial management and marketing – including options available, budgeting, saving, repayment processes, pricing, market quality standards and control etc. Such training must be designed in a gender sensitive and highly context specific manner. This must be coupled with sensitization of male community members, to overcome persistent disparities between women and men’s access to financial, technical, infrastructural and capacity building resources.

The deliberate and targeted appointment of extension service delivery partners is fundamental to achieving many of these recommendations, meaning the selection of RE-GAIN’s implementation partners must incorporate a specific technical competency on demonstrated experience of working with women, youth and other marginalised groups.

Adopting a dual top-down bottom-up approach to amplify women and youth’s voices on FL-RS is needed to create sustainable shifts in the way women and youth engage in, and benefit from, RE-GAIN’s support. This means that on-the-ground, community centred efforts must be coupled with more inclusive, enforceable FL-RS policies.

These recommendations, together with RE-GAIN’s overarching goal, outcomes and outputs, were used to develop the Gender and Inclusion Action Plan which includes both qualitative and quantitative targets over the five-year period. Implementing the measures set out in this Plan will result in 1,284,321 women (~50% of the total beneficiaries) benefitting from RE-GAIN’s support.

A dedicated RE-GAIN Gender Expert will oversee the delivery and M&E of the Gender and Inclusion Action Plan, and ensure continued compliance with related processes, including application of the grievance mechanism, safeguarding protocols and ESMS. The cost of this expert has been budgeted for the full five years, along with related expenses required to deliver all activities.

## Financial Monitoring Systems

228. AGRA transitioned from Navision to an integrated system - Oracle system. It employs robust financial monitoring systems to oversee the management and utilization of funds. These systems are designed to ensure that funds are used effectively and in line with AGRA’s strategic goals. Key components include:

- **Budget Monitoring**: AGRA uses a budgetary control system that monitors expenditures against approved budgets. This allows for real-time tracking of financial performance and ensures that any variances are identified and addressed promptly.
  
- **Financial Reporting**: AGRA’s financial monitoring includes regular financial reporting. These reports provide detailed insights into the financial health of the organization and are used by management to make informed decisions. The reports are typically produced on a quarterly basis and include budget variance analysis, fund utilization reports, and financial forecasts.

- **Grant Management System (AMIS)**: For managing funds disbursed through grants, AGRA uses a dedicated grant management system - AMIS. This system tracks grant disbursements, monitors compliance with grant terms, and ensures that grantees are using the funds as intended.

## Financial Accounting

229. AGRA’s financial accounting practices are designed to ensure accuracy, consistency, and compliance with international accounting standards. The key elements include:

- **Accrual and Modified Cash Accounting**: AGRA uses both accrual and modified cash accounting system, which records financial transactions when they are incurred and in some cases its only when cash is exchanged as this is a requirement for some of our donors. This method provides a more accurate picture of the organization’s financial position.

- **Internal Controls**: AGRA has a set of internal controls in place to prevent and detect errors or fraud. These controls include segregation of duties, authorization of transactions, and reconciliation of accounts.

- **Financial Software**: AGRA uses Oracle financial management software to record and manage its accounting activities. This software integrates various aspects of financial management, including accounts payable, accounts receivable, general ledger, and fixed assets management.

## Auditing

230. AGRA places significant emphasis on auditing to ensure the integrity of its financial management. The auditing process includes:

- **Internal Audits**: AGRA conducts regular internal audits to assess the effectiveness of its internal controls, compliance with policies and procedures, and the accuracy of financial records. The internal audit function reports directly to the Audit Committee of the Board to maintain independence.

- **External Audits**: In addition to internal audits, AGRA is subject to annual external audits conducted by independent audit firms. These audits assess the accuracy of AGRA’s financial statements, compliance with relevant regulations, and the effectiveness of its financial management systems. The external audit reports are shared with stakeholders, including donors and board members, to ensure transparency.

- **Audit Committee**: AGRA has an Audit Committee that oversees both internal and external audit processes. The committee is responsible for reviewing audit findings, ensuring that management takes corrective actions, and making recommendations to the Board on matters related to financial governance.

# 4. Disbursement Structure and Methods

231. AGRA’s disbursement structure is designed to ensure that funds are distributed efficiently and in accordance with donor agreements and organizational objectives. The key elements include:

- **Procurement**: AGRA provides support through the procurement of various partner organizations/implementing agencies, including governments, research institutions, and NGOs. AGRA follows stringent procurement processes to ensure value for money. All procurement activities are conducted in accordance with AGRA’s procurement policy, which includes competitive bidding, vendor evaluation, and contract management. Project payments are typically made in tranches, contingent upon the meeting of specific deliverables and reporting requirements. This ensures that funds are used effectively and for their intended purposes.

- **Direct Payments**: For certain projects and operational costs, AGRA makes direct payments to vendors or service providers. These payments are processed through AGRA’s financial management system, ensuring proper documentation and authorization.

- **Banking and Treasury**: AGRA’s treasury management includes maintaining relationships with banking partners, managing cash flows, and ensuring that funds are available to meet the organization’s financial obligations. AGRA uses secure and reliable banking systems to process disbursements and manage its funds.

## AGRA Procurement Process Overview

232. AGRA has a fully resourced procurement unit with clear policies and procedures that meet best practices. The procurement policy and procedures manual are approved by the board and clearly outline the detailed processes to be followed and the approval matrix.

233. As a policy requirement, we follow competitive processes in sourcing goods and services for most of our needs. The procurement process is fully automated starting from budget approval all the way through to contracting.

### Key steps in the procurement process

- **Planning and Budgeting** - Initiated by user units through annual budgeting and procurement planning.
- **Vendor Management** - Handled by the procurement unit and includes vendor due diligence, prequalification, registration and performance management.
- **Requisitioning** - User units initiate requests through AGRA’s automated SCM management system.
- **Sourcing** - Procurement initiates sourcing through AGRA’s automated SCM management system. This includes evaluation of proposals/quotations by the Technical Evaluation Panels, negotiations and review of proposed awards by the Procurement Committee, Executive Committee and the Board as per the approval matrix.
- **Contracting** - This involves drafting of contracts, sharing with proposed consultants/vendors and signing of the agreements. This is also done through AGRA’s automated SCM management system.
- **Contract management** - This includes management of all contracts by the requesting units, vendor payments, and performance management.

## Escrow Accounts

234. During the implementation of the financial models under Output 2.2, escrow accounts will be used to hold funds until specific conditions have been met. In these instances, AGRA will open an account either at the bank of the supplier, at the partner financial institution, or at its own bank (depending on the specific agreement reached with the supplier and FI). This will be a USD denominated deposit account to which AGRA is a signatory. Depending on the agreements reached, the other parties (suppliers, financiers) will be included as co-signers as needed. Funds can only be released if the trigger in the agreement has materialized.

# Governance and Oversight

235. The roles and responsibilities are clearly outlined in the procurement policy and procedures manual. The Board is responsible for reviewing and approving the policies. The President appoints the Procurement Committee comprising of nine members including the Chair, Vice chair, Secretary and Members who are responsible for providing oversight and ensuring that the procurement system is fair, transparent, competitive, and cost-effective. Internal Audit Function provides independent review and assurance on risk management, controls, and governance processes.

# Procurement Risk Assessment and Management

236. AGRA has an elaborate Risk Management Framework that is overseen by the Enterprise Risk Management Unit. AGRA defines procurement risk as the possibility that an event may impact its ability to achieve its objectives, either adversely or favorably. To address this, AGRA's risk management process involves identifying, assessing, measuring, and managing procurement risks to ensure they remain within acceptable tolerance levels. This proactive approach aims to enhance transparency, accountability, and stakeholder confidence in the procurement process. The procurement unit and other units have risk registers that are updated on quarterly basis.

# Other policies

237. AGRA has several other policies that complement the procurement policy. These include Human Resources Policy, Code of Conduct Policy, The Whistle-blower Policy, Anti-Fraud Policy, and Anti-Money Laundering and Counter Terrorism Financing policy. To gain further insight into our procurement procedures, please refer to our procurement policy document embedded below.

# G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

☒ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

• full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy, and

• redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

- ☒ Annex 4 Detailed budget plan (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 5 Implementation timetable including key project/programme milestones (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 6 EandS document corresponding to the EandS category (A, B or C; or I1, I2 or I3): (ESS disclosure form provided)
  - ☐ Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) or
  - ☐ Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) or
  - ☒ Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS)
  - ☐ Others (Please specify – e.g. Resettlement Action Plan, Resettlement Policy Framework, Indigenous People’s Plan, Land Acquisition Plan, Etc.)
- ☒ Annex 7 Summary of consultations and stakeholder engagement plan
- ☒ Annex 8 Gender assessment and project/programme-level action plan (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 9 LEGAL DUE DILIGENCE (REGULATION, TAXATION AND INSURANCE)
- ☒ Annex 10 Procurement plan (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 11 Monitoring and evaluation plan (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 12 AE fee request (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 13 Co-financing commitment letter, if applicable (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 14 Term sheet including a detailed disbursement schedule and, if applicable, repayment schedule

## H.2. Other annexes as applicable

- ☐ Annex 15 Evidence of internal approval (template provided)
- ☒ Annex 16 Map(s) indicating the location of proposed interventions
- ☒ Annex 17 Multi-country project/programme information (template provided)
- ☐ Annex 18 Appraisal, due diligence or evaluation report for proposals based on up-scaling or replicating a pilot project
- ☐ Annex 19 Procedures for controlling procurement by third parties or executing entities undertaking projects financed by the entity
- ☒ Annex 20 First level AML/CFT (KYC) assessment
- ☐ Annex 21 Operations manual (Operations and maintenance)
- ☒ Annex 22 Assessment of GHG emission reductions and their monitoring and reporting (for mitigation and cross-cutting projects)

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
Page 108  

## Section 1. Secretariat’s assessment of the applicant under the project-specific assessment approach  

1. The document “PSAA accreditation: application and review checklist” submitted by the applicant, AGRA, has been assessed against the GCF accreditation standards. The result of the entity capacity assessment and the Secretariat’s endorsement and recommendations for accreditation under the GCF project-specific assessment approach (PSAA) are as follows:  

- **Overall capacity**: High  
  - The entity has a well-developed financial management system;  
  - A well-functioning control framework, including risk management;  
  - And robust systems and policies in place to prevent money laundering and the financing of terrorism and other prohibited practices, such as fraud and corruption.  
  - The entity’s system has sufficient scope to manage environmental and social and gender risks and impacts with a low likelihood of negative impact on the entity’s ability to undertake the proposed project as designed.  

- **Fiduciary functions**: Basic fiduciary standards  
  - Specialized fiduciary standard for project management  
  - Maximum environmental and social risk category  
  - Limited adverse risks (category B)  
  - Conditions: N/A  

2. The Secretariat has augmented its capacity by using external experts in undertaking the entity capacity assessment review.1 A summary is presented below.  

### I. Entity capacity assessment summary  

#### 1.1 Introduction and institutional assessment  

##### 1.1.1. Basic information about the project and the entity  

3. AGRA (formerly the Alliance for a Green Revolution in Africa), established in 2006 by the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and the Rockefeller Foundation, is a pan-African institution headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya.  
AGRA’s vision is to contribute to a food system-inspired inclusive agricultural transformation across Africa, focusing on reducing hunger, improving nutrition and adapting to climate change.  
AGRA’s strategic objectives include empowering and building the resilience of smallholder farmers and supporting the development of inclusive markets and finance to strengthen agricultural systems, as well as strengthening the capability of African countries to sustain agricultural transformation.  
AGRA collaborates with both public

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 109  

### 4. In 2023 AGRA’s total revenues were USD 128.3 million, up from USD 80.3 million in 2022. AGRA is applying for a USD 75.6 million grant from GCF for a five-year programme, RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa (hereinafter referred to as the RE-GAIN programme), which falls into the category of medium projects (up to USD 250 million). The annual volume of the proposed programme is less than 15 per cent of the average realized annual revenues in 2022 and 2023. The programme is assigned a category B risk rating as defined in the GCF revised Environmental and Social Policy.

### 1.2 Methodology  

#### 5. AGRA passed the Climate Investment Committee endorsement step (known as CIC2) on 11 October 2023 and submitted the first set of materials for review round #1 (completeness check) on 17 September 2024 using the customized application form and material request. Two further review rounds took place from September to November 2024. The external review team (ERT) conducted a comprehensive review of the submitted materials and assessed AGRA’s policies, procedures, capacities, track record and human resources against the GCF accreditation standards and checklist.

#### 6. Reference check interviews were conducted with AGRA’s two existing donor partners: the German Development Bank Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW), which has supported AGRA since 2017; and the United Kingdom Foreign & Commonwealth Development Office, which has supported AGRA since 2019. The referees were selected on the basis of their earlier contributions to interventions similar to the RE-GAIN programme under the Partnership for Inclusive Agricultural Transformation in Africa, and their mandate as public government donors. The interviews confirmed that no issues related to the fiduciary role or other capacities of AGRA had been identified during the long-term donor relationship.

#### 7. In addition to the desk review, the ERT conducted a site visit to AGRA’s headquarters (HQ) in Nairobi, during which the ERT validated the desk review findings, walked through processes, and engaged in interviews and presentations across all areas of the assessment. A total of 26 representatives from AGRA participated in the discussions, which provided further evidence of the capacity and procedures within AGRA.

#### 8. The applicant was assessed in accordance with the GCF policies and standards to the extent applicable to accreditation below:  

(a) “Updated Strategic Plan for the Green Climate Fund 2024–2027”;  
(b) “Matters related to the accreditation framework” regarding the accreditation process (decisions B.24/13 and B.26/01);  
(c) “Guiding Framework and Procedures for Accrediting National, Regional and International Implementing Entities and Intermediaries, Including the Fund’s Fiduciary Principles and Standards and Environmental and Social Safeguards” (decision B.07/02);  
(d) “Policy on Prohibited Practices” (decision B.22/19);  
(e) “Anti-Money Laundering and Countering the Financing of Terrorism Policy” (AML/CFT Policy) (decision B.18/10);  
(f) “Policy on the Protection of Whistleblowers and Witnesses” (decision B.BM-2018/21);  
(g) “Environmental and Social Management System: Environmental and Social Policy” (decision B.19/10);

## 1.3 Contribution to the mandate of the GCF  

9. The RE-GAIN funding proposal outlines AGRA’s contributions to the GCF focus areas:  
forests and land use; livelihoods of people and communities; and health, food and water  
security. The proposed programme aims to enhance climate adaptation by supporting  
innovative practices in the harvest and post-harvest value chain across seven African countries.  
It also seeks to improve food security by addressing climate change impacts on post-harvest  
food losses through the widespread adoption of food loss-reduction solutions (FL-RS) by  
smallholder farmers. In addition, the funding proposal includes initiatives to raise awareness  
among farmers about the effects of climate change on post-harvest losses and the benefits of the  
FL-RS available in the market. Beyond the proposed programme, AGRA’s overall strategy and  
mission align with the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024–2027 and contributes to the  
mandates of the GCF.  

## 1.4 Track record and implementation of similar projects  

10. As evidence of its track record, AGRA referenced four earlier or ongoing interventions  
that demonstrate its capacity to manage and deliver similar sized programmes in the proposed  
regions. AGRA has a track record of both developing and leading continuous activities regionally  
and of managing country-specific earmarked donor-funded interventions under different  
business lines with defined timelines, targets, activities and reporting requirements.  

11. The four referenced interventions are the multi-country Partnership for Inclusive  
Agricultural Transformation in Africa (over USD 400 million), the Kaduna Consortium and the  
Niger Consortium (two separate initiatives aimed at enhancing farmer resilience in Nigeria,  
each with a funding of USD 1.5 million) and the Strengthening Regenerative Agriculture in  
Kenya project (USD 4 million). These examples use grants as the primary financial instrument  
and demonstrate AGRA’s track record of working with programmes involving agricultural  
productivity, climate resilience and market development. Each intervention incorporates  
practices aimed at addressing the impacts of climate change and promoting broader resilience  
in food systems.  

12. Globally, AGRA has a network of donor partners ranging from international foundations  
to government donors, including the founding partners Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and the  
Rockefeller Foundation, the MasterCard Foundation, the United States Agency for International  
Development, German Cooperation (KfW/BMZ) and the United Kingdom Foreign &  
Commonwealth Development Office, demonstrating a strong funding base.  

## 1.5 Legal status, registration, permits and licences  

13. AGRA was registered in 2006 in the United States of America as a non-profit  
organization under the US Internal Revenue Code (para. 501(c)(3)).

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 111  

### 14. AGRA has established country offices in 12 African countries and is physically headquartered in Nairobi, Kenya. In Kenya, AGRA operates and is registered with the Registrar of Companies as a branch of the AGRA US registration under section 366 of the Companies Act, Cap 486 in 2006. This registration grants AGRA legal status and certain rights and obligations as governed by the Kenyan Companies Act. AGRA will be both the accredited entity (AE) as the contractual party with GCF and the executing entity (EE) of the RE-GAIN programme through the programme management unit and the country offices.

### 15. AGRA has established legal entities in six countries (Ghana, Kenya, Mali, Mozambique, Rwanda and United Republic of Tanzania) to implement its country strategy and operations as well as ensuring legal compliance. The country offices are extensions of AGRA and operate under the supervision of AGRA HQ. In countries where AGRA is not registered, administrative operations are managed through hosting arrangements with partner organizations.

### 16. In addition, several African governments (Burkina Faso, Ghana, Kenya, Malawi, Mali, Rwanda and United Republic of Tanzania) have granted operational privileges and exemptions through host country agreements.

## II. Accreditation assessment  

### 2.1 Basic fiduciary standards  

#### 2.1.1. Key administrative and financial capacities  

##### 17. AGRA’s global board of directors includes members from the Rockefeller Foundation, the Bill & Melinda Gates Foundation and independent directors, supported by board committees dedicated to audit and risk, finance and human resources, nomination and governance, and programme, whose task is to oversee key functions. In addition, AGRA has established five oversight committees governed by their respective policies: procurement, grants, ethics, data protection and safeguarding. Procedures for appointment, termination and remuneration are defined.

##### 18. AGRA’s organizational structure is documented, outlining responsibilities and reporting lines. The process for setting objectives involves a five-year strategy cycle and annual reviews, with key performance indicators and a corporate scorecard guiding the tracking of progress.

##### 19. Monitoring and reporting mechanisms are detailed in a quarterly management review, with assigned roles and reporting frequencies. External evaluations are conducted at closure of the larger programmes and at other strategic periods.

##### 20. AGRA’s financial statements are prepared as per the United States Generally Accepted Accounting Principles, and include detailed statements of financial position, performance, cash flows, changes in financial position, and notes and disclosures.

##### 21. AGRA uses an enterprise resources planning system for financial management. The system is designed to support compliance with accounting principles and to manage fund accounting. AGRA has a financial operations policy and its Procedures Manual covers financial reporting and donor fund management.

##### 22. The finance team, led by the chief financial officer and consisting of 15 people at the headquarters and eight in-country finance officers, is responsible for maintaining financial reporting systems. The IT infrastructure, which includes the AGRA Management Information System, supports financial tracking and grants management.

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 112  

### 23. On the basis of AGRA’s track record, this assessment concludes that AGRA has demonstrated that it is capable of managing and reporting financial activities aligned with its strategic framework and has operational capacity to handle international payments.

### 24. AGRA has an audit and risk committee that oversees both internal and external audit functions. The internal audit function is organized through a four-person internal auditing unit (IAU), which operates under a formally approved charter. The IAU adheres to international standards. The IAU reports functionally to the chair of the audit committee and administratively to the AGRA President. Reports are also shared with senior management, who participate in the process of addressing recommendations by the IAU.

### 25. AGRA appoints an independent external audit firm for the audit process through a competitive selection process, and audits are conducted in line with International Standards on Auditing. The external auditor issues annual audit opinions. Management letters from the auditor are reviewed by the audit and risk committee, which monitors internal controls and financial reporting.

### 26. AGRA’s control framework is documented through various policies and manuals that govern key operational areas, including governance, human resources and administration, grant management, finance, procurement, risk management and internal audit. Internal policies and procedures support regulatory compliance. The accuracy of financial data and reporting is supported by the built-in controls within the enterprise resources planning system, such as access controls, data validation controls and segregation of duties.

### 27. AGRA’s risk management processes involve a risk register and proactive measures, with updates to the management and the board. Risks are assessed at multiple levels, and actions to enhance mitigation measures are developed and documented. A comprehensive strategic risk report covering seven key risk areas is prepared quarterly for the board.

### 28. AGRA has detailed procurement guidelines, including a procurement policy and a Procurement Procedure Manual, outlining procurement actions, requirements and responsibilities. AGRA has mechanisms for regular oversight of procurement, including risk assessments, audits, due diligence, key performance indicators and segregation of duties.

### 29. AGRA uses an e-procurement system, which helps with streamlining workflows and is designed to improve financial control and compliance with procurement policies. In addition, the system offers reporting and analytics to support decision-making.

## 2.1.2. Transparency and accountability  

### 30. AGRA’s transparency and accountability policy framework is built on its ethics policy which hosts various institutional policies including partner and staff codes of conduct, and other targeted policies such as the anti-fraud and AML policies and a policy on sexual harassment. AGRA also has a conflict of interest policy requiring declarations from staff on affiliations. AGRA’s ethics committee oversees compliance with the policies.

### 31. AGRA has policies and measures in place for communicating zero tolerance on fraud and corruption, and for preventing and addressing financial mismanagement. Accountability and transparent organizational culture is promoted in training events and reporting structures with grantees and other partners. Grantees are required to execute a copy of the AGRA Partners’ Code of Conduct and safeguarding acknowledgement form, which are annexed to the grant award letter confirming their commitment to AGRA’s ethical standards.

### 32. AGRA has a whistle-blower policy with various reporting channels, but accessibility to reporting forms for different stakeholders could be improved. AGRA is committed to enhancing accessibility of reporting forms on their website. AGRA’s audit and risk committee and IAU are responsible for investigating allegations of fraud, financial mismanagement and other forms of

## Page 113  

### misconduct or prohibited practices  
The ERT noted that there is limited documentation of cases escalated to the formal investigation process. AGRA has indicated its commitment to enhancing documentation related to investigations.  

### AML/CFT and fraud prevention  
33. AGRA has demonstrated that it has policies in place related to AML/CFT and fraud prevention, and provided evidence of its procedures for carrying out adequate due diligence process on partners and fund recipients. The grants unit and the procurement unit conduct organizational capacity assessments on grantees and check the service providers for adverse mentions on sanction lists using the Refinitiv tool.  

## Specialized fiduciary standards  
### Project management  
34. AGRA has structured its operations around thematic areas or business lines to achieve its mission of transforming agriculture in Africa. Under the purview of the Vice-President – Center of Technical Expertise, AGRA has thematic units aligned with the business lines for policy and state capability, seed systems, climate adaptation, sustainable agriculture and resilience, and inclusive markets, trade and finance. Under the purview of the Vice-President – Programs Delivery, AGRA has the regional structures and country offices, as well as units for private sector partnerships, and gender, youth and inclusiveness.  

### Grant award and/or funding allocation mechanisms  
38. AGRA has not applied for accreditation for the specialized fiduciary standard for grant award and/or funding allocation mechanisms. AGRA will not award grants in the proposed RE-GAIN programme, but the field-level implementation partners will be selected and contracted through a procurement process. However, AGRA has provided evidence of its granting process and many of the established grant-making practices and policies will be applied in the RE-GAIN programme.  

### Project identification, design and management  
37. AGRA has demonstrated its experience in project identification, design and management throughout the project cycle. There are fiduciary policies for quality control and oversight mechanisms to monitor budgets and risks in project implementation phase. AGRA has broad programme management and MEL capacity with defined procedures to adhere to established policies and guidelines.  

## Monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) framework  
36. AGRA has a clear monitoring, evaluation and learning (MEL) framework and a comprehensive MEL plan aligned with the AGRA Strategy 3.0. The MEL plan outlines the principles, procedures, tools and responsibilities to execute the MEL framework. AGRA’s overall corporate theory of change and results framework expand into detailed business line-specific theories of change. The Director of Strategy & Monitoring & Evaluation and Learning reports directly to the President and oversees the operational units for strategy and analytics, and monitoring and evaluation (M&E). There are regional M&E officers for West Africa and for Southern and East Africa, as well as in-country M&E officers in eight countries.  

### Grant management policies & procedures  
35. AGRA implements projects through a variety of instruments and partners, including grant-making to local non-governmental organizations, farmer organizations, research institutions, extension service providers and so on. All projects and interventions are aligned with broader strategic goals as well as regional and country priorities. Grantees are the primary implementing partners who execute interventions contained in the AGRA strategy. AGRA has a comprehensive Grants Management Policies & Procedures Manual that outlines policies and procedures for managing the entire grant lifecycle.  

### Project implementation  
37. AGRA has demonstrated its experience in project identification, design and management throughout the project cycle. There are fiduciary policies for quality control and oversight mechanisms to monitor budgets and risks in project implementation phase. AGRA has broad programme management and MEL capacity with defined procedures to adhere to established policies and guidelines.  

### Grant-making practices  
38. AGRA has not applied for accreditation for the specialized fiduciary standard for grant award and/or funding allocation mechanisms. AGRA will not award grants in the proposed RE-GAIN programme, but the field-level implementation partners will be selected and contracted through a procurement process. However, AGRA has provided evidence of its granting process and many of the established grant-making practices and policies will be applied in the RE-GAIN programme.

## 39. AGRA’s approach to awarding and managing grants is documented in its Grants  
Management Policies & Procedures Manual. Typically, AGRA applies a structured grant-making  
process based on calls for proposals, with defined eligibility and selection criteria and  
structured reporting procedures. Country teams develop annual grant pipelines, which are  
approved by the Vice-President – Country Support, Policy and Delivery. The final grant  
approvals are handled by the Head of Grants for smaller grants and the grants review  
committee for larger grants.  

## 2.2.3. On-lending and/or blending for loans and equity  
40. AGRA has not applied for accreditation for the specialized fiduciary standard for on-  
lending and/or blending for loans and equity.  

## 2.3 Environmental and social safeguards  
41. AGRA’s Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) is mandated by its  
highest decision-making body and based on the IFC Performance Standards (PS). The key ESMS  
document includes an environmental and social policy and separate sections covering (1)  
identification of E&S risk and impacts, (2) ESMS management and organization, (3) ESMS  
screening, (4) environmental and social impact assessments, (5) stakeholder engagement  
(including a grievance mechanism) and (6) collaboration, information sharing and reporting.  
AGRA’s ESMS is comprehensive and is supported by a set of detailed implementation manuals,  
guidelines and templates.  

## 42. AGRA does not have an Indigenous Peoples policy, but risk screening and monitoring  
procedures for matters relating to Indigenous Peoples are covered in the ESMS implementation  
manual/annexes, and sample reports provide evidence of implementation. The project-specific  
Indigenous Peoples screening and vulnerability assessment annexed to the RE-GAIN funding  
proposal further indicates AGRA’s capacity to comply with PS7 on Indigenous Peoples.  

## 43. AGRA’s ESMS requires and guides the identification and assessment of E&S risks and  
impacts. The process for screening projects is described in the manuals, and the impacts are  
assessed against PS1–PS8 using the environmental and social screening checklist. An E&S risk  
categorization guideline provides guidance for assessing the E&S risk category.  

## 44. Most of AGRA’s interventions in the agriculture sector are classified as risk category C or  
B (i.e. low to medium risk). The proposed RE-GAIN programme is classified as category B  
because of identified potential exposure to social risks related to labour conditions and child  
labour in post-harvest activities and work safety with agriculture machinery. The  
environmental risks identified in the RE-GAIN programme are minimal, due to the focus on the  
post-harvest stage in the agricultural value chain.  

## 45. AGRA has incorporated a system in the delivery model for managing the E&S risks in the  
grant projects and other activities. All grantees sign an undertaking (comprising a grant award  
letter and a grant commitment checklist) that commits them to screening and mitigating  
identified risks. AGRA’s grantees are required to document the E&S risks in a project-specific  
risk register. AGRA is in the process of integrating the ESMS elements in the institution-wide  
AGRA Management Information System where grant processing, reporting and results  
monitoring are managed.  

## 46. The roles and responsibilities related to AGRA’s ESMS are defined in the ESMS Manual.  
The information provided by AGRA demonstrates its capacity and expertise on environmental  
and social matters at the HQ level and at the country level. A senior resilience specialist, sitting  
in the Adaptation, Sustainable Agriculture and Resilience Unit, oversees the implementation of  
the ESMS and has been closely engaged in the design of the RE-GAIN programme and its

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 115  

### Environmental and Social Components  
Regional environmental and social officers, programme officers and safeguarding officers in country offices support and review AGRA grantees’ E&S risk screening. Country offices monitor and report performance to HQ. AGRA’s ESMS was developed with an external consultant supported by earmarked KfW funding, and external support for E&S risk matters is contracted as needed. AGRA’s ESMS training is mandatory both for staff in environmental and social related positions and for grantees and other implementing partners.

### 47. AGRA adopted the ESMS in 2020 and it has been rolled out in three West African pilot countries: Burkina Faso, Ghana and Nigeria. AGRA has provided evidence of its track record of implementing the ESMS with local grantees in those countries (E&S risk screening, development of environmental and social action plans, monitoring and performance reporting). However, the ERT assessment noted that, with the exception of Burkina Faso, the ESMS and required capacities have not been fully established in six out of the seven countries targeted by the GCF-funded RE-GAIN programme (Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania and Zambia). AGRA has indicated that according to its training plan, all country training scheduled in RE-GAIN countries would be completed by March 2025.

### 48. AGRA has provided evidence of information disclosure and stakeholder consultation both in terms of past track record and in the context of the current funding proposal. AGRA has a whistle-blowing mechanism and other channels for receiving communications on E&S risk matters. Guidance and templates for setting up a project-level grievance redress mechanism are embedded in the ESMS and the annexed E&S Risk Assessment Toolkit. According to AGRA, complaints related to E&S risk are rarely escalated from the beneficiary level to AGRA; rather, they are typically handled locally by the grant partner.

### 49. The ERT assesses AGRA’s approach to environmental and social risk management as appropriate and sufficient for category B. At the HQ level and in selected countries, AGRA has policies, procedures and capacity to screen, monitor and manage E&S risks and impacts, but the roll-out of the ESMS in all target countries of the RE-GAIN programme has not been completed. AGRA has processes for independent reporting and grievance redress mechanisms at the global and country level and capacity to investigate alleged E&S risk violations.

### 50. AGRA has zero-tolerance on Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) which is covered in the human resources and administration policy, ethics policy and safeguarding of vulnerable persons and youth policy. The applicant has identified potential SEAH risks for the programme that include incidents involving staff or programme partners and backlash against women in non-traditional roles. Additionally, subprojects will also be screened for SEAH risks. Mitigation measures to manage these risks include training on AGRA safeguarding policy for staff and programme partners, including regular mandatory training on SEAH/GBV awareness and mitigation, integrating SEAH risk management into staff performance reviews and programme planning as well as SEAH awareness sessions in onboarding for new staff. In addition, the programme will establish community gender dialogues to address power dynamics and promote positive masculinity as well as include men in household financial management training to reduce tensions and offer support to survivors and victims of SEAH. Furthermore, the applicant will ensure that access to grievance mechanisms for SEAH incidents is provided to stakeholders. The applicant’s ESMS includes safeguarding officers at the country level for undertaking SEAH assessments and training.

### 2.4 Gender  
51. The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore complies with the updated gender policy of the fund. The RE-GAIN programme's gender assessment highlights significant challenges and barriers to gender equality in the agricultural value chains of target countries.
