# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 116  

### Countries  
Women face restricted access to critical resources such as land, credit, and climate-resilient technologies, exacerbating disparities in productivity and income compared to men. Sociocultural norms perpetuate unequal responsibilities, with women disproportionately shouldering unpaid care work, limiting their participation in economic and decision-making activities. Women are also underrepresented in leadership roles and formalized agricultural structures, and they lack adequate training and access to extension services tailored to their needs. Climate change further amplifies these gaps by intensifying women’s vulnerability due to their reliance on natural resources and exclusion from climate resilience planning and interventions. Institutional barriers, such as inadequate sex-disaggregated data and weak implementation of gender-sensitive policies, continue to impede progress toward equality.

### 52. The gender and inclusion action plan for the RE-GAIN programme focuses on addressing gender disparities through targeted and actionable measures.  
The gender action plan defines activities, indicators, and targets for women's participation and benefit with corresponding timelines, budget and gender expertise. During the programme start-up phase country-level gender assessments and action plans will be developed. Key activities include implementing capacity-building activities to enhance women's technical skills, leadership abilities, and access to climate-resilient agricultural practices. To strengthen women’s participation in decision-making, the plan includes strategies to establish women-led cooperatives and community-based networks. It also supports awareness campaigns to challenge sociocultural norms that limit women's roles in agriculture and land governance. Additionally, the action plan incorporates provisions for training women in post-harvest handling technologies and facilitating their access to markets through value chain enhancements, as well as finance by identifying relevant credit schemes and financial instruments.

### Section 2. Secretariat’s assessment of FP257  
**Proposal name:** RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa  

#### Project-specific assessment approach applicant: AGRA  
**Country/(ies):** Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania, Zambia  
**Project/programme size:** Medium  

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  
60. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:  

#### Strengths  
- As the first project targeting food loss mitigation in the current GCF portfolio, this initiative holds significant potential for transformative change by promoting the adoption of food loss-reduction solutions by engaging both the demand and supply side, with a strong focus on market creation and enhancement, and active private sector participation.  
- Six out of the seven targeted countries are least developed countries, with limited public and private investment in food loss reduction.  
- Significant numbers of beneficiaries, with a focus on smallholder farmers and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs), will be supported through capacity development initiatives, the adoption of food loss-reduction solutions, and improved access to finance.  

#### Points of caution  
- Potential implementation risks given the multi-country nature of the programme, including several contexts affected by fragility and conflict. However, the implementation arrangements, including the legal terms, include adaptive management provisions for mitigating some of these risks.  
- A potential risk factor for model 2 is the inability of beneficiaries to access finance or the risk of loan defaults, which could lead to a reduced number of beneficiaries. To address this risk, AGRA has developed food loss-reduction solutions deployment models that have multiple layers of de-risking, including smart subsidies, incentives for the private sector, and business development support for MSMEs.  
- As the first multi-country programme with a direct access entity under the project-specific assessment approach (PSAA), active project Secretariat origination efforts in collaboration with AGRA – leveraging its sectoral expertise, local presence, and proven track record – have been deployed in the development of this innovative PSAA initiative.

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 119  

### 61. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the  
term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the AE and, if considered appropriate, subject to  
the conditions set out in annex II of document GCF/B.41/02.  

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment  

### 2.1 Programme background  

62. Many African countries face recurrent droughts that affect growing seasons, often  
shortening the period suitable for agriculture and limiting the viability of farming in marginal  
areas. Projections indicate that crop yields in some countries could decline by up to 50 per cent  
by 2030, and net revenues for crops may fall by as much as 90 per cent by 2100, with  
smallholder farmers being the most affected. Food security, however, is affected not only by  
reductions in crop production but also by changes throughout the crop value chain, including  
post-harvest. There is, therefore, a crucial need to address the impacts of climate change on the  
full agriculture value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, processing and  
distribution.  

63. To address these challenges, the programme aims to build the climate resilience and  
adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers by promoting the wide-scale adoption of FL-RS, and  
thereby increasing awareness, capacity and demand for, improving access and the availability  
of, and enabling environment support for wide-scale adoption of these solutions. These  
activities will contribute to achieving the targets of the updated Strategic Plan for the GCF 2024–  
2027: Target 2 (new direct access entities); Target 4 (food systems); Target 9 (adaptation and  
locally led action); and Target 10 (local private sector early-stage ventures and MSME  
innovation).  

64. The total financing for this proposed programme is USD 105 million with the total GCF  
funding of USD 75,623,754 in grants, which leverages USD 29.3 million in grants in co-finance  
by AGRA. The project is rated as ESS category B. The programme will directly benefit 2.6 million  
vulnerable people and indirectly benefit a further 2.7 million vulnerable people (1.72 per cent of  
the regional population) across seven countries: Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda,  
United Republic of Tanzania and Zambia.  

65. This initiative is the first PSAA multi-country programme in the African region,  
supporting a direct access entity (regional). The PSAA applicant, AGRA, serving as both the AE  
and the EE, has over 15 years of experience, operations in 11 countries and a team of over 200  
professionals. AGRA’s experience and proven track record make it well positioned to lead this  
large-scale PSAA initiative, with its extensive network including partnerships with  
governments, local agribusinesses and civil society organizations in the agriculture sector.  

66. This funding proposal was developed with support provided by the Project Preparation  
Facility.  

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis  

#### Component 1: Increased adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers (total cost: USD 43.1 million;  
GCF cost: USD 22.57 million)  

67. Outcome 1 demonstrates several technical strengths, particularly in its comprehensive  
approach to increasing the adoption of FL-RS by smallholder farmers. By integrating multiple  
strategies, such as awareness-raising, training, technology transfer and market linkages, the  
programme effectively addresses the demand side of FL-RS adoption.

Its scalable design enables replication across regions; meanwhile, empowering women and youth fosters social equity and resilience. By reducing food loss, improving farmer incomes and promoting climate-resilient technologies, outcome 2 aligns with broader development and environmental goals, creating a de-risked investment environment that encourages sustained participation from suppliers, vendors and financial institutions across seven countries.

74. Outcome 3 establishes an enabling environment for adopting and scaling FL-RS by addressing policy, institutional and regulatory barriers. This component ensures the sustainability and scalability of interventions under outcomes 1 and 2 while fostering systemic change and long-term impact.  

75. Key strengths include a comprehensive policy focus, institutional capacity-building and private sector engagement. ## III. Assessment against Investment Criteria  

### 3.1 Impact Potential  
**Scale: High**  

#### 77. The proposed programme delivers transformative adaptation benefits by addressing the vulnerabilities of smallholder farmers and communities across seven African countries. By reducing food losses exacerbated by climate change, the programme strengthens food security and enhances farm-level profitability. It prioritizes vulnerable groups such as women, youth and MSMEs, empowering them through capacity-building, market development and social cohesion. These efforts strengthen community resilience and directly support broader climate adaptation objectives at both the regional and the national level.  

#### 78. The programme also complements traditional agricultural adaptation strategies by addressing critical gaps in the food value chain. Its emphasis on preserving harvest crops alleviates pressure on land and water resources, promoting environmental sustainability while mitigating greenhouse gas emissions from compensatory agricultural production.  

#### 79. Through catalytic grants and partnerships with the private sector, the programme creates a foundation for sustainable markets and attracts additional investments, ensuring benefits extend beyond the initial funding. Its scalable and replicable design further amplifies its impact, positioning it as a model for regional and global food security initiatives.  

### 3.2 Paradigm Shift Potential  
**Scale: High**  

#### 80. By leveraging GCF grants, the programme catalyses a paradigm shift in post-harvest development pathways through market stimulation, private sector engagement and scalable FL-RS, addressing climate-induced challenges in the agricultural value chain. The programme builds resilient agricultural ecosystems by empowering smallholder farmers with accessible, climate-resilient food loss-reduction systems. It integrates multi-stakeholder engagement and proven last-mile delivery systems, and it aligns with national and regional frameworks to ensure long-term sustainability. These efforts create robust linkages between farmers, markets and financial systems.  

#### 81. The programme’s design means there is potential for significant scaling and replication. Strengthening local supply chains, fostering private sector innovation and embedding successful models into national flagship programmes bring the potential to extend its reach. Partnerships with regional climate institutions amplify its impact, facilitating adaptation across Africa. Complementing this is a focus on knowledge-sharing, which leverages best practices and innovative financial models to influence broader strategies.

A participatory design process further strengthens the programme’s responsiveness. ### 95. This emphasis on government and local ownership is central to the programme’s sustainability. fostering systemic change and long-term resilience.

### 109. Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)  
AGRA has zero tolerance of SEAH, which is covered in the AGRA human resources and administration policy, its ethics policy and its policy on safeguarding vulnerable persons and youth. The applicant has identified potential SEAH risks for the programme, such as incidents involving staff or programme partners and backlash against women in non-traditional roles. Subprojects will also be screened for SEAH risks. Mitigation measures to manage these risks include training on the AGRA safeguarding policy for staff and programme partners, including regular mandatory training on SEAH and gender-based violence awareness and mitigation; integrating SEAH risk management into staff performance reviews and programme planning; and SEAH awareness sessions in onboarding for new staff. In addition, the programme will establish community gender dialogues to address power dynamics and promote positive masculinity as well as including men in household financial management training to reduce tensions, and offering support to survivors of SEAH. Furthermore, the applicant will ensure that access to grievance mechanisms for SEAH incidents is provided to stakeholders. The applicant’s ESMS includes safeguarding officers at the country level for undertaking SEAH assessments and training.

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
Page 126  

## 111. The RE-GAIN programme’s gender assessment highlights significant challenges and barriers to gender equality in the agricultural value chains of the target countries. Women face restricted access to critical resources such as land, credit and climate-resilient technologies, exacerbating disparities in productivity and income compared with men. Sociocultural norms perpetuate unequal responsibilities, with women disproportionately shouldering unpaid care work, limiting their participation in economic and decision-making activities. Women are also underrepresented in leadership roles and formalized agricultural structures, and they lack adequate training and access to extension services tailored to their needs. Climate change further amplifies these gaps by intensifying women’s vulnerability due to their reliance on natural resources and exclusion from climate resilience planning and interventions.  
Institutional barriers, such as inadequate sex-disaggregated data and weak implementation of gender-sensitive policies, continue to impede progress toward equality.  

## 112. The gender and inclusion action plan for the RE-GAIN programme focuses on addressing gender disparities through targeted and actionable measures. The gender action plan defines activities, indicators and targets for women’s participation and benefit, with corresponding timelines, budget and gender expertise. During the programme start-up phase country-level gender assessments and action plans will be developed. Key activities include implementing capacity-building activities to enhance women’s technical skills, leadership abilities and access to climate-resilient agricultural practices. To strengthen women’s participation in decision-making, the plan includes strategies to establish women-led cooperatives and community-based networks. It also supports awareness campaigns to challenge sociocultural norms that limit women’s roles in agriculture and land governance. In addition, the action plan incorporates provisions for training women in post-harvest handling technologies and facilitating their access to markets through value chain enhancements, as well as finance by identifying relevant credit schemes and financial instruments.

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
Page 127  

## 116. There is a misalignment in the timing of the distribution of economic benefits between the local service providers (e.g. manufacturer, agro-dealers) and smallholder farmers.

Service providers benefit from the grant immediately upon the completed agreement for the sale of agricultural equipment, while farmers accrue benefits gradually through extended access to the equipment or service provided using the equipment. This disparity creates a risk that GCF concessionality may be eroded owing to:

- (Model 1) Lack of a monitoring system on pricing: The memorandum of understanding to be signed between AGRA and the supplier lacks mechanisms to monitor pricing practices by agro-dealers (e.g. margins and sales records). This absence of oversight may dilute GCF concessionality and result in farmers not benefiting from the intended affordability;
- (Model 2) Default risk: Youth groups managing the equipment as a business venture may default if they lack sufficient capacity or face economic challenges;
- (Model 2) Premature exit risk: Youth groups may choose to exit the business prematurely by disposing of equipment before the economic benefits are fully realized by farmers;
- (Model 2) Market risk: The manufacturer that provides the repurchase assurance may be put at risk if the repurchase assurance does not cover the total outstanding loan, for example, if the resale value has depreciated due to the market situation.

## 117. To mitigate these risks, the project should incorporate:

- A provision for passing down the GCF concessionality to the relevant smallholder farmers by specifying the monitoring/spot check/reporting mechanisms in models 1 and 2;
- Incentives for youth groups to prioritize long-term capital creation over short-term economic gains;
- Mechanisms ensuring the accessibility and affordability of equipment for local farmers;
- Recovery provisions for the residual value of the grant if the business is terminated early, such as second lien pledges on equipment or negative covenants to redirect funds to similar initiatives; and
- An assurance that the financial institutions and supplier agree on clear depreciation terms for the repurchase value and the risk-sharing ratio and the establishment of regular maintenance requirements to preserve equipment value.

## 4.3.5. Co-financing risk:

## 118. The co-financing commitment from AGRA amounts to USD 29.3 million, representing 27.9 per cent of the total project cost.

While AGRA’s track record and capacity provide confidence in its ability to deliver, delays or shortfalls in co-financing disbursement could affect the timely execution of project activities and reduce the overall impact.

## 4.3.6. Compliance risk (low risk)   

## 119. The programme’s proposed activities are associated with a relatively low inherent risk of money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF).

## 120. The PSAA applicant has assessed ML/TF and other prohibited practices risk as low–medium on the basis of its internal impact and likelihood matrix assessment, which indicated the likelihood to be rare and the potential impact to be major.

Mitigating measures include grantee screening and pre-onboarding due diligence as part of organizational capacity.

125. The RE-GAIN programme will be implemented by AGRA as both the AE and the EE.

The programme will work by increasing demand and absorption capacity for FL-RS by smallholder farmers, enhancing supply and affordability of the solutions, and strengthening enabling environments for scaling them up. This will result in reducing exposure and vulnerability of key grain and legume crops that are grown by smallholders and that are strategic for food security, in regions identified as vulnerable to climate hazards – especially unpredictable rainfall with heavy downpours, high temperatures and flooding (with drought of secondary importance).  

These hazards are already increasing with climate change and are predicted to intensify, worsening food losses during harvesting, post-harvest handling and storage, and processing, transport and logistics – through mould, rotting, decay, heat damage, pests, rodents and afla/mycotoxin contamination.  

### Programme structure for RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa  

Abbreviation: MSMEs = micro, small and medium enterprises.  

In addition to extensive work in Component 1 on awareness and training for smallholders and value chain actors to build demand, as well as support to governments in Component 3 to enhance enabling environments, the biggest proportion of the budget (54 per cent of GCF funds) will go to Component 2 to develop the supply side for technical solutions to food loss.  

Output 2.1 will build partnerships for market linkages and provide training to suppliers and service providers. At the heart of the programme is Output 2.2, in which temporary financial mechanisms will be put in place to support smallholders and MSMEs to adopt FL-RS. According to the detailed programme budget (annex 4 to the funding proposal), a total of USD 20.42 million of GCF funds will be used to subsidize tarpaulins, plastic sheets, metal and plastic silos, and hermetic bags for smallholders farmers to protect harvest crops (in Activity 2.2.1), and USD 12.34 million from GCF will be used to subsidize larger items for which service providers take loans – mechanical multi-crop threshers, moisture meters, communal storage structures, storage protectants and control agents (in Activity 2.2.2).  

This is to be achieved through two innovative business and financial models, which can be summarized as follows:

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 133  

### (a) Model 1  
Model 1 will promote the local supply of FL-RS interventions by using conditional procurements to effectively subsidize interventions at smallholder farmer level, facilitated by 5,350 village-based advisers across the 35 priority regions. This model will enable agrodealers to provide FL-RS to approximately 540,000 smallholder farmers at a discounted rate, with GCF funds used to procure one item for every two items procured and sold by an agrodealer. The subsidy will be passed down to the smallholder farmers as a discount of 33 per cent on the purchase price (see paragraphs 95–105 of the funding proposal for a detailed explanation of the carefully designed model, including use of an escrow account to manage risk), also explained by the PSAA entity as a “Buy two, get one free” promotional offer.  

The aim of this model is to make these interventions more affordable for farmers, and to stimulate production and manufacturer capacity by placing pre-emptive orders.  

### (b) Model 2  
Model 2 will match MSMEs and cooperatives4 with suppliers and financial institutions, facilitating the signing of multi-stakeholder agreements to allow the MSMEs to purchase more expensive equipment for post-harvest handling (such as solar-powered multi-crop threshers and shellers, moisture meters, and communal storage structures). The MSMEs will become service providers, operating the equipment on a fee-for-service basis with smallholder customers.  

Under each agreement, AGRA will commit to an upfront co-payment of 30 per cent of the purchase price, with the MSME taking a loan from the financial institution to pay the other 70 per cent out of its service fee earnings. In the event of a successfully completed loan repayment, the equipment is owned by the MSME. If the MSME defaults, the supplier is obliged in terms of the agreement to buy back the equipment, accounting for depreciation.  

The establishment of the service operations will be facilitated through the business development support under Output 2.1.  

The aim of this model is to reduce borrowing costs to MSME service providers, through subsidies on the purchase price, and to reduce credit risk to local financial institutions.  

### 5.  
The subsidy mechanisms have been carefully designed to achieve these effects, ensuring that the benefits of the subsidies do reach the targeted smallholder farmers without leakages, that systems are in place to ensure agrodealers’ compliance with subsidy conditions, and that contingencies are in place to deal with market price fluctuations or supply chain disruptions.  

In response to a query from iTAP about the possibility of renting-to-own, AGRA confirmed that variations on the model will be possible, using the equipment as collateral for the loans (asset finance, buy-back guarantees by the suppliers, rent-to-own, etc.) – to be specified in the four-way agreement.  

The programme has already engaged the following financial partners: AgLeaseCo in Zambia, Equity for Africa (EFTA in Tanzania and EF-KEN in Kenya), and Coris Bank in Burkina Faso, the last of which AGRA has previously implemented an equipment finance scheme.

# GCF/B.41/02/Add.05  
## Page 134  

### 7. Climate change  
The funding proposal highlights the vulnerability of all seven countries to climate change, with negative impacts experienced and anticipated – not only on crop production and yields but also on volumes of crops that remain of sufficient quality for consumption or sale at harvest and beyond. Historical trends are shown, based on data from the World Bank Climate Change Knowledge Portal, with temperature indicators (such as annual mean surface temperature and the number of days with extreme heat – i.e. temperatures over 35 °C) displaying an increasing trend over time, and the rate of increase accelerating in recent decades. Historical rainfall trends display far greater variability, and for some countries a measure of uncertainty regarding the direction of the trend. The perceived historical increase in the frequency of intense rainfall events is not shown as a key climate variable.

### 8. Data from the same portal, using two future emissions scenarios, indicate that future projections of rainfall (such as annual mean precipitation, the number of consecutive wet days, and – as a corollary – the number of consecutive dry days) suggest a projected rise in rainfall levels and rainfall days across most of East Africa, likely to increase flooding. The exception is Zambia, where projections display a slight drying signal, consistent with regional trends for southern Africa. Continued trends of increasing variability in rainfall patterns are expected, as are increasing heat extremes. AGRA acknowledges that the impact of climate change on post-harvest food loss has not been well studied, either in terms of observed or projected impacts, but suggests that “[o]f the range of climate hazards that affect harvest and post-harvest value chains of cereal crops and legumes, scholarly publications and country experts pointed to variable, erratic, and unpredictable rainfall (including an increase in heavy rainfall events), high temperatures (extreme heat), and flooding as the priority hazards of concern”.

### 9. Adaptation impact  
The programme aims to yield significant adaptation benefits for vulnerable smallholder farmers and their households across the seven countries, through reducing climate change-induced food losses. This will enhance farming communities’ resilience to climate change by improving management at harvest and post-harvest stages, maintaining higher levels of food availability for consumption and sale than would be the case without the GCF–AGRA investment. The intended impacts of the programme are considered complementary to the broader adaptation strategies for agricultural resilience within each target country, which tend to be focused more on primary production. The programme will target vulnerable members of society, aiming to increase the resilience of rural livelihoods through investing in developing local markets and building up farmer cooperatives, youth and women’s groups, and MSMEs, leveraging partnerships with financial institutions and private sector actors to mobilize significant investment beyond the proposed programme.

### 10. Through these results, the programme aspires to directly benefit over 2.6 million people and indirectly benefit a further 2.7 million people, distributed across the seven target countries as shown in table 7 of the funding proposal (see methodology and detailed calculations in supplementary annex 1 to the funding proposal). The 35 priority regions, shown in detail in maps in annex 16, were selected on the basis of crop profiles and vulnerability to identified climate hazards. Smallholder farmers adopting new climate-resilient practices with FL-RS will be direct beneficiaries, experiencing improved food and nutrition security as well as increased food quality and market connections that improve their incomes. MSMEs and agrodealers will experience improved livelihoods as a result of increased business through the programme subsidy and stimulation of demand. The indirect beneficiaries include local buyers, whose access to affordable and good-quality grains and legumes will improve as a result of the programme interventions, and market players such as suppliers and processors, who will benefit from improved market linkages.

### 11. At GCF outcome level (see funding proposal section E.3), the programme will measure the number of beneficiaries reached, the number of farmers adopting innovations that strengthen climate change resilience, the number of people with new climate-resilient livelihood options, and the number of vulnerable farm households with improved food security,

Using farmer surveys employing the Food Insecurity Experience Scale. Since the innovations and livelihood options in question are predefined and relate to specific FL-RS, these key indicators should deliver credible tracking of results. Although food losses before and after programme interventions will not be quantified, a co-benefit indicator of the percentage of produce meeting quality standards will measure the percentage of grain rejected due to quality concerns associated with afla/mycotoxins, with the aim being to halve the rejection rate.

Overall, it can be anticipated that food loss reductions will be significant, at least during the programme implementation period (i.e. while the subsidies are in operation), improving resilience through better nutrition, greater income from crop sales and enhanced business incomes along the value chains for technical solutions.

## 12. Overall, impact potential is rated as high.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential                 Scale: Medium  

## 13. Paradigm shift relates to the degree to which the proposed programme can successfully catalyse impact beyond the GCF one-off investment of USD 75.6 million over five years, together with the AGRA co-finance. The iTAP recognizes the hard work and careful thought that have gone into designing the programme in a manner that intends to elicit a paradigm shift in the harvest and post-harvest development pathways for the seven target countries and continentally in Africa. A clear focus on achieving scale and sustainability is embedded in the programme design, both through the comprehensive value chain approach to removing barriers to scaling of FL-RS and through the work with governments on country-level enabling environments in Component 3.

The design of the business and financial models, however, relying as it does on a large subsidy that will be abruptly withdrawn at programme end, does not seem optimal for achieving sustained demand or long-term market transformation, as discussed further below.

## 14. Enabling environment for scaling up. Component 3 of the programme seeks to create an enabling environment for the uptake of FL-RS. This will involve working with governments on new policies, such as stimulating tax exemptions, subsidy or reduced interest rates for FL-RS investments. RE-GAIN will provide technical assistance to local and national government entities to build their institutional capacity for scaling FL-RS through partnerships, platforms, and support on tools, guidelines and monitoring. RE-GAIN will also work with governments and other development partners to promote additional private sector investment in post-harvest infrastructure, including storage facilities, processing units and cold chains, as well as helping regulatory bodies to develop and implement quality standards and certification processes for FL-RS products and services.

Support will also be provided to enhance the capacities of extension services and climate advisory services to monitor food loss in relation to climate conditions and the impact of FL-RS in reducing loss. Although the RE-GAIN investments aim to prolong grain shelf life in the absence of good rural road access and renewable-powered cooling systems, engaging with wider government adaptation and development initiatives will enable complementarity in this regard.

## 15. Innovation in institutional markets. A welcome innovation is the focus on building smallholder farmers’ market access by linking them to institutional markets for staple crops – such as schools, universities, armies, hospitals and prisons – as well as to technical and financial partners supporting public sector feeding schemes. This will be achieved by facilitating purchase agreements with farmer cooperatives or producer groups, and, where no such organizations exist, by using aggregators. ## Page 136  

Used as collateral to obtain credit from financial institutions.  

Technical assistance includes support by village-based advisers to wheat flour millers, enabling them to avoid unnecessary food loss from overprocessing to refined flour, and instead to switch to producing healthier wholegrain bread flour for school-feeding markets.  

RE-GAIN will leverage the institutional markets through government procurement processes and work with partners such as HarvestPlus, the Partnership for Child Development, Save the Children and the World Food Programme.  

### 16. Sharing lessons beyond the target countries  
Evidence generated and lessons learned in FL-RS from the target communities and countries will be used to develop capacity, influence policy and promote best practices across Africa. Activity 3.1.4 has budget set aside to work actively to leverage the lessons from the programme in other contexts, building on the extensive networks of AGRA.  

AGRA will facilitate collaboration with continental and regional bodies and national governments, including African regional climate institutions and regional economic communities. This will include field days and media events bringing together the private sector, government officials, farmers from non-programme areas, and development partners to showcase success stories and encourage replication.  

Knowledge products will be developed and widely shared, and a business case will be developed for investments in FL-RS manufacturing, supply, service provision and financing.  

### 17. Exit strategy for subsidies  
The funding proposal states: “Long-term sustainability is at the core of the programme design, with GCF investments seen as catalysers of change within local systems. A key aspect of this GCF intervention is having an exit strategy through the creation of an enabling environment to shift away from the smart-subsidies, catalysing further investments in FL-RS under Component 2” (paragraph 166).  

Once the programme ends, however, there will be nothing in place to manage this transition away from the subsidy. Prices are likely to go up at least 30 per cent as soon as the subsidy is removed, which may lead to a fall-off in purchasing and thus lower adoption rates for the FL-RS.  

The iTAP wonders whether a subsidy that was smaller, more gradually phased out, or both, would be less disruptive, and enable continued growth in sales and loans. In sharing examples of successful market stimulation  
9, AGRA made no mention of other similar price subsidy programmes. Arguably, support to local manufacture might be a more appropriate level at which to utilize a subsidy – to stimulate long-term sustainable production of inputs, rather than providing a one-time subsidy of inputs, for which there is no ceiling on imports.  

### 18. Local manufacturing not prioritized  
The programme does not prioritize direct support to local manufacturing of FLRS, which could have been an alternative focus for subsidization in the form of seed capital or expansion grants to local manufacturers. Although business development support is part of the programme (Activity 2.1.2), this consists of training workshops and provision of market intelligence, and is aimed at businesses that will buy machinery and operate it on a fee-for-service basis.  

Unlike some of the other AGRA programmes cited, such as the SaYeTech support to artisans in Ghana to manufacture threshers, the RE-GAIN programme does not include any such direct support to local manufacturing.  

### 19. The response to a question from iTAP about import substitution  
While “AGRA fully agrees that long-term sustainable domestic production of inputs is critical for maximizing the impact of the programme,…for the first years of programme implementation, RE-GAIN will prioritize delivering its support to the target groups over import substitution to ensure timely programme delivery and to stimulate and prime the market for local manufacturing capacity.”

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential                        Scale: Medium to high  

21. The programme is expected to have a number of social, economic and environmental co-benefits, as outlined below.  

#### 22. Socioeconomic and health co-benefits. In targeting highly vulnerable smallholder farming communities, the proposed programme will yield multiple socioeconomic co-benefits.  

Reducing food loss can help stabilize food prices by ensuring a more consistent supply, helping to protect both producers and consumers from price volatility. Improved post-harvest processes that reduce food waste will improve the quality of food reaching consumers, enhancing the nutritional value and reducing the risk of food-related illnesses resulting from contamination by pests, moulds, aflatoxins and other pathogens. Not only will this benefit individuals but also a more stable and safe food supply will reduce public health costs.  

Working with local suppliers and agrodealers, and supporting business development of MSMEs as service providers, the proposed programme will generate employment opportunities that should extend beyond the initial investment, with the intention for these opportunities to be of particular benefit to vulnerable groups such as women and youth.  

#### 23. Gender and youth co-benefits. On the customer side, the fee-for-service business model of the programme has significant potential for breaking down barriers to access by women farmers to climate adaptation technology. This model allows small-scale farmers, including female-headed households, to rent equipment, labour for services, or both, that they would otherwise find difficult to access.  

An interesting example is provided by previous programmes, where AGRA has tested and proven the deployment of “Uber for tractors”¹² digital solutions, resulting in more women getting access to mechanization services. The digital service requests are anonymous, and the service provider deploys the equipment on a first-come, first-served basis, so cannot discriminate against female requesters.  

#### 24. On the operator side, although AGRA hopes to prioritize women- and youth-led enterprises in supporting 2,000 MSMEs and cooperatives to take loans and start fee-for-service businesses, inclusivity is more challenging, given the (reasonable) eligibility criteria set out in annex 2 of the funding proposal.  

The qualifying groups must have a registration certificate and constitution, preferably a documentary track record as a service provider (in extension, aggregation of produce, selling of inputs or provision of mechanized services), and preferably qualified staff or members that have experience in operating, repairing and servicing the machinery.  

Given that, in many of the rural communities to be engaged in the programme, these are customarily seen as men’s areas of business, it seems unlikely that there will be many women-led businesses or cooperatives that will have sufficient baseline experience to qualify.  

The funding proposal does not suggest any small steps towards engagement of women’s cooperatives – for example, in providing auxiliary services, such as bookkeeping or keeping machinery clean and oiled. Such options could be explored through Activity 1.1.3 (on training for complementary services).

On youth groups, however, AGRA has suggested an innovative sub-model which takes into consideration that the eligibility criteria will in practice likely also exclude most youth groups (despite the characterization of the target MSMEs and cooperatives in the funding proposal, somewhat confusingly, as "youth groups"). RE-GAIN will work with formalized MSMEs to partner with local youth groups. The established MSME will take the lead in entering into the multi-stakeholder agreement (including taking a loan to acquire machinery) and taking account of the management of the agreement. The MSME will then recruit and work with an informal youth group from the community to operate the equipment, sharing the income and in the process developing the group's capacities. Through this MSME-supervised approach, the informal youth groups will have the opportunity to build a track record, demonstrating their governance structures and creditworthiness for future opportunities.

26. Environmental co-benefits. The RE-GAIN programme interventions to reduce post-harvest food loss will have some environmental co-benefits. Reducing post-harvest food loss will increase the net productivity of agricultural land, improving the efficiency of land and water use and preventing their wasteful use in producing food that is never eaten. The need to compensate for food loss can be a driver of agricultural expansion, and AGRA posits this as a major environmental co-benefit – avoiding land degradation and biodiversity loss (also used in estimating emissions avoided). In the view of the iTAP, however, increased market access and improved grain quality might in fact cause some farmers to expand the land under production to take advantage of additional income-generating opportunities, potentially causing deforestation. So this co-benefit is not a given, and there is nothing in place to test these assumptions about non-expansion. The same applies to the argument that avoiding "regrowing of lost food"13 will reduce the environmental impact of fertilizers and pesticides, and related energy demands. A clear benefit is the reduction in air pollutants from burning of waste food, and the reduction of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from decomposition.

27. Mitigation co-benefit. GHG emission reductions, as a co-benefit of the programme, will be achieved through three pathways (with the emphasis on the first two in funding proposal section D.3): (a) reduction of emissions arising from the production of compensatory food (i.e. food produced to compensate for food lost in harvest and post-harvest without considering land expansion); (b) reduction of food loss as a driver of land-use change linked to compensatory agricultural expansion; and (c) reduction of emissions from wasted food left to rot, causing methane and carbon dioxide emissions. Combined, it is estimated that these pathways could result in a total emission reduction of 111,275 tCO2e (tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent) over the programme lifespan of 10 years (see methodology in annex 22 and country-specific breakdown in table 9 of the funding proposal). Given the doubt raised in the previous paragraph about the accuracy of the assumptions on avoided agricultural expansion, which could undermine pathways (a) and (b), it is positive that this is being considered as a co-benefit only.

28. Mitigation methodology. The iTAP asked why AGRA chose not to utilize the most obvious choice for estimating emissions reductions, i.e. the Verra VM0046 Methodology for Reducing Food Loss and Waste, choosing instead to base the estimations on a single publication, and queried whether the resultant estimates could be considered as suitably conservative. In a written response,14 AGRA explained that this methodology was initially considered but that it can only be effective in data-rich environments, which are not present in the programme context of informal and smallholder value chains, while gathering new data would be a big challenge in 14 value chains (two crops in each of the seven countries). The response explained: "In light of these constraints and considerations, the team went back to the first principles and developed an alternative approach that would make optimal use of the data available. The calculations are considered conservative in that they: (i) were focused only on those portions of

### 30. Sustainable development potential is rated as medium to high.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient              Scale: High  

### 31. RE-GAIN will target seven African countries, six of which are classified as least developed countries and all of which are recognized as being highly vulnerable to climate change. Of particular concern is the highly affected agriculture sector, which employs between 40 and 80 per cent of the total population and contributes between 20 and 40 per cent of gross domestic product across the target countries. In addition to their sensitivity to climate impacts, all seven of these target countries are recognized as having low adaptive capacity and low readiness to adapt,15 which highlights their need for support. Most of these countries have a high level of sovereign indebtedness, and need grant funding to help address the challenges faced by poor, small-scale farming households, which are frequently highly exposed and vulnerable to negative impacts of climate change.

### 32. Climate change-driven (or -exacerbated) food spoilage and contamination in post-production stages of agricultural value chains compound productivity and yield losses already being experienced by these countries during cultivation, as a result of the changing climate.  
This results in a double blow to farming communities from climatic factors such as rising temperatures, higher air moisture levels, more frequent or intense heavy rainfall events, and more frequent flooding. Increasing post-harvest losses with climate change reduce the output that farmers have left to sell, depress incomes and jeopardize smallholder livelihoods, perpetuating vulnerability to climatic shocks and stresses as well as prolonging the cycle of persistent poverty. Farmers experiencing increasing losses during harvesting, field-drying and on-farm storage may choose to keep less of their reduced output for domestic consumption, worsening household food insecurity in countries that already have markedly high levels of undernourishment17. All of these reduce adaptive capacity and must be ameliorated to ensure the resilience of smallholder farmers in the seven countries.

#### 15 According to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) Index for 2021, where “adaptive capacity” is the ability of society and its supporting sectors to adjust to reduce potential damage and to respond to the negative consequences of climate events, and “readiness” refers to the ability to make quick, effective use of investments for adaptation.

#### 16 In response to an iTAP question about whether these losses in the target countries have been quantified, the PSAA entity explained (in responses received by the iTAP on 6 January 2025 from AGRA to questions it received from the iTAP on 18 December 2024):  
“There is currently an absence of robust quantitative/quantified evidence on the current or projected impact of climate change on post-harvest food loss. The climate change linkage in the post-harvest value chain stages is increasingly being investigated, but the body of knowledge on this is relatively nascent and has yet to yield a modeled estimate or quantification that is tied directly to climate projections. For example in Chapter 5 on Food Security of the 2019 IPCC Special Report on Climate Change and Land

Given the high levels of poverty, weak markets and low adaptive capacity experienced in smallholder farming communities, farmers are frequently unable to adopt climate-resilient FL-RS without initial external support. 1.5 Country ownership                          Scale: Medium to high  

36. The proposed programme arose from discussions between AGRA and GCF, as well as extensive engagements AGRA has conducted in each of the target countries during the development of its five-year country strategy (2023–2027) – engagements resulting in a gap analysis across the agriculture and food system sector of each country. As a continental institution in agriculture and food systems, AGRA has widespread infrastructure and partnerships on the continent, with strong country teams in the target countries. Using its continental forums, the Africa Food Systems Forum and the Agribusiness Deal Room, AGRA will bring on board connections with governments, partners and other stakeholders to enhance ownership and coordinate actors, knowledge and resources that are fundamental to take food loss innovation to scale.

37. Engagement with governments. AGRA co-developed the programme together with the seven participating governments. Following initial consultations with the lead ministry (agriculture) and GCF national designated authority for each country, draft concept notes were shared to enable joint development of context-specific solutions. This was followed by multiple local and national stakeholder workshops (detailed in annex 7 of the funding proposal), which were combined with desktop research and experience in the target countries to establish a baseline identifying technical, institutional and market gaps that need to be filled to build the resilience of the target smallholder farmers. This process of stakeholder engagement will continue throughout the programme’s implementation. At the country level, the programme will be implemented under the overall guidance of a programme steering committee, which will meet quarterly. Each such committee will be co-chaired by a representative of the national designated authority and the AGRA country manager, with representatives of other key government departments and agencies (ministries of agriculture and departments for land resources conservation, crop development, agriculture extension services and agriculture planning services), the private sector and civil society organizations.

38. Alignment with policy. The focus on post-harvest loss reduction is aligned with key agriculture and adaptation strategies, as outlined in various policy documents and development strategies in each of the seven focus countries, detailed for each country in table 11 of the funding proposal. For example, the Burkina Faso National Economic and Social Development Plan (2016–2020) included a key focus on reducing post-harvest losses through improved storage, processing and distribution techniques to enhance food security and promote sustainable agricultural practices. The funding proposal states that “the proposed programme is designed through the participation of a wide array of public and private institutions, and civil society (including national and international and development actors).”¹⁸ The RE-GAIN programme is closely linked to the broader AGRA strategy, which “works across four strategic priority areas and is implemented through an integrated public-private programme and/or value chain platforms through which 28 million farmers are targeted for yield improving technology through access to extension, improved seeds and markets.”¹⁹ The iTAP notes that some civil society stakeholders favouring an agroecological approach have publicly contested what they perceive as AGRA’s focus on sustainable intensification of production through technology-led solutions.

AGRA stated that it is “currently prioritizing partnerships with more than 60 civil society and farmers’ organizations, including regional farmers’ organizations (East African Farmers Federation, Southern Africa Confederation of Agricultural Unions, Association des Grossistes et Détaillants des Intrants Agricoles, Association Burkinabè d'Action Communautaire, Sasakawa Africa Association etc.), to collectively align on key issues to rally around. These efforts are part of AGRA’s deliberate strategy and work plan for engaging with African farmers organizations and civil society, and will continue to help mitigate any perceived risks of working with them, as well as aid in the response to occasional criticism from actors who have different priorities and ideologies.”²⁰ It is hoped that as AGRA moves into its next phase, there will be space for a multiplicity of voices from the African continent and a vigorous debate about which approaches are best to achieve the organization’s vision “to contribute to a food system-inspired inclusive agricultural transformation across Africa, to reduce hunger, improve nutrition, and adapt to climate change”²¹.

### 41. Capacity of AGRA  
Founded in 2006, AGRA is an African-led organization dedicated to sustainably growing the food systems of Africa. The organization operates in 12 countries, employing over 300 staff and managing an average annual operating budget of USD 90 million²². In terms of the due diligence processes for the GCF PSAA, AGRA underwent a two-round capacity assessment that determined its suitability as a PSAA applicant (equivalent to an accredited entity for this funding proposal), the fiduciary functions it can play (basic fiduciary standards and project management) and its maximum environmental and social risk category (category B).

### 42. In terms of track record with similar initiatives  
AGRA has managed the USD 4.6 million YieldWise project, aimed at reducing Tanzanian maize post-harvest losses at scale, as well as related projects funded by the Global Environment Facility and the IKEA Foundation. ### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness              Scale: Medium to high  

44. The PSAA applicant AGRA, whose permitted fiduciary functions for this GCF programme do not include non-grant instruments, is seeking 100 per cent concessionality for the programme. With a total budget of USD 105 million, AGRA is seeking USD 75.6 million, or 72 per cent of the total, and would itself provide USD 29.4 million, or 28 per cent of the total, in grant co-finance.  

45. The AGRA request for full concessionality is argued based on the nature of the investments, which “require targeted capacity development support combined with strategic conditional procurements to catalyse a paradigm shift in the post-harvest value chain using innovative FL-RS in the seven target countries.” The two key financial models – 1) catalytic grants for smallholder farmers and 2) catalytic grants for agricultural MSMEs – use concessional funding to incentivize private sector suppliers and financial institutions (FIs) to provide physical solutions that are within the affordability envelope of farmers, while remaining commercially viable to private sector suppliers; and to provide tailored financial products that are affordable to cooperative and MSME service providers, while mitigating credit risk to financial institutions.  

46. Concessionality and derisking. In response to a query from iTAP about the risk of crowding out existing private sector suppliers, AGRA responded that “all interested and eligible suppliers (to be sourced through an open call following the eligibility criteria detailed in annex 2 of the funding proposal) will benefit from the subsidies to build and kick-start the market. The programme seeks to build markets where they are non-existent and incentivizes all suppliers that want to discover new markets and new client segments.”  

The programme addresses the current market failure of FL-RS not being available or affordable in specific targeted geographies or for specific segments because of a range of barriers related to information, capacity, technology and finance. The intention is to grow the market and crowd in private sector players by derisking the rollout of new technologies. RE-GAIN will thus:  

(a) De-risk first-time users investing in new technology that remains unproven in their context. The “pay for two, get one free” type of investment will make sure the concessional funding ends up in farmers’ pockets;  

(b) Reduce the cost of distributing FL-RS to rural areas where demand is not yet confirmed (concessional funding will de-risk agrodealers and distributors from remaining with unsold stock);  

(c) Reduce the risk for FL-RS service providers investing in equipment for which the business case is not yet proven. The manufacturers will be paid, and the service

47. Financial and economic return. AGRA expects the RE-GAIN programme’s combined approach of addressing supply- and demand-side constraints while improving enabling environments to mobilize USD 144.95 million worth of physical solutions over the next five years, representing a catalytic effect or return of 1.38 times the original investment of USD 105 million by GCF and AGRA. This anticipated financial return on investment excludes the long-term impact of the programme’s ability to demonstrate the market viability of FL-RS to both users (farmers) and suppliers (manufacturers and financial institutions), which AGRA hopes will catalyse new private sector markets while addressing food loss and associated climate-related challenges.  

48. AGRA undertook an economic analysis for the programme using a cost–benefit analysis approach. Programme costs and benefits were assessed over a 10-year period and using country-specific social discount rates as presented in annex 3 of the funding proposal. The analysis weighed up the total programme costs against the projected benefits associated with the uptake of equipment by smallholder farming households. These benefits included the income gains associated with the value of food that would otherwise have been lost in the absence of the programme interventions, the health benefits from reductions in aflatoxin exposure, the improved nutritional status (particularly of infants) from avoided food losses, and the climate benefits associated with reduced GHG emissions from food production. The results of the economic analysis suggest that, under the current set of assumptions, the total package of interventions is cost-beneficial, with the total programme investment generating an economic net present value of USD 69 million, with a benefit-to-cost ratio of 8:1 and an economic internal rate of return of around 56 per cent. On this basis, and with a sensitivity analysis conducted to show that these statistics are robust to changes in input values, there is a clear economic case for implementing the proposed programme.  

49. Efficiency and effectiveness are rated as medium to high.  

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

50. This proposed programme – RE-GAIN: Scaling solutions for food loss in Africa (brought to GCF through the PSAA) – is expected to make a significant contribution in helping the smallholder farming sector in Burkina Faso, Ethiopia, Kenya, Malawi, Uganda, the United Republic of Tanzania and Zambia to reduce their exposure and vulnerability to staple grain and legume crop losses that are worsening as a result of intensifying climate change hazards.  

51. The iTAP retains a concern that the significant subsidies to be provided to reduce the costs of food loss reduction solutions, derisking the scale-up of these solutions in rural markets, might not have a long-lasting effect, given the subsidies’ large and short-term nature. There may be the unintended consequence of a marked drop-off in demand following the subsidies’ withdrawal. Nonetheless, the iTAP believes that the programme can have a major impact during the five years of its implementation alone, reducing the vulnerability and enhancing the adaptive capacity of this highly affected sector in vulnerable priority regions of the participating countries.

### 52. 
The iTAP recommends that AGRA closely track the impacts of the subsidies intended to stimulate market demand in the 35 priority regions of the seven countries – both the subsidy on small-scale food loss reduction solutions being purchased by farmers, and the subsidy on larger technologies for which MSMEs and cooperatives are taking loans in order to set up fee-for-service businesses. Since AGRA will remain engaged in these countries during the programme lifespan of 10 years, it should be feasible to use AGRA resources to track the development of these markets in the five years following programme closure. Although attribution is complex, and other factors will be at play, an evaluation can be made, based on this market tracking, of the extent to which these temporary subsidies, together with the programme's value chain and enabling environment strengthening activities, have been able to create sustained demand.

### 53. 
The iTAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# 6 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

## Introduction

### 1.1 PROGRAMME BACKGROUND

A great deal of attention has been paid in recent decades to the impacts of climate change on crop production, i.e., on growing risks to agricultural productivity. Scholarly investigations and public and private research have invested heavily in identifying and – where feasible – quantifying the ramifications of climate change on crop yields, yield stability over seasons, and in exploring plausible management options for the emerging challenges (CGIAR, 2023). As governments and societies look at how to minimize the risks of climate change, the impact of these changes on food production is increasing, fuelling concerns about food security and livelihoods for current and future generations.

Food security, however, is affected not only by changes in crop production but by changes occurring throughout the crop value chain, including during post-harvest phases (Akoth, 2020). It is therefore crucial to examine the impacts of climate change on a crop’s value chain, including production, aggregation, storage, transportation, processing, and distribution. Each stage comprises several sub-processes, and climate change may plausibly affect many or all of the sub-processes too.

With the lion’s share of research and resources for resilience interventions in the agricultural sector having been focused on production, the RE-GAIN project is an effort to give dedicated focus to harvest and post-harvest stages of the value chain – specifically, harvesting, post-harvesting handling and storage, processing, transportation, and logistics. As summarized in Table 1-1, the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) report highlights a range of climate change concerns in the post-production stages of value chains and potential adaptation interventions that could increase resilience against such climate change concerns (IFAD, 2015).

### Table 1-1 - Illustrative climate change risks and climate change risk management interventions in post-production value chain processes (adapted from IFAD, 2015)

| Value Chain Components | Climate Risk Issues | Risk Management Interventions |
|------------------------|---------------------|-------------------------------|
| Post-harvest management | Rising losses in harvest volume; declining safety, market quality and nutritional value due to increasing temperatures, humidity, pests and diseases. | Improve knowledge sharing on harvesting techniques to reduce losses. incentivize waste reduction measures and value addition for by-products; provide renewable energy sources to cover changing requirements for cooling, drying, milling, and threshing. |
| Siting of processing facilities | Extreme climate events (such as, floods, heatwaves, and storms) may damage processing facilities; shifting climatic conditions may render some sites redundant or increase transportation costs. | It could create sustainable environment to pests and diseases, affecting both product quality and its suitability for consumption Use hazard exposure and crop suitability maps to inform the siting of processing facilities; retrofit processing facilities with protective features; insure processing facilities against extreme climate events. |
| Energy in processing | High dependence on local bioenergy (wood, charcoal, dung, crop residues) has trade-offs with better soil management; rising temperatures require more energy for cooling. | Provide renewable energy sources (such as solar photovoltaic panels for cooling/drying/milling/heating, wind, biogas); equip processing facilities with energy-saving appliances (e.g., solar lighting, solar charging, efficient cook stoves); adopt pollution control measures. |
| Water in processing | Declining and more irregular water supplies; growing competition with other domestic or industrial users. | Re-site facilities closer to more suitable water sources; increase water storage and distribution capacity (water harvesting, communal ponds, groundwater recharge); introduce demand-side |

# RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

## Value Chain Components

### Climate Risk Issues

#### Water efficiency measures; support conflict resolution for different water users (e.g., water user groups).
- Rising temperatures and humidity may increase or decrease post-harvest losses and waste, as well as impact food safety, particularly if current packaging materials are impacted by high temperatures leading to produce damage or poor quality.
- Design suitable packaging materials in parallel with waste and storage management strategies.

#### Packaging materials and methods
- Rising temperatures and humidity may increase or decrease post-harvest losses and waste, as well as impact food safety, particularly if current packaging materials are impacted by high temperatures leading to produce damage or poor quality.
- Design suitable packaging materials in parallel with waste and storage management strategies.

#### Processing infrastructure
- Buildings and roads are exposed to higher peak rainfall, winds, and heat stress.
- Introduce protective features and reinforcements into the design of critical infrastructure to handle run-off and higher temperatures; improve ventilation in buildings; harvest surplus water and energy from rooftops and appliances; use early warning systems.

#### Transport hubs and routes
- Routes may become seasonally or permanently impassable (or open up); extreme events will disrupt logistics.
- Re-site hubs; develop contingency plans for road, rail, water, and air transport; co-design value addition, storage, and transport components to avoid high-risk transport routes and seasons; upgrade docks, jetties, roads, and railways.

#### Refrigeration and cold chains
- Temperature rises increase requirements for and costs of refrigeration; rising energy requirements increase greenhouse gas emissions.
- Conduct cost-benefit analyses of dependency on refrigerated cold chains to assess best routes; introduce renewable energy sources for cooling and ventilation; optimize storage and transport management.

#### Just-in-time logistics
- Extreme climate events (floods, storms, heatwaves) can make it impossible to comply with “just-in-time” requirements.
- Develop contingency plans for climate shocks and extreme events; create contingency storage opportunities; link into regional markets to avoid over-dependence on high-value export markets.

#### Demand from retail and consumers
- Shifts in quantity and quality requirements and seasonality with climatic trends; disruptions in demand with climate variability, hence higher price fluctuations.
- Assess market risks and opportunities before value chain implementation, including likely climatic impacts on high-value markets; strengthen and diversify storage to buffer price fluctuations; diversify into “off-season” crops.

#### Commodity labelling and certification
- Increased consumer awareness as climate change may create new markets for sustainably produced and processed commodities with a low carbon footprint.
- Explore opportunities for sustainable procurement, green labelling, and certification.

## AGRA

AGRA is a continental institution working in 15 African countries addressing food systems focusing on smallholder farmers’ production, marketing and nutrition. In the countries where AGRA operates, which are highly diverse in terms of climate, soils, crop choices and institutional capacity, neither all of these climate-related concerns may be applicable, nor all of these potential interventions possible. Even within the range of what may be applicable, this programme is likely to look at a subset of risks that may be viable to address, and – given resource constraints – only a limited number of high-priority resilience interventions may be feasible to design and deploy.

RE-GAIN is an effort to identify the most salient risks, select the most impactful solutions, and implement the priority interventions through a well-structured, strategic, multi-country programme.

There is a clear gap in knowledge, data and interventions designed to target the impacts of climate change at the harvest and post-harvest stages of the value chain, despite the mounting evidence of the ramifications on food loss and the impact

# 8 RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

This has on land use changes and associated climate change mitigation. The majority of the current programmes designed to tackle climate-induced food loss focus on the pre-harvest stages of the value chain.

To address the pressing need for broader implementation of solutions aimed at reducing climate-related harvest and post-harvest food loss, the proposed programme is designed to raise awareness and build capacity to promote the adoption of Food Loss Reduction Solutions (FL-RS). It will do this by creating institutional capacity, facilitating the uptake of FL-RS by end users and service providers, increasing options of solutions’ availability, and enabling practical application through policy interventions. This will include enhanced financial access for farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), empowering them to invest in climate-friendly FL-RS and incentivising vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers of climate-adapted FL-RS, fostering a robust market ecosystem.

A key focus is on strengthening the capabilities of countries to develop climate-resilient post-harvest infrastructure, both through providing physical solutions alongside capacity building along the value chains. This includes investing in strategic frameworks and implementation plans, including a regulated quality-based pricing system and tax exemptions on imports, for reducing food loss. By enhancing access to markets, the programme will encourage farmers to adopt FL-RS products and services, thereby boosting their climate and economic resilience.

## Target Countries Overview

During the 2023–2027 period, AGRA plans to target 28 million farmers across 15 Sub-Saharan African countries, 40% of which will be women. The RE-GAIN Programme focuses on AGRA's activities in seven target countries, as shown in Figure 1-1 below. The RE-GAIN Programme is designed to combat food loss during the post-harvest stages and to boost climate resilience by fostering awareness and by building capacity for the adoption of Food Loss Reduction solutions (FL-RS). The programme aims to transfer these solutions to end users and service providers for practical application while facilitating financial access to farmers and Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) to invest in climate-resilient FL-RS. The programme plans to incentivize vendors, manufacturers, and suppliers to adopt these solutions and enhance the capacity of countries to develop climate-resilient post-harvest food handling infrastructure.

Crop selection  
Key crops were identified by major stakeholders in the respective countries and expert assessments, supported by AGRA and the National Designated Authority (NDA) of each target country. Two major crops per target country were selected, based on area coverage, importance for food security and income, and climate vulnerability, to ensure that sufficient resources would be available for the crafting and execution of targeted solutions. Selected crops are representative of the agricultural dynamics of each country and aligned with the specific needs and strategic agricultural goals of the nation. In addition, these crops hold substantial importance to the country's food security and/or experience particularly high rates of loss within the value chain. Finally, these crops are produced in large parts of the respective countries by a significant number of smallholder farmers. The key crops, therefore, reflect the agronomic and economic realities of each country and provide opportunities for targeted enhancement of food security and sustainable agricultural practices. Additionally, the improved management of these crops is also expected to significantly reduction of GHG emissions contributing to the NDC targets of the countries involved.

# RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

## Harvesting and Post Harvesting Definition

For the RE-GAIN programme, the key value chain stages considered are shown in Figure 1-2.

### Figure 1-2 Strategic value chain stages included in the RE-GAIN Programme

The harvesting process within this RE-GAIN Programme proposal is defined as the interval between the culmination of agricultural production, marked by the crop reaching its maturity, and the initiation of post-harvest treatment. This process encompasses the identification of the optimal harvesting time and is further delineated into four distinct stages:

1. Removal of contaminated seeds, heads or cobs of matured crops at harvest
2. Reaping, which involves cutting, pulling, or gathering the mature crops.
3. Threshing, the process of separating the grain from the rest of the plant.
4. Cleaning, such as winnowing, to remove chaff and other impurities.
5. Hauling, which entails the transportation of the harvested produce to storage or processing facilities.

The post-harvest handling and storage stage commences once the crop exits the field and is typically conducted on the farm. This stage encompasses several key operations, including:

1. Threshing, which can be performed manually or with mechanical threshing machines.
2. Drying, utilizing cribs, tarpaulins, and similar methods.
3. Cleaning and sorting, such as through winnowing, to remove impurities.
4. On-farm storage, which includes the use of granaries, hermetic bags, ordinary bags, stacks, metal silos, and plastic silos.
5. In some instances, primary processing activities, such as grinding, hulling, pounding, milling, drying, and sieving, are also conducted during this stage.

The processing, transportation, and logistics stage involves farmers selling their harvested crops either directly to traders, who collect the produce from the farm, or to collection centres and processors. These market participants then undertake the tasks of product accumulation, initial processing, quality control, grading, packaging, and transportation to wholesale buyers.

> 1 In this instance, a field is where the crops are grown, and a farm consists of the whole small holding including the small aggregation site.

- Including threshing, cleaning, sorting, storage and primary processing
- Post-harvest handling and storage
- Including packaging and distribution, and impact on shelf life
- Processing, transportation and logistics

# RE-GAIN | Green Climate Fund Proposal

## 1.3 REASONING FOR REQUESTED FUNDING

Africa's food insecurity challenge has been exacerbated by climate change. Sub-Saharan Africa stands at a crossroads with an unprecedented opportunity for food systems transformation, driven by the demands of a rapidly growing population of 1.5 billion and the pressures of a changing climate (World Bank, 2023) (Worldometer, n.d.). The continent faces significant development challenges including food insecurity, resource degradation, poverty, gender inequality, and social exclusion. The vicious cycle of poverty and environmental degradation in Africa is evident in low crop productivity, deforestation, land degradation, conflict, migration, and vulnerability to climate shocks, which perpetuate persistent food insecurity and poverty.

The effects of climate change are expected to be severe in Africa, where the capacity to adapt and respond to a changing climate is weak.

The impacts of climate change have increased over the past decades in Africa, manifesting in more frequent, intense, and prolonged extreme weather events, such as floods, droughts, heatwaves, locust outbreaks, desertification, and sandstorms. These extreme weather events have resulted in increased temperatures and humidity, shifts in precipitation patterns, water stress, and soil erosion. Most African countries already face recurrent droughts that affect growing seasons, often leading to short growing periods reducing the viability of farming in marginal agricultural areas. Projected reductions in crop yields in some countries could reach as much as 50% by 2030, and crop net revenues may fall by up to 90% by 2100, with smallholder farmers being the most affected (IPCC, 2018).

Therefore, the RE-GAIN programme aims to enhance the climate resilience and adaptive capacity of smallholders by promoting the widespread adoption of FL-RS in seven African countries. According to the World Bank estimates, a one percent reduction in post-harvest losses in Sub-Saharan Africa could lead to economic gains of $40 million each year, and most of the benefits would go directly to smallholder farmers (World Bank, 2011). Moreover, food loss and waste are the result of an extremely inefficient use of resources and account for about 3.3 gigatonnes of greenhouse gas emissions globally (FAO, 2013). Large amounts of water and fertilizer also go into the production of food that never reaches human mouths.

Recovering the food that is lost during harvest and post-harvest handling some can help close that calorie gap in Africa while strengthening livelihoods and improving food security — without imposing any additional environmental cost. Therefore, facilitated by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) investment, RE-GAIN will roll out a suite of physical interventions alongside capacity building and enhanced financial and market access. Not only will this benefit the respective countries as whole, but it also has the potential to benefit the region and the wider planet.

## 1.4 PROGRAMME GOAL STATEMENT

If the capacity of the target countries and communities to respond to climate-triggered food losses is strengthened through improved and inclusive access to financing, promotion of context-specific and gender-responsive innovations to reduce food losses, and better enabling conditions for public and private investments, THEN smallholder farmers will have enhanced food security and livelihood resilience, BECAUSE the widespread use of food loss-reduction technologies will reduce food loss and reduce the carbon footprint of food systems, while increasing household income and building the resilience of smallholder farmers, MSMEs and rural communities to climate shocks.
